# P02M0099 - Universal standards-based driver set

Status: OPEN. It closes when every item below is done, and not before.

**WHAT THIS MILESTONE IS WAITING FOR, AS QUESTIONS SOMEBODY CAN ANSWER (2026-09-19).** Thirty items
are open and not one of them is waiting for an implementer to be free. Every wall was checked against
the machine or the code rather than taken on trust, and what is left reduces to decisions that are
not this file's to take. They are written here rather than only inside the items so that opening this
file shows them:

1. **Does the system want any of these features?** A Bluetooth stack, a power-state service, a
   smart-card service, a modem service, a camera vocabulary, a MIDI wire, a print spool, a media
   importer, a trusted firmware-admin path. THIRTEEN driver items deliver into one of those and none
   of the nine is named anywhere outside the item that needs it - not in another milestone, not in
   the architecture. Each `yes` creates a milestone for the SERVICE and unblocks one or two drivers
   behind it. The section under the blocker table has the search that established this.
2. **Who owns the firmware-node device identity?** Five items bind through an ACPI or FDT node rather
   than a PCI function, and that identity has no numbered owner. An AML interpreter sits behind three
   of the same five.
3. **May a process hold a capability to itself, and may an I2C controller item exist?** The first is
   asked by P02M0103's 3D floor and reaches this file through nothing; the second blocks two items
   here that both say no I2C controller and no I2C provider exist.

NONE OF THESE IS A DRIVER, AND AN IMPLEMENTER TAKING ANY OF THEM UNILATERALLY IS THE THING THIS FILE
REFUSES EVERYWHERE ELSE: a driver that grew its own destination service, a capability widened to make
a number work, a mechanism invented to unblock the item that wanted it.

## WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-19): 34 DONE, 30 OPEN, AND WHAT EACH OF THE 30 IS WAITING FOR

THE SHAPE OF THE REMAINDER CHANGED, AND MEASUREMENT IS WHAT CHANGED IT. Fourteen items say BLOCKED
in their own titles and the table below is about those. What the table did NOT cover is the other
twenty-two, and going through them one at a time found that most are not waiting for an implementer
either - they are waiting for something this harness cannot supply, and each now says which, with
the check that established it:

                              scope decision here and not an unfinished half

    CDC-ACM                 `usb-serial` is an FTDI adapter, vendor class 0xFF - not a
                              communications-class device (recorded 2026-09-18). `usb-braille` was
                              tried as the other candidate on 2026-09-19 and the probe was
                              INCONCLUSIVE: attached with a null chardev it did not appear in the
                              controller's device list at all, so this row still rests on the
                              `usb-serial` measurement and not on a second one

    A second CDC adapter    NetworkService opens the FIRST `net` provider and closes the

    UAS task management     the tag IS the stream in this transport, so a management request needs
                              a second stream - which is the same work as tagged concurrency, not
                              a separate item
    GOP as a provider       two kernel mechanisms nobody owns: an object wrapping an EXISTING
                              physical range, and a publication path for a program with no device

    SDHCI ADMA above 4 GiB  nothing in this system can ask for memory a 32-bit device can address;

    SDHCI UHS and eMMC      the controller advertises no UHS-I mode (`CAPABILITIES_1` bits 0-2 are

AND THE PORTS ARE DONE (2026-09-19). Every driver item that was up on x86_64 carried the same last
line - acceptance on aarch64 and riscv64, a separate measurement on each - and both measurements are
in: `drivers,pci,slow` passes on aarch64 with 100 tests in 1539 s and on riscv64 with 102 in 2097 s,
each bringing up NVMe, AHCI with NCQ, SDHCI with ADMA2, HDA, virtio-scsi across more than one target,
virtio-vsock, and an xHCI reporting keyboard, pointer, storage, network, UAS and audio. Four items
closed on it outright, and two more closed later the same day on work rather than on a measurement;
the rest each keep a remainder that is theirs and is named in them.

34 items done, 30 open (was 47). Six closed on 2026-09-19. Four of them - virtio-vsock,
virtio-scsi, NVMe and AHCI - closed when the emulated ports came in with nothing else outstanding.
The other two are the day's implementation work: UAS gained per-stream rings, task management and
several commands in flight, and HDA gained a second codec and the response-ring defect that one
codec had been hiding. What the remaining number hides is that those 30 are not 30 pieces of driver
work waiting for an implementer: NINETEEN OF THEM ARE WAITING ON SOMETHING NOBODY HAS DECIDED, each
stated inline against the items it blocks and counted here.

AND THOSE NINETEEN ARE TWO DIFFERENT KINDS OF WAITING, WHICH THE TABLE USED TO RUN TOGETHER
(2026-09-19). SIX are unowned MECHANISMS - a firmware-node identity, an AML interpreter, an I2C
controller, a range-scoped port-I/O authority, a display controller that can reach a bus - and a
mechanism is something this project intends to have and has not built. THIRTEEN are destination
services, and the section under the table shows that not one of them is named anywhere outside the
driver item that needs it: not in another milestone, not in the architecture. Those are not waiting
for an assignment, they are waiting for the system to DECIDE IT WANTS THE FEATURE.

THE COUNTS BELOW DO NOT SUM TO NINETEEN, AND SAYING SO IS THE POINT. Six items are blocked more than
once - UCSI on three causes, the ACPI battery classes on three, the Time and Alarm Device, IPMI SSIF,
HID-over-I2C and the 16550 on two each - so a column that added up would be counting those items
twice. What each row answers is "how many items does deciding THIS one thing reach", which is the
question an owner actually asks, and an item with two causes is reached by neither alone.

| reached by deciding it | items | what it is |
| ---: | ---: | --- |
| 13 | an owned destination service or IDL | the drivers whose bytes have nowhere to go: Bluetooth HCI, HID power device, CCID, CDC-MBIM, UVC, MIDI, printer, PTP, DFU, UCSI, the ACPI WDAT watchdog and both IPMI transports. Each needs a typed service to deliver to, and none of those services has an owner. ANSWERED 2026-09-19 - see the section below: the answer is not a name, it is that no phase has asked for the feature |
| 5 | the FIRMWARE-NODE DEVICE IDENTITY | PL011, ACPI battery/AC/thermal, UCSI, the ACPI Time and Alarm Device, HID-over-I2C. All bind through an ACPI or FDT node rather than a PCI function, and that identity has no numbered owner anywhere. The 16550's MMIO-scoped alternative is a sixth, and its legacy slice is blocked separately |
| 3 | an AML interpreter | ACPI battery/AC/thermal, the ACPI Time and Alarm Device and UCSI on x86. All three are ACPI NAMESPACE devices with control methods, and this kernel reads static tables only. Not hardware work at all |
| 2 | an I2C controller item with a real fixture | IPMI SSIF and HID-over-I2C. This index states in two places that no I2C controller and no I2C provider exist |
| 1 | a range-scoped revocable PORT-I/O authority | the 16550's legacy x86 slice, which also needs legacy fixed-IRQ routing and an atomic handoff from the kernel's own COM1 path. The ACPI fixed-hardware event-delivery item is the architectural decision under it and is open, though not itself marked blocked |
| 1 | a display controller that can reach a bus | DDC/AUX transport and monitor discovery, which fetches EDID over a bus the BOUND display controller supplies |

**WHO OWNS THE THIRTEEN DESTINATION SERVICES, ANSWERED (2026-09-19) - AND THE ANSWER IS NOT A NAME.**

The row above was written as though an owner existed and had not been assigned. IT WAS SEARCHED FOR
BEFORE IT WAS ASSIGNED, and what the search found changes the shape of the question:

    service the driver needs        named anywhere outside the item that needs it?
    a sandboxed Bluetooth stack     no milestone. `docs/CONCEPT_EN.md` has one table row,
                                      `Wi-Fi/Bluetooth | driver services`, which places the DRIVER
                                      and says nothing about the stack above HCI
    a platform power-state service  nowhere at all. `system-power` carries reboot and power-off
    a smart-card service            nowhere at all
    a typed modem service           nowhere at all
    a camera vocabulary and service nowhere as a service. `CONCEPT_EN.md` lists `mic/camera/
                                      screenshot portals` under LATER, which is a permission
                                      portal and not a frame path
    a MIDI event/timestamp wire     nowhere at all
    a spool/stream service          nowhere at all
    a media/import service          nowhere at all
    a trusted UI/admin policy path  nowhere at all

SO NOBODY OWNS THEM AND THAT IS NOT AN OVERSIGHT. A service with no owner in a roadmap that names
every other service it intends to have is a service NOBODY HAS ASKED FOR. The architecture does not
list a Bluetooth stack, a smart-card service, a modem service, a MIDI wire, a print spool or a media
importer among the things this system is going to have - not as unfinished, not as later, not at all.

AND THE DRIVER ITEM CANNOT OWN IT, WHICH THE ITEMS THEMSELVES SAY. Each one draws the line in its own
words: the Bluetooth driver "stops at HCI" because pairing, keys, L2CAP and profiles belong to a
separately sandboxed service "whose size/risk does not leak into xHCI"; UVC "explicitly refuses to
tunnel video through DisplayService" and asks for a camera vocabulary FIRST; CCID says PIN handling
and authentication policy do not go in the transport. A driver that grew its own destination service
would be the one thing every one of these items forbids.

**SO THE RULE IS THIS, AND IT IS A DIFFERENT RULE FROM THE OTHER FIVE ROWS.** The other rows name
unowned MECHANISMS - a firmware-node identity, an AML interpreter, an I2C controller, a port-I/O
authority, a display controller that can reach a bus - and a mechanism is something this project
intends to have and has not built. A destination service that no phase names is something this
project has not decided to want. THOSE ARE NOT THE SAME KIND OF BLOCKED, and counting them together
made thirteen items look like work waiting on an assignment when they are work waiting on a PRODUCT
DECISION.

WHAT UNBLOCKS ONE OF THEM IS THEREFORE NOT "name an owner" BUT "decide the system has this feature".
That decision creates a milestone for the SERVICE, with its own vocabulary, sandbox and threat model;
the driver item then follows it rather than leading it, and closes on delivering to it. Until then
each of the thirteen is PHASED BACKLOG in the sense this file already defines at its head - a
candidate whose phase is decided when it is picked up - and none of them is counted in any phase's
completion.

AND THE ONE THING THAT WOULD BE WRONG is to let a driver item quietly invent the service to unblock
itself. Nine of these would then each carry a stack, a sandbox boundary and a policy surface written
by whoever happened to want the driver, which is the arrangement this file refuses everywhere else.

CORRECTED 2026-09-16, AND THE PREVIOUS TABLE WAS WRONG IN FOUR WAYS rather than out of date. It said
43 open when the file holds 47, and has held 47 since before the table was written. It filed the
ACPI WDAT watchdog and USB DFU under "an AML interpreter": both are blocked on an unowned destination
service, WDAT's own text says it must never execute AML and the specification table below calls it a
static table with no AML, so that row named two items neither of which it described. It counted the
destination-service cause at 9 when thirteen items state it. And it omitted the I2C controller and
the display controller as causes, so three blocked items appeared in no row at all. The four rows
were also presented as a partition of seventeen items, which they were not: the overlap above is
why an arithmetic total is the wrong shape for this question.

**WHAT THE THREE-TARGET RUN ACTUALLY FOUND (2026-09-17), AND IT IS THE ARGUMENT FOR THE RULE.** This
file says a driver item closes on a run on three targets and not on working. That sentence was
cheap to write and it earned itself in one afternoon: the first run on the other two machines found
FIVE defects, four of which x86_64 cannot express at all.

  - THE HARNESS ONLY BUILT THE MACHINE ON ONE TARGET. NVMe, AHCI, SDHCI, HDA, virtio-scsi and
    virtio-vsock were wired into the x86_64 machine when each driver was written and into neither of
    the others, so their oracles could only ever fail there - "the device table should hold at least
    one AHCI controller" was the first thing the run said. Every one of those is a PCI function and
    both other machines have a PCI bus. They are attached on all three now.
  - AN AHCI DRIVER THAT ONLY WORKS AFTER SOMEBODY ELSE INITIALISED THE PORT. On x86_64 the firmware
    enumerates the controller before the driver ever sees it, so the link was already up and `PxSIG`
    already latched. On aarch64 the firmware does not touch it, and the driver read a port whose
    link had never been brought up: "port 0 carries nothing", with a disk plainly attached and QEMU
    reporting it. The driver resets the port itself now - and the step that actually mattered is the
    one that is easy to miss: `PxSIG` IS NOT A REGISTER THE HBA FILLS BY ITSELF. It is copied out of
    the device's first register FIS, which the HBA can only receive once the port has a FIS receive
    area and `PxCMD.FRE` is set. A port probed with the receive engine off reports a link that is up
    and a signature that is zero, for ever.
  - A `net` PROVIDER PUBLISHED WITH NO ADAPTER BEHIND IT, which wedged the aarch64 boot at seventeen
    of twenty-four services. Recorded in full under the CDC item: the block contract answers and a
    link does not.
  - THE BOOT MEDIUM'S ESP WAS SIZED FROM THE KERNEL AND THE LOADER and carried the bootstrap set and
    the volume archive as well - 4.8 MB and 11.6 MB against 16 MB of slack. It fitted until it did
    not, and then said "Disk full" and nothing else. Everything that goes on it is counted now.
  - AND ONE DIFFERENCE IN CODEGEN PRESENTING AS A DIFFERENCE IN THE LIBRARY GRAPH: `render2d` calls
    the stack's one square root, which x86_64 and aarch64 inlined and riscv64 emitted as a dynamic
    import - so the riscv64 image failed to link and the other two were clean. The function is
    `#[inline(never)]` and the provider row is declared, so all three agree by construction.

  AND THREE MORE THAT THE HARNESS ITSELF OWED, found in the same afternoon and each silent:
  - THE riscv64 BOOT MEDIUM IS AN NVMe DEVICE, attached that way on purpose because U-Boot's default
    boot order tries `nvme 0` first. Two scratch NVMe controllers added AHEAD of it made `nvme 0` a
    blank disk with no partition table, and the machine fell through to a TFTP boot that was never
    going to work. The suite's devices go on the bus AFTER the boot medium now, and the NVMe oracle
    takes the LAST controller rather than the first - on that machine the first one is the disk the
    guest is running from.
  - A BASH NAMEREF THAT REFUSED ITSELF. The helper that attaches those devices took `local -n arr`
    and passed `arr` to functions that also declare `local -n arr`, which bash refuses as a CIRCULAR
    NAME REFERENCE - with a warning on stderr and an empty array, not an error. Every device it meant
    to attach went nowhere and the only trace was three warning lines in a run log. It passes the
    array's NAME through now and takes no nameref of its own.
  - AND A LEFTOVER GUEST. A run that fell to the U-Boot prompt sat there holding this tree's disk
    images until the next run refused to start. That is the harness working as designed - it names
    the pid and says to stop it - and it is recorded because it is what a boot-order regression looks
    like from one layer up.

  **AND THEN ALL THREE TARGETS PASSED (2026-09-17).** The driver oracles: x86_64 51, aarch64 48,
  riscv64 50, each including NVMe, AHCI, SDHCI, HDA, virtio-scsi, virtio-vsock and an xHCI reporting
  `(keyboard) (pointer) (storage) (network) (uas)` - the CDC Ethernet adapter and the UAS target over
  bulk streams, on machines whose firmware and PCI enumeration have nothing in common with x86_64's.

  **AND THEY PASSED AGAIN WITH THIS SESSION'S WORK ON THEM (2026-09-19), WHICH IS WHAT THE EIGHT
  ITEMS BELOW CLOSE ON.** x86_64 201 tests under `drivers,pci,slow,storage,usb,network,service,
  console,shell`; aarch64 100 under `drivers,pci,slow` in 1539 s; riscv64 102 under the same tags in
  2097 s. Each of the three brings up `ahci` with `ncq 4 tags`, `sdhci` with `adma2 256 blocks`,
  `virtio-scsi` across more than one target, and an xHCI that now reports `(audio)` alongside
  `(keyboard) (pointer) (storage) (network) (uas)` with `audio sink bound - 2 channel(s), 16-bit,
  192 byte packets`.

  AND THE ISOCHRONOUS PATH IS WHAT TCG CHARGES FOR. `xhci_driver_enumerates_the_usb_bus` costs 87 s
  on aarch64 and 824 s on riscv64 - nine and a half times, in a suite whose other tests run within a
  factor of two of each other. Isochronous transfers are paced by the controller's microframes and
  an emulated controller pays for every one of them, so the number is the emulator's and not the
  driver's; it is written down because a reader watching that test sit silent for a quarter of an
  hour will otherwise take it for a hang, which is exactly what happened here before it finished.

  AND SDHCI IS THE ONE DRIVER WHOSE REPORT DIFFERS BETWEEN TARGETS, for the reason recorded in its
  item: x86_64 boots sometimes place the descriptor table above 4 GiB and the driver falls back to
  `pio one block`, honestly and in the log. aarch64 and riscv64 never did in these runs. The same
  binary reports `adma2 256 blocks` whenever the descriptor memory is addressable in 32 bits.

**AND THE FULL SUITE FAILS WHERE NO SCOPED SELECTION DOES (2026-09-19), WHICH IS RECORDED HERE
BECAUSE IT IS THE KIND OF THING A MILESTONE LOSES.** `./test.sh --arch x86_64` with no tags fails
deterministically in `kernel.services.a_tty_a_job_left_raw_comes_back_cooked` - a stray `x` is in
the console input before the test types anything - and passes in every scoped selection tried,
including a 201-test one carrying every driver and device change made here. Nothing in the suites
feeds an `x`, the serial receive path checks its data-ready bit, and the code it fails in is
untouched. It is an ORDER DEPENDENCE between suites, it is reproducible, and it is not this work's.
The bisection a next attempt needs is by tag, and that is possible: the filter selects a test when
ANY of its tags was requested. CORRECTED 2026-09-19 - this said "the tag filter requires EVERY tag a
test declares to be selected, which is why a narrower run can silently not include the test one is
looking for", and the code says otherwise. What is all-shaped is a VETO over exactly two tags: a
test carrying `slow` or `stress` is excluded unless that tag is requested by name. A sentence that
called the one available bisection impossible was discouraging the only thing that finds this.

**AND THE ABI SNAPSHOTS WENT STALE OVER THE HOT-PLUG WORK, WHICH NOTHING NOTICED FOR A DAY
(2026-09-19).** `host-tests` was RED in the committed tree and the failure was this milestone's own:
`SYS_DEVICE_EVENTS = 86` was added and the `SYSCALLS` table still ended at 85, and `DeviceInfo`
gained `on_bus` so `_pad2` moved from 52 to 53 while the layout snapshot still asserted 52.

BOTH GUARDS DID THEIR JOB AND NEITHER WAS RUN. The syscall snapshot's own comment records the last
time this happened - `SYS_DEVICE_QUIESCED = 74` added against a table ending at 73 - and it was
rewritten then to compare the declared set against the named one precisely so a later addition could
not slip past. It did not slip past: it was caught the first time the gate ran. What was missing was
the RUN, in a change that touched the ABI and was verified with a build and a guest suite, both of
which pass over a stale snapshot because neither compiles that crate's tests.

SO THE RULE THIS PAYS FOR IS THE ONE THE `host-tests` GATE ALREADY STATES: a change to `src/abi`
needs that gate and not a build. It is cheap - 107 suites in ninety seconds - and it is the only
thing in this tree that compiles them.

**THE BIND WINDOW IS MEASURED FOR EVERY DRIVER AND IT IS ONE MEASUREMENT (2026-09-19).** Three items
still carry it as an open point each - NVMe's says "not measured on any target", AHCI's "unmeasured
on every target", HDA's "recorded per target" - and all three are older than the reporting.
DeviceManager prints the window on the ORDINARY path rather than only when something failed, so a
boot that brings the machine up measures every driver on it at once. From the x86_64 suite boot of
2026-09-19, in the 100 Hz ticks the allowance of 200 is counted in:

    virtio_blk      7, 8      nvme            63, 65
    virtio_rng     77         ahci            68
    virtio_console 79, 87     sdhci           69
    virtio_snd     74         hda             71
    virtio_vsock   77         virtio_scsi     87
    xhci           75

THE CONDITION IS PART OF THE NUMBER AND IT IS NOT THE ONE RECORDED EARLIER. An older reading has
`xhci` at 40, `nvme` at 32 and `hda` at 23; this boot carries every device the suite attaches -
three SCSI units across two targets, two NVMe controllers, AHCI, SDHCI, HDA, an xHCI tree with five
functions on it - and they bring up TOGETHER. What the window measures is BIND to READY on a
contended machine, which is the machine an operator has; a driver measured alone reports a number
nobody will see. Every one of them is inside the allowance, none is close to it, and the spread
between `virtio_blk`'s 7 and `virtio_scsi`'s 87 is enumeration: a disk that is told its own size by
its transport against one that walks targets asking.

AND THE TWO EMULATED TARGETS ARE A SEPARATE MEASUREMENT EACH, which is what those three items say and
is still true - they are part of the ports pass at the end.

**AND A DEFECT THE THREE-TARGET RUN COULD NOT FIND, BECAUSE NO TARGET EXPRESSES IT (2026-09-19): FIVE
DRIVERS STOPPED SERVING WHEN A CONSUMER LEFT.** `virtio-scsi`, `nvme`, `ahci`, `sdhci` and
`virtio-vsock` - every driver written in this milestone that serves consumers - read a closed
endpoint and merely continued their loop. Every driver written BEFORE them drops it and tells the
manager; these five did not, and they were the five newest.

WHY IT IS WORSE THAN THE SPIN IT LOOKS LIKE. A closed endpoint is a READY one - the kernel's
readiness is `!inbox.is_empty() || is_peer_closed()`, which is how a reader learns of a closure at
all - and the serve loop answers with the FIRST ready index. So a departed consumer at index zero is
answered ahead of a live one at index one, on every pass, for ever: not a core burned, a driver that
has stopped serving everybody. And nothing reports it, because the manager's channel is drained
first on every pass - the heartbeat is answered by a driver doing nothing else, which is exactly the
driver a watchdog must not kill and exactly the driver it cannot tell from a working one.

AND THE SECOND HALF IS THE COUNT. The `consumers` bound in the registry is on CONCURRENT consumers
and the manager only counts up until a `Disconnect` names the publication, so without it a kind
admitting one is refused for the rest of the boot the moment its first consumer closes.

THE FIX IS ONE COPY OF THE RULE AND NOT FIVE. `common::recv_from_consumer` receives a consumer's
request or drops and reports the consumer that has gone, and all five call it; a sixth driver gets
it by writing the loop the same way. Five independent omissions of the same rule is the evidence
that a rule which is only written down in the drivers that got it right will be missed again.

THE ORACLE IS THE STARVATION AND NOT THE SPIN, because the starvation is what a test can see: the
vsock oracle mints a second consumer the way DeviceManager does, drops the first, and requires the
second to be answered - and then requires the `Disconnect` frame naming the publication's token,
which is the half a driver that only dropped the endpoint still gets wrong. Driven by mutation, and
the mutation's signature is a TIMEOUT rather than an assertion: a driver that keeps the departed
endpoint never goes idle, so the harness dies on its wall clock. That is recorded in the test, so
nobody later loosens it for looking flaky.

AND THE RULE IS CHECKED RATHER THAN REMEMBERED, because five independent omissions is what a rule
kept only inside the code that got it right looks like. `source-hygiene` refuses a file under
`src/user/drivers` that serves a consumer set and never hands an endpoint back - by `close_at` or by
the helper, because the drivers that got it right do not agree on the shape: `xhci` closes at four
sites, `virtio-input` on `PolledCaps::Closed`, `virtio-net` on `Received::Closed` inside a
`ProviderReady` match. THE FIRST VERSION OF THAT RULE PASSED ON A COMMENT - the drivers naming the
helper in the comment above the call satisfied a match that looked anywhere - so it takes the same
leading-content class the literal-address rule beside it uses, and it was made to REFUSE a driver
with the call taken out before it was trusted to approve the tree.

**AND THE BOOTED-SYSTEM TEST FAILS FOR A REASON ITS OWN ASSERTION NAMES NOWHERE (2026-09-19).**
`kernel.boot.init_package_starts_system_manager` carries `[Boot, Service, VolumeLayout]`, and it
fails intermittently on x86_64 and on both aarch64 runs of 2026-09-19, with the same numbers each
time: `every manifest service must report online`, 9 of 24. What the guest log offers a reader above
that line is `ProcessService: no artifact at vol://system/libexec/resource_manager.lsexe`, six more
like it, and `DeviceManager: <driver> is named by the registry and not on the volume` for eleven
driver kinds. EVERY ONE OF THOSE SENTENCES IS FALSE. The volume carries all eighteen.

THE PROOF THAT IT IS NOT THE VOLUME IS THREE RUNS OF THE SAME BYTES. The x86_64 runs at 07:26, 07:31
and 07:38 on 2026-09-19 boot ONE medium, `sha256 5010eb2e...`, against one system volume image. The
middle run reports eighteen things missing; the two either side report one, `wasi_host.lsexe`, which
is deliberate. And reading the names out of the volume image built that day finds
`resource_manager.lsexe`, `session_service.lsexe` and every driver name the middle run called
absent.

WHAT ACTUALLY HAPPENS IS A WATCHDOG CASCADE UNDER A MOUNTED VOLUME. `storage: vol://system mounted
through its block provider` is in the failing log, two hundred lines ABOVE the first "no artifact".
Then `virtio-blk stopped answering its control path inside the deadline its registry entry declares`
for all four of them, each followed by `went away holding published providers; they are withdrawn`,
and then the same for `virtio-console`, `virtio-scsi`, `virtio-snd`, `virtio-vsock`, `xhci`, both
`nvme`, `ahci`, `sdhci` and `hda`. The MOUNT SURVIVES the withdrawal of the thing underneath it:
StorageService goes on answering for `vol://system`, and every lookup through it comes back NOT
PRESENT rather than with an error saying the provider under this mount is gone.

SO THE DEFECT WORTH FIXING IS THE ANSWER AND NOT THE DEADLINE. A driver that misses a deadline under
load is recoverable and the manager recovers it - `restarting virtio-blk` is four lines further on.
What is not recoverable is that nothing remounts; what is not honest is the reply. A reader of that
log concludes the volume was built wrong and goes to look at the build, which is exactly what this
file did twice in one day before the three-run comparison above was made. `not present` and `the
provider under this mount was withdrawn` are different answers and only one of them is true.

AND THE TRIGGER IS CONTENTION, WHICH IS MEASURED. The failing boot reports bind windows of 126 to
177 ticks where a passing boot of the same medium reports 7 to 87. The cascade is OLDER than this
milestone's drivers and it tracks the DEVICE COUNT: nine drivers went away in the runs of
2026-08-27, thirteen on 2026-09-16, sixteen on 2026-09-19 - and over that span the harness gained a
third SCSI target, an IDE disk, a duplex HDA codec and a USB audio function, every one of them
wanted by an item here. What that establishes is that the control-path deadline is now the thing
this boot is closest to, and that the booted-system test is where it shows first because it is the
only test that brings the WHOLE machine up at once.

NOT FIXED HERE AND NOT HIDDEN EITHER. The two halves belong to StorageService's answer and to the
manager's provider-withdrawal path, neither of which is a driver; widening the deadline instead
would be weakening the criterion that caught it.

**AND THE HARNESS ADVERTISES TAGS THE KERNEL REFUSES (2026-09-19).** `./test.sh --list-tags` reads
the tag table out of the kernel source with a character class that stops at a hyphen. So it prints
four names that are not tags at all - `arch`, `capability`, `permission`, `volume` - and hides
eleven that are: `arch-aarch64`, `arch-riscv64`, `arch-x86_64`, `audio-service`, `capability-tcb`,
`dynamic-reject`, `lico-load`, `permission-service`, `process-service`, `volume-layout`,
`volume-scope`. Four of the eleven truncate ONTO A DIFFERENT REAL TAG - `audio`, `dynamic`, `lico`,
`process` - so nothing in the output looks wrong.

WHAT IT COSTS IS THE SELECTOR FOR THE TEST ABOVE. `volume-layout` is how the booted-system test is
run on its own, and it is one of the eleven a reader cannot discover from the tool that exists to
list them; `--tags permission` exits with `test filter error: unknown tag 'permission'`, which is
the kernel correctly refusing a name its own harness had just advertised.

FIXED, AND THE FIX IS GATED RATHER THAN REMEMBERED (2026-09-19). The character class takes the
hyphen, and `check-test-tags` now compares the two sides: it reads the declared wire names out of
the tag table, runs `--list-tags`, and requires the sets to be equal. THAT GATE COULD NOT HAVE
CAUGHT THIS BEFORE, and saying so is the point - its existing checks prove every tag a test USES is
declared, which is a different fact from what a caller is TOLD, and the two had drifted apart with
nothing looking at the gap. The rule is proven to REFUSE first, on three fixtures: a truncated name,
an advertised name the kernel does not have, and an agreeing pair in a different order so it is not
passing by refusing everything. Then the real defect was put back and the gate named both
directions - the four it advertised and the eleven it hid - before it was trusted to approve.

EIGHT OF THEM ARE NOW WRITTEN AND RUNNING ON x86_64 (2026-09-17): NVMe, AHCI, SDHCI, HDA,
virtio-scsi, virtio-vsock, USB CDC-ECM and USB Attached SCSI, each with a host-tested decision core,
a manifest entry and DMA declaration, a QEMU model in the harness, a measured bind window and an
oracle that moves bytes through the device. None is ticked, and the section above says why: what a
driver item closes on is a run on three targets.
THE LAST THREE EACH LEFT SOMETHING BEHIND THAT IS WORTH MORE THAN THE DRIVER. vsock brought the
`local-stream` provider kind and the first oracle in this suite whose far end is a process on the
HOST rather than a model inside QEMU. CDC-ECM brought the shared communications-class decisions,
NCM's transfer-block arithmetic as host tests, and a defect that was not about CDC at all - a
synchronous wait in the controller silently discarding another class module's completion. And UAS
brought xHCI BULK STREAMS, the first two-providers-of-one-kind publication in this tree, and the
discovery that `MAX_INITIAL_OFFERS` was four because four was what the drivers of the day needed.
WHAT LANDED WITH THEM IS WORTH MORE THAN THE THREE DRIVERS. The plain-PCI resource profile, which
every later plain-PCI driver inherits and which cost AHCI one field and SDHCI one row. The canonical
block wire, which replaced three hand-written copies. A torn-completion defect, found in NVMe and
fixed in the already-shipping xHCI driver. Bind windows reported for EVERY driver at every boot,
which every driver item owed and none produced. And the first consumer of the `none` DMA policy,
which had been built and never used.

THE OTHER 21 ARE IMPLEMENTATION WORK AND ARE NOT BLOCKED - whole drivers, each days of work, each
with its own plan to write first: virtio-vsock, -scsi, -crypto, -balloon/-mem, -fs, NVMe, AHCI,
SDHCI, HDA, EHCI, OHCI/UHCI, PCIe hotplug, TPM, NFIT, the USB HID expansion, CDC-ACM, UAS, CDC-ECM/
NCM, USB Audio, the ACPI power button, and the UEFI GOP handoff (two of whose three parts are done).
Twenty-eight open items are not marked blocked: these 21, the ACPI fixed-hardware event-delivery
DECISION, and the six ordering, planning and gate rows at the end of this file.

WHY THIS TABLE IS HERE RATHER THAN IN A READER'S HEAD. Each blocker is already written against each
item it blocks, which is right and is also why nobody can see the shape: "BLOCKED on an unowned X"
read forty-seven times is forty-seven sentences, and the fact that six Xs account for nineteen of
them is not visible from any one of them. Deciding who owns the destination-service vocabulary is
not one item's unblocking, it is thirteen.

CORRECTED 2026-09-13. This line said "NON-COMPLETABLE INDEX. NOT A MILESTONE, NOT A PHASE-2 GATE,
AND NEVER TICKED", and the index carried it as a row that could not be ticked and was counted in no
phase's open work. That is a completion criterion weakened rather than met. A document nobody may
close is a document nobody has to finish, and none of the work below was ever decided to be
optional - what the old line was really describing is that the items have DIFFERENT prerequisites
and close one at a time, which is true and is not the same claim. So: items still close one at a
time against their own prerequisite matrix below, there is still no global hard gate over all of
them, and this document is finished when the last of them is. Saying there was a global gate is
what made non-driver items inherit a driver's dependencies.

Corrected 2026-08-30: this line said "PLANNED AFTER P02M0098, P02M0161 AND P02M0162, WHICH ARE THE
HARD GATE" while the body already declared the file an index, and the two cannot both be true.

This index lists every approved driver candidate whose controller, protocol or device-class interface is standardized
across vendors. It does not promise support for arbitrary real machines: PCI/USB/ACPI/
FDT discovery, IRQ/DMA attachment, clocks, resets, pinmux and power can still require
platform glue, while PHASE 5 owns drivers and quirks for concrete chips, products, SoCs
and boards.

CORRECTED 2026-08-30: this file said "Phase 4" of concrete-hardware work throughout, and the
architecture does not. `docs/CONCEPT_EN.md` makes Phase 4 the DESKTOP PLATFORM on the reference
virtual machine - compositor, shell, the full audio stack over virtio-sound - and states in the same
block that real GPUs and real-hardware audio drivers follow in Phase 5. Phase 5 is where it then puts
device- and board-specific drivers for concrete NIC/GPU/Wi-Fi/audio/storage controllers, support for
specific machines and SBCs, bare-metal ARM64/RISC-V, and per-deployment power management. Every
handoff below that reads "remains Phase 4" therefore means PHASE 5, and each has been changed.
Native VT-d, AMD-Vi and ARM SMMU backends are Phase 5 for the same reason.

AND THIS FILE IS NOT A PHASE-2 COMPLETION SET. Phase 2 is defined as a deliberately small, bounded
universal set chosen for appliance value, and its own definition of done names the capability-scoped
appliance and the QEMU suite on three targets - not NVMe, not TPM, not UVC. So the Phase-2 subset is
named ITEM BY ITEM, and it is NOT "group 1" (corrected 2026-08-30). Assigning a whole group was an
unbounded assignment resting on a false premise: the harness configures virtio-blk, virtio-net,
virtio-gpu, virtio-input, virtio-console, virtio-snd and xHCI, and it does NOT run the proposed RNG,
vsock, SCSI, crypto, balloon or virtio-fs items - so "what the reference virtual machine actually
runs on" was true of seven entries and claimed for the whole group. (virtio-gpu was omitted from the
first correction and is added 2026-08-30: `docs/CONCEPT_EN.md` lists `driver.virtio-gpu` in Phase 2,
the harness configures `virtio-gpu-pci` in the direct and UEFI profiles, and P02M0173 counts display
among the current DMA endpoints. Leaving it out left a shipped Phase-2 driver's remaining work riding
on an unrelated future EDID item.) The group also carries unowned kernel and
ResourceManager mechanisms and a development-only filesystem backend this file itself calls
month-scale, none of which belongs in a phase the architecture defines as deliberately small and
selected by appliance value.

    Phase-2 subset   the drivers the reference machine ALREADY runs, which this index would only be
                     improving - virtio-blk, virtio-net, virtio-gpu, virtio-input, virtio-console,
                     virtio-snd, xHCI - plus the prerequisites they need in order to be improved
                     safely: the host-test seam, P02M0172's declared DMA policy, and the index-state
                     tooling change above.

Every other entry in all three groups, the remaining virtio ones included, is phased backlog: a
candidate whose phase is decided when it is picked up, counted in no Phase-2 completion report.

THE GATE IS THREE SLICES, NOT FORTY-ONE ITEMS. P02M0098 was one milestone carrying the whole of the
driver-binding work, and this one waited on all of it - so no driver could be written until
observability, operator policy and power lifecycle were finished, none of which a driver needs. That
milestone is now seven (see its head for the split), and what a new driver actually requires is the
first three TOGETHER - no one of them is "the foundation": the kernel-owned claim, its generation and
its revocation (P02M0098), the start-and-report handshake (P02M0161), and a bind that rolls back when
it fails (P02M0162). NOT the stop: `STOP`/`STOPPED` is P02M0165's, whole, and P02M0161 says so in its
own goal.

The four that follow - discovery breadth, providers, supervision, observability - are what the driver
SET needs to be usable rather than what any one driver needs to exist, and they may land beside the
drivers rather than in front of them. A driver written here still consumes the registry, the claim,
the transactional bind and the generation, and none may add a private launch path, singleton service
handoff, source `match` arm or product-ID-first binding shortcut.

AND THIS IS AN INDEX, NOT ONE PIECE OF WORK. Driver candidates across three groups, plus the
prerequisites named in the classification below - and several of the candidates are families rather
than single drivers, so "done when every item is done" would state a direction rather than something
anybody finishes.

THE COUNT IS NOT AN INVARIANT AND IS NOT MAINTAINED ANYWHERE (2026-08-30, and this time the numbers
were actually removed). This said "forty-two checkboxes", then "forty-one driver candidates", and
`docs/todo/TODO.md` carried the second - so a round that declared the count unmaintained left two
copies of it in place. Both are gone. A count in prose that nothing recomputes is a fact that rots;
what matters is that every entry is CLASSIFIED, and that is checkable by reading the entry.

AND THE INDEX STATE IS A TOOLING CHANGE THIS FILE OWNS, NOT A SENTENCE (2026-08-30). Two rounds have
now declared this row "never ticked from this list and counted in no phase's completion", and nothing
implements that: `docs/todo/TODO.md` carries it as `- [~]`, and `check-milestone-index.sh` treats
`[~]` as an ordinary OPEN state - its own comment says so - and fails only the `[x]`-over-open-tasks
direction. So a tool and a reader both still see an open Phase-2 milestone. The bounded deliverable
that closes this is an ITEM in the ordering section below - an explicitly SUPPORTED index state for a
non-completable reference row, taught to the index checker so the marker keeps its title check and
cannot be read as phase-open, with a watched-fail case proving the checker refuses an unknown marker
rather than silently accepting one. Until that lands, the claim in the roadmap row is a statement of
intent and says so.

SO IT IS A CROSS-PHASE UMBRELLA AND NOT A PHASE-2 GATE, and it is listed under Phase 2 because that
is where its first items belong, not because Phase 2 waits for its last one. Phase 2's own definition
of done names the capability-scoped appliance and the QEMU suite on three targets; nothing in it
requires NVMe, TPM or UVC. STARTING WORK HERE MEANS PICKING ONE ITEM, not starting "M0099": the item
states what it requires, what it owns and its one integration gate, and it closes on its own.

## Per-item prerequisites and current acceptance ledger

Rechecked 2026-09-08 against the prerequisite contracts and their current production paths. The
three binding slices are the floor for a DRIVER, not a global gate for every entry in this index.
A parser, shared library or architectural decision inherits only the contracts it actually uses.

| Item class or operation | Required contract |
| --- | --- |
| Every driver | P02M0098 claim/generation/revocation, P02M0161 start/report and P02M0162 transactional bind/rollback; P02M0172's required registry DMA policy, including `none` for a driver that must never master the bus |
| Every driver, on each claimed target | P02M0162's measured bind-window budget for that target; x86_64, aarch64 and riscv64 acceptance each need their own measurement |
| Any DMA-capable driver | P02M0153's DMA/IOVA, admission, completion and quarantine contract in addition to the P02M0172 declaration |
| Plain-PCI driver | P02M0163 discovery breadth and the resource-profile extension owned by its first consumer below |
| Firmware-node-bound driver | P02M0163's discovery boundary plus the separately approved device-node identity prerequisite below |
| Publishes or consumes a provider | P02M0164 catalogue, per-consumer `open`, connection accounting, withdrawal and requires-edges, plus the destination-specific lifecycle work below |
| Stops, removes or is operator-disabled | P02M0165 STOP/STOPPED, bounded drain and confirmed/quarantined settlement; P02M0166 operator policy and observable binding state |
| Accepted on three targets | P02M0167 run identity, exact selection/tags, immutable artifacts and recorded results; its live concurrent-selection proof is discharged below, while parallel acceptance still inherits P02M0170's open medium-input acquisition condition |

A prerequisite is its CONTRACT, not its status line and not a fixed list of old audit findings.
Every item checks the applicable contract and this ledger before closing. A verified defect or an
unmet required acceptance proof is added with evidence; a repair is recorded with its date and
removes only that defect. Historical findings are not permanent gates. A narrower host-testable
composition accepted by the prerequisite is not replaced here by a demand for an unavailable direct
host call into a `no_std` service binary.

### What ticking a driver item costs, which is why two finished ones are not ticked

STATED HERE BECAUSE IT IS A PROPERTY OF THIS FILE RATHER THAN OF ANY ITEM IN IT (2026-09-16). The
precedent is explicit and is the one this file set for itself: `virtio-blk maintenance` carried
"IMPLEMENTED 2026-09-09, awaiting its three-target suite run before it is ticked" and was ticked only
after that run. So a driver item is not closed by working; it is closed by working ON THREE TARGETS.

THAT MAKES THE SLOW-TARGET RUNS PART OF EVERY DRIVER ITEM'S COMPLETION AND NOT A FINAL SWEEP, and
the two written this week are where it shows. NVMe, AHCI, SDHCI, HDA and virtio-scsi each have a host-tested decision core, a
manifest entry and DMA declaration, a QEMU model wired into the harness, an oracle that writes a
sector and reads it back, and a measured bind window. On x86_64 they are finished. None is ticked,
and the only thing missing from any of them is the run on aarch64 and riscv64.

THE CONSEQUENCE IS WORTH SEEING PLAINLY: if those runs are deferred until this file is complete, this
file cannot become complete, because completing it means ticking items that the runs are what closes.
The two orderings that resolve it are a decision rather than a discovery - run the slow targets at
each driver item's boundary, which is what the precedent above describes, or accept a target-by-target
close and tick an item on x86_64 with the other two recorded as outstanding. This file does not choose
between them; it records that one of them has to be chosen, and that the choice is not the
implementer's to make quietly.

UNTIL IT IS MADE, THE RATIONAL ORDER IS TO BATCH. Every driver finished before the next slow-target
run closes in that run rather than needing one of its own, so the cost of the decision falls with
every item written in the meantime.

### Outstanding acceptance conditions

- **P02M0172: LANDED, AND THIS CONDITION WAS STALE** (rechecked 2026-09-16). The bullet below stood
  for six days after the thing it waits for arrived, and it is the load-bearing half of this
  section: read as written it says every one of the twenty-one open driver items is gated on a
  mechanism nobody has built, which is why "which driver do I start" had no good answer. P02M0172 is
  COMPLETE (2026-09-10) and the mechanism is in the tree rather than in its status line:
  `abi::DMA_POLICY_NONE`, `DMA_POLICY_IOMMU_REQUIRED` and `DMA_POLICY_TRUSTED_UNTRANSLATED` are
  declared, `kernel/dma_policy/` resolves each to a `Policy` and enforces it at admission, and its
  suite holds the mode/policy matrix, refusal by name, admission by declaration rather than by rank,
  the manifest migration table and the degraded-isolation record. THE `none` POLICY EXISTS, so the
  sentence "only the planned `none` policy can forbid that capability" now describes something a
  driver declares rather than something it waits for.
  WHAT A DRIVER ITEM STILL OWES IS ITS OWN DECLARATION, which is work inside the item: an entry
  naming the device, its policy, and the rules that say which devices it is declared for.
- **P02M0162: ALSO LANDED, AND THE MEASUREMENT IS A STEP INSIDE EACH ITEM RATHER THAN A WAIT**
  (rechecked 2026-09-16). P02M0162 is COMPLETE (2026-09-09) and its M5 is closed. The constants this
  bullet named are the ones M5 itself calls obsolete: the current inputs are 3,000 ticks for x86_64,
  4,000 for aarch64 and 40,000 for riscv64, they are the `boot_userspace` arguments, and the tree
  agrees - `main.rs` calls `boot_userspace(3000)`, `arch/aarch64/boot.rs` calls `(4000)` and
  `arch/riscv64/boot.rs` calls `(40000)`. So "300/400/4000 round counts consumed as ticks" described
  a setting that is no longer there.
  **AND THE MEASUREMENT IS NOW MADE, FOR EVERY DRIVER, ON x86_64** (2026-09-16). It was owed by every
  driver item and produced by none, because nothing in the running system reported it: the incident
  report carries a silence interval and only once something has already gone wrong. DeviceManager now
  stamps the tick `BIND` goes out and prints the window when `READY` is admitted - the ordinary path,
  every driver, every boot - so the number a driver item has to record is in the boot log rather than
  in a measurement somebody has to arrange.
  MEASURED OVER THREE BOOTS. The timer runs at 100 Hz on every architecture, so a tick is ten
  milliseconds, and READY's maximum from BIND is two seconds, which is 200 ticks:

      driver           window (ticks)   of the 200-tick budget
      ahci                 17                 9%
      nvme                 19 - 22           11%
      xhci                 30 - 31           16%
      virtio_rng           38 - 40           20%
      virtio_snd           38 - 41           21%
      virtio_console       35 - 44           22%
      virtio_blk            6 - 11            6%

  EVERY DRIVER THIS MACHINE BINDS SITS INSIDE A FIFTH OF ITS BUDGET, and the two written this week
  are the fastest of them, which is what a polled bring-up with no interrupt handshake costs. The
  spread within a driver is the machine rather than the driver: `virtio_blk` binds four times a boot
  and the four differ by five ticks.
  THIS DISCHARGES THE CONDITION FOR x86_64 AND FOR NO OTHER TARGET. aarch64 and riscv64 each need
  their own measurement, on their own hardware constants - 4,000 and 40,000 ticks of outer window
  against x86_64's 3,000 - and those runs are deliberately left until the whole job is done.

  THE REQUIREMENT ITSELF SURVIVES THE CORRECTION and is restated without the false premise: M5 makes
  no hardware timing claim, so a driver item measures its own bind window on each target it claims
  acceptance on and records the number. That is a step the item performs, not a prerequisite owned
  elsewhere, and reading it as a wait is what turned a per-item task into a global gate. The
  synchronous kernel release is not a defect: P02M0162 M4 deliberately requires it, with bounded
  hardware work and quarantine on expiry.
  NEITHER CORRECTION TICKS ANY DRIVER ITEM. Twenty-one drivers are still unwritten and the four
  unowned prerequisites tabled at the head of this file still block seventeen items. What changes is
  that the remaining items are blocked by what is missing from them rather than by two milestones
  that are finished.
- **P02M0165: retain the shutdown-settlement negative-control evidence condition.**
  Both withdrawal oracles now exist: `dev.gpu-restart` asserts the live DisplayService announcement,
  and development `device_manager/tests.rs::unopened_provider_withdrawal` calls production
  `Catalogue::withdraw_binding` with real channel handles and asserts peer closure, duplicate/old
  generation safety, late-subscriber emptiness and the actual withdrawal frame. DeviceManager runs
  that check at development boot; the restart harness requires its success marker. The same harness
  requires `pending_shutdown_outcomes`, which drives production settlement with a ready one-handle
  remainder and an unconfirmed timeout. These positive oracles have now executed: P02M0165's
  2026-09-08 closure records the missing-close negative and restored startup/shutdown; the retained
  `/tmp/libersystem-final-dev-recheck-result.json` records all startup markers and successful
  shutdown, and `/tmp/libersystem-final-dev-recheck-checks.log` records `dev.gpu-restart` passing with
  withdrawal, rebind and actual presentation. Credit those executed effects. The separate deliberate
  mutation of production shutdown settlement has not been verified in the retained records reviewed
  here and remains the specific negative-control evidence condition. This does not demand another
  first positive run or rebuilding either oracle. The combined development command's later
  performance failure is recorded by P02M0165 and is not treated as a passed whole-command result.
- **P02M0170: the medium-input producer/acquisition boundary and its mutation proof remain open.**
  Staged kernel/loader inputs and content-keyed USB/system-disk generations close their own races.
  P02M0167's ordinary live same-architecture selection/tag proof passed on 2026-09-08 and is credited
  below alongside the existing host overlap/mutation coverage. The wider medium boundary is still missing:
  `mkimage.sh` reads shared init/volume packages, the manifest and fallback bootstrap files, and its
  assembly lock does not cover their producers. Its after-assembly key comparison detects a moved
  input and fails instead of making both concurrent runs succeed. P02M0170 M1/M2 own the remaining
  producer-coordinated acquisition/snapshot and the stronger concurrent-selection proof with inputs
  mutated during acquisition and assembly. Until that repair and gate pass, acceptance retains the
  conservative serialized operating condition: one guest architecture at a time without overlapping
  selection or medium-input production. The successful ordinary overlap, fixed disk templates,
  scheduler host gate and catalogue repair do not establish stability under producer mutation.

### Current contracts and discharged findings

- **P02M0153 dedicated topology, corrected 2026-09-08.** The enforcing hostile fixture sets
  `IOMMU=1 DMA_FIXTURE=1`; `qemu-run.sh` omits preparation and attachment of the system-volume
  virtio-blk disk before the optional-device cutoff. Its test kernel enters the test suite directly
  and reads its archive from the boot medium. The remaining allowed participants are firmware SATA,
  the controller, EDU and virtio-net, as M2 requires. The extra-block-endpoint defect in the latest
  audit is discharged, not an open DMA prerequisite. Every DMA item's acceptance still requires
  current evidence from the named enforcing gate. Adding a translated virtio-blk positive oracle
  below must leave this hostile fixture unchanged.
- **P02M0153 fault attribution, corrected 2026-09-08.** The former bounded 64-event attribution
  allowance is removed from M4 and from this ledger. The current contract refuses replacement
  attachment while the predecessor's fault tail is undrained, uses bounded drain attempts and keeps
  bus mastering disabled on refusal. The ending domain remains associated through the final drain
  and count copy, preventing double accounting. Driver items inherit this drain-before-replacement
  contract, not permission to charge faults to another binding within a guessed window.
- **P02M0165 withdrawal announcement and shutdown indexing, discharged 2026-09-08.**
  DisplayService handles `live: false` and the restart gate checks its exact announcement. Current
  `settle_shutdown_node` polls process readiness with `wait` and claim state with `device_claim_info`
  before `advance`, then uses `wait_any` only to wake the next pass. It never tests `ready > 0` and
  therefore does not discard the zero-based first ready index. `stop_all` settles every applicable
  node under one overall stop-plus-teardown bound. The concrete-close effects also have the retained
  negative/restored-positive evidence above; the remaining evidence condition concerns the separate
  shutdown-settlement negative control, not a missing close oracle.
- **P02M0166 policy composition, discharged 2026-09-08.** Its definition of done deliberately
  extracts the decisions from DeviceManager into `driver-binding`. The registered
  `a_selection_and_a_granted_attempt_survive_the_sequences_that_used_to_spend_them` test drives live
  selection through the next bind, retry through a missing dependency with exactly one attempt, and
  policy on a quarantined binding. Production consumes those same rules. The ordinary `CAP_CONFIG`
  rejection has its kernel test, `the_reserved_device_policy_namespace_answers_only_its_owner`.
  The previous demand for a direct host invocation of production `apply_policy` exceeded this
  accepted contract and is removed. P02M0166 remains a dependency for operator-controlled drivers;
  it has no outstanding finding in this ledger.
- **P02M0167 catalogue variants, discharged 2026-09-08.** Activation calls the shared
  `candidate::evidence_failures` before any write. Its catalogue comparison preserves the complete
  `(check, architecture, environment, configuration)` identity and detects a lost variant while
  the check and its covers remain. The real-registry regression removes one configuration without
  removing the check and asserts that its covered component loses evidence. This repaired defect
  is independent of the open P02M0170 medium-input acquisition condition.
- **P02M0167 live concurrent selection/tags, discharged 2026-09-08.** Its M3 live-guest corrective
  execution and combined verification closure record the actual `concurrent-selection` catalogue
  gate as passed. The retained `/tmp/libersystem-final-concurrency-recheck.log` reports two
  overlapping x86_64 suites with different exact selections and `dma`/`domain` tags, each passing
  with its own medium and result logs, followed by the recorded passed catalogue key. The two named
  runner logs also show distinct content-addressed ISOs and QEMU exit 33. The production gate checks
  that each guest executed its own selected IDs and none of the other selection's IDs. This is
  executed guest evidence, so no driver item owes a first execution of that same proof. It does not
  discharge P02M0170's separate acquisition/assembly boundary or its deliberate producer mutations.
- **Previously discharged 2026-09-03 and 2026-09-04:** P02M0098 forced-release/MMIO retention and
  claim-time counter baselines; P02M0153 fault-service latency, stale device-wide carry and malformed
  completion handling; P02M0162 permanent-failure transitions, duplicate terminal frames, missing
  artifact after backoff, and recovery-window opening/reset; P02M0166 enable overwriting an existing
  dependency stop; P02M0167 scheduler cost/order, guest-slot accounting, zero budget, failed-duration
  storage, separate gate/conformance steps, and registry ownership/architecture narrowing detection.
  These are historical repairs, not permission to skip the current prerequisite contracts or the
  acceptance conditions above.

Each item states which of these it needs and does not inherit the others. Each item is closable on
its own and ships on its own; THIS FILE NEVER CLOSES, and nothing in it waits for the whole of it.
("The milestone closes when its items do" stood here and contradicted the index rule three hundred
lines below it - a completion condition stated in the introduction is the one a reader believes.) The groups fix PRIORITY, not permission: an item in group 3 whose prerequisites are met
is not held by an unfinished item in group 1 it does not need.

AND THE ITEMS ARE NOT ALL DRIVERS, so one driver-shaped Definition of Done cannot be applied to all
of them. Every checkbox is classified, and the classification decides which gate closes it:

    driver                  binds a device, claims it, publishes or consumes providers
    in-controller
      class module          touches a real device THROUGH a controller's claim and holds none of
                              its own (added 2026-08-30 - a USB class driver under the first USB
                              execution model is neither a driver nor a shared library, and without
                              this kind it inherited gates it cannot satisfy or a parser's gates
                              that prove nothing; its contract is in the USB section)
    shared library          a parser or engine with no device and no binding (EDID, a SCSI
                              layer, a HID report parser)
    kernel/bus infra        changes the binder, the claim, discovery or DMA admission
    destination service     the service side a driver's output must reach
    architectural
      prerequisite          a decision or mechanism that is not a driver at all (the USB
                              execution model, the AML interpreter, the identity discriminant)

A shared library closes on host tests and hostile fixtures and never on a bind. An in-controller
class module closes on the contract in the USB section - controller-driven lifecycle, a bounded
per-class resource budget and an observable effect - and never on an independent bind. A prerequisite
closes on the decision being recorded and the mechanism existing, not on a device appearing.

## What the binder does not reach yet, and who extends it

Measured 2026-08-28, because three of the four things groups 2 and 3 assume were explicitly REFUSED
by [P02M0163](P02M0163.md) and are not arriving from anywhere else.

- **A claimable device exists for every PCI function; a RESOURCED one does not.** Corrected
  2026-08-30: this said NVMe, AHCI and HDA "have no entry", and P02M0163 closed that - `device.rs`
  now pushes a `DeviceEntry` for every remaining function of the scan, carrying the class triple and
  the address under `TRANSPORT_PLAIN_PCI`. What those entries carry is `bar_phys: 0, bar_len: 0,
  msix_cap: 0`: the function is claimable and has NO resource profile, so there is still nothing to
  hand any of them a BAR or an MSI-X vector through. That, and not the entry, is what the first
  plain-PCI item owns.
- **There is no firmware inventory.** P02M0163 refuses ACPI and FDT in as many words: the FDT parser
  produces a `BootInfo` - RAM banks, CPU ids, controller addresses - not an inventory with
  `compatible` strings a rule could match, and there is no AML namespace on x86_64 at all.
- **The binding unit is a PCI function.** `BindingId` is bus, device, function and generation. A USB
  interface, an NVMe namespace and a hotplug slot are not expressible in it.
- **There is no arrival or removal event.** The PCI scan runs once at boot. P02M0163 refuses hotplug
  because a milestone promising it would have had to build the mechanism as well.

- **A PROVIDER CAN BE WITHDRAWN AND THE CATALOGUE NOW SAYS SO; NO DRIVER DOES IT.** Corrected
  2026-08-30 - P02M0164 closed most of this and the rest of the paragraph would otherwise send the
  first implementer to build what is already there. `Withdraw` is opcode 6 of the driver protocol and
  DeviceManager handles `BindingEvent::Withdrawn`; the catalogue's `subscribe` is a SERVED stream
  carrying the snapshot and the live additions, `provider-info` now carries a `live` flag so a
  withdrawal travels on that same stream identified by slot and provider generation, and a
  `requires` edge both WAKES a `DependencyPending` node when its provider appears and STOPS an online
  one when its provider is withdrawn. A per-consumer `open` mints a connection per consumer with a
  declared consumer limit, so a second or late consumer is no longer stranded IN SHAPE.

  **AND ITS CONNECTION ACCOUNTING HAS LANDED; THE FIRST-HANDOFF RULE IS WRITTEN HERE SO THE NEXT
  MIGRATION READS IT RATHER THAN REDERIVING IT** (corrected 2026-08-31). The previous version of this
  paragraph declared the accounting a BROKEN PREREQUISITE in three places and assigned the repair to
  the first destination migration. That migration happened - AudioService - and the repair went with
  it. All three are closed, and the paragraph is kept rather than deleted because what replaced them
  is a RULE later items must follow:

  - THE INITIAL CONNECTION IS COUNTED, and the rule is that EXISTING PLUS PROMISED is the number
    every minting path checks. Publication initialises `consumers` to zero and the offered channel
    the driver made itself is counted separately while it still sits in the entry; the two together
    are `outstanding`. So a provider declaring one consumer cannot have its initial holder and one
    `open` alive at once, which is what the old zero-plus-uncounted-offer arrangement allowed.
  - THE OFFERED CHANNEL IS THE FIRST PUBLIC CONNECTION, and served `open` is where it is handed out.
    This is the first-public-handoff rule, and it is one rule rather than two paths: `open` MOVES the
    offered endpoint to the asking consumer and counts it, and only mints a new pair once that one is
    gone. It is never DUPLICATED, because a duplicate shares the driver's reply queue with whoever
    holds the original - the exact failure the per-consumer factory exists to prevent. Without this,
    the public factory could not deliver the first connection for a default single-consumer provider
    at all: the one connection it may serve was reachable only through the private `Catalogue::take`.
  - EVERY MINTING PATH IS ACCOUNTED, THE BOOT'S OWN INCLUDED. `Catalogue::take`, `mint_connection`
    and served `open` all check the driver's declared bound before handing anything out and all
    increment on success; `Catalogue::disconnected` gives the place back when a driver reports a
    consumer's endpoint closing, which is what makes reconnect expressible. The boot probe was the
    one path that minted without consulting or incrementing anything, so a declared bound was
    understated before the first public ask; it is checked now, and a driver asked for more
    connections than its entry declares is a manifest that is wrong about the driver.
  - THE MANIFEST BOUND IS RECONCILED AT BUILD TIME. `system-manifest` carries the same
    `MAX_PROVIDER_CLIENTS = 8` the drivers' served set is sized to and refuses an entry declaring
    more, so a count that is accepted at build time is one the driver can actually serve.

  So P02M0164 is MET as a mechanism, and what remains is per DESTINATION. A destination migration no
  longer owns a repair - it owns its own subscribe, attach, detach and reconnect, and it can rely on
  the accounting above being there.
  WHAT IS ACTUALLY MISSING IS THE PRODUCING SIDE AND THE MIGRATION. No driver sends the message: xHCI
  creates its block channel unconditionally, "Always created" by its own comment, answers with an
  error status when no stick is attached, and on a detach drops its storage state and leaves the
  publication standing. Destination discovery has since moved: NetworkService and InputService open
  their initial providers through catalogue snapshots; AudioService watches audio publications; and
  DisplayService subscribes and reacquires a display connection after a driver restart. These are
  partial lifecycle migrations, not the complete multi-provider contract. The destination table
  below owns the remaining identity, live selection, detach/failover and reconnect work.

- **THE ONE RULE THIS MILESTONE STATES IS BROKEN BY THE MECHANISM EVERY NEW DRIVER MUST USE.** The
  head says no driver may add a "source `match` arm", and resources are handed out by exactly that:
  a literal name list - `driver_name == b"virtio_input" || driver_name == b"virtio_net" || ...` -
  decides which drivers get an MSI-X vector. A new driver that needs an interrupt has no way to say
  so except by being added to it, so the rule describes an intention rather than a constraint.

AND ONE OF THESE IS NOT A NARROW EXTENSION, WHICH THE RULE BELOW MUST NOT SWALLOW (added
2026-08-30). The binding unit being a PCI function is not a gap a driver widens on its way past. The
identity is BDF plus generation in `BindingId`; provider origin is bus/device/function in
`provider-info`; the manifest's match vocabulary has exactly two transports, `virtio-pci` and
`plain-pci`, and every predicate beside them is a PCI field; and the kernel's claim and release
address a PCI function. A PL011, a firmware-described 16550, an ACPI namespace device, an I2C child,
a USB interface and an NVMe namespace are not one row added to that - they are a DISCRIMINATED
identity, and changing the identity type reaches matching, claim and release, binding ids, provider
origins, policy keys, diagnostics, revocation and generation handling at once.

Owning that inside whichever UART or storage driver arrives first is how a system-wide name acquires
one driver's shape: every later item then inherits whatever that driver happened to need, and the
non-PCI items that follow have no sound public identity at all. So it is an ARCHITECTURAL
PREREQUISITE with its own approval, not a driver's bullet - stated here, owned elsewhere, and named
by every item that needs it:

    a discriminated, stable device-node identity carried through matching, claim/release,
    binding ids, provider origins, policy, diagnostics and generation, with transport-specific
    resource and teardown hooks, and a manifest match vocabulary that can express a
    firmware-described node without pretending it is a PCI function.

Until it exists, the items that need it are BLOCKED and say so; the plain-PCI resource profile below
does NOT need it, because a plain-PCI function is already expressible.

THE RULE FOR EVERYTHING ELSE, AND IT IS THE WHOLE ANSWER: the FIRST driver of a family that needs one
of these owns the NARROW extension it needs and says so in its own bullet.

**AND "FIRST" MEANS FIRST IMPLEMENTED, NEVER A NAMED DRIVER** (corrected 2026-08-30). A previous
round wrote the rule and then fixed three of its instances to particular drivers - the plain-PCI
resource profile to NVMe, the SCSI core to virtio-SCSI, the I2C contract to HID-over-I2C - while the
same round said elsewhere that a non-gating order lets AHCI, HDA, SDHCI or another eligible consumer
land first. Both cannot be true, and the fixed name is the one that breaks: it makes an item wait on
a driver nobody has started, or lets two parallel items each believe the other owns the contract.
The rule is therefore applied uniformly and in every occurrence, including the tables below:

    the plain-PCI RESOURCE PROFILE - the BAR and MSI-X vector a plain-PCI entry does not carry - is
      owned by the first implemented plain-PCI item, whichever it is. NVMe is the LIKELIEST because
      it stands first in the list, and it is not the owner by name. This is a NARROW EXTENSION and
      not a new identity: a plain-PCI function is already expressible, which is exactly the
      distinction the firmware-node identity fails
    the SCSI core is owned by the first implemented SCSI consumer - virtio-SCSI, UAS or another
    the I2C controller contract is owned by the first implemented I2C consumer - HID-over-I2C,
      SSIF or another

  The firmware-node identity is NOT under this rule. It is the architectural prerequisite stated
  above, it is owned by NO DRIVER, and it HAS NO NUMBER YET - which is stated plainly here because a
  previous round wrote "it has ONE separately numbered owner" and no such number exists anywhere in
  the roadmap (corrected 2026-08-30). Claiming an owner that does not exist is worse than admitting
  there is none: it reads as scheduled work. It is an UNOWNED BLOCKED PREREQUISITE, and every
  consumer of it is marked BLOCKED in its own bullet, on the same terms as the missing destination
  services - a blocked item may be planned and may not be started, and what unblocks it is an
  approved and numbered milestone rather than a driver deciding to build it.

  Its consumers, marked as such in their bullets: `16550` (the firmware-described variants),
  `ARM PL011`, `HID over I2C`, and every group-2 and group-3 item that binds through an ACPI or FDT
  node rather than a PCI function. The x86 legacy-port `16550` is NOT one of them - it needs no
  firmware identity, and its own bullet already records the three other things it does
  need. A previous round said "owned elsewhere" and "owned by no driver" and
  then, one paragraph later, that whichever UART is implemented first builds it - which is the fixed-
  name rule applied to the one contract this file says must not have it. `16550` and `ARM PL011` are
  both CONSUMERS of that prerequisite and neither builds it.

AND ONE PATH NEEDS NO FIRMWARE NODE AT ALL - BUT IT IS NOT CHEAP, AND THIS SAID IT WAS (corrected
2026-08-30). An x86 16550 at the legacy port is the UART the kernel's own early console already uses,
at an address it already knows, so it needs no firmware identity. It needs three things nothing in
this tree has:
a RANGE-SCOPED, REVOCABLE PORT-I/O AUTHORITY - the public driver resource vocabulary is device MMIO,
one IRQ, keys, system power and console, and there is no port-I/O object or syscall at all;
LEGACY FIXED-IRQ ROUTING AND ITS REVOCATION, which the MSI-X path does not provide;
and AN ATOMIC HANDOFF, because the x86 kernel programs COM1 directly, owns IRQ4 and the receive path,
drains transmit and polls receive from the idle hook after userspace has started - ownership that is
monotonic today and has no transfer step. A userspace driver given raw access without one races the
kernel for the same registers and the same interrupt.
So the legacy slice is NOT the cheap one. Either it owns those three mechanisms and a stated
panic-only fallback and reacquisition rule, or the first 16550 item is scoped to FIRMWARE-DESCRIBED
MMIO UARTs - which needs the identity prerequisite instead - and the legacy path is a later item that
says what it takes. What it may not do is start on the assumption that a claimable entry is all it
needs. There is no `USB hotplug` checkbox and two
rounds have now named a wrong owner for it. "The first group-3 item written" is wrong for the reason
this same file states two hundred lines earlier: group 3 contains HID over I2C, which is not USB and
attaches nothing to a USB bus. The catalogue subscribe, an `OFFER` on attach, a `WITHDRAW` and a
closed child server on detach, and a new provider generation on reattach are owned by **THE FIRST
ITEM THAT ACTUALLY ATTACHES A USB CHILD AT RUNTIME** - the USB execution-model prerequisite if it
lands first, otherwise the first USB class slice. A sub-function binding unit arrives with the first driver that is actually split. And the DECLARATIVE
RESOURCE PROFILE - the registry entry says what the driver needs and the manager reads it - is owned
by THE FIRST NEW DRIVER THAT WOULD OTHERWISE REQUIRE A CHANGE TO THE NAME LIST, which is a wider
trigger than the one written here first. "A shape the list cannot express" would have let a driver
with an ordinary MSI-X need be handled by appending one more name, which is exactly the edit the
head's rule forbids - so the rule would have been broken by every driver that obeyed the trigger. What is refused is a universal discovery framework
built ahead of the drivers that would show what it has to hold - which is the shape P02M0163 declined
for exactly this reason.

## 0. Phase-2 maintenance: the seven drivers that already ship

The head names a Phase-2 subset of drivers this index "would only be improving", and a subset with no
items is not a subset - a previous round listed the names and gave them no scope, no owner and no
gate, so Phase-2 work could not be scheduled or accepted (corrected 2026-08-30). These are BOUNDED
MAINTENANCE items over drivers that exist and boot today, not new drivers, and each closes on the
known defects of one driver rather than on a family.

Every item here is subject to the same three-slice claim/handshake/rollback prerequisites as any
other driver item, plus P02M0172's declared DMA policy - `none` included, since these are the
drivers whose policy rows have to be migrated first.

- [x] **The host-test seam** (SHARED LIBRARY - closes on host tests, binds nothing). Owned here and
  built first, because every item below is gated on host tests that cannot currently run. Its
  contract, and the reason it is here rather than with "whoever arrives first", are in the ordering
  section's host-test paragraph.
  DONE (2026-09-09), the cfg/feature design: `rt` gained the feature `host-tests`, which drops the
  entry stubs, the allocation-error hook, the panic handler and the global-allocator registration
  and turns every syscall into an `ERR_UNSUPPORTED` answer - there is no kernel on the host; the
  driver crate enables it through its `[dev-dependencies]` entry for `rt` alone, so cargo unifies
  it into `cargo test` and into no shipped program, and marks its `no_std` binaries `test = false`.
  The gate the design owes runs: `cargo test --manifest-path src/user/drivers/core/Cargo.toml` from
  the repository root, and the model derives `host.drivers` from it. The fake-controller seam is
  `Queue::over` in `drivers::virtio`, a ring over test-owned memory with the device's half written
  by hand.

- [x] **virtio-blk maintenance.** THE ITEM PLAN IS WRITTEN (2026-08-31), which is what the rule in
  the ordering section requires before any code.
  IMPLEMENTED 2026-09-09, awaiting its three-target suite run before it is ticked: DRV-001 is
  `check_used_element`/`check_used_advance` in the shared virtio path, applied by `take_used` and
  `submit` for every driver, with the id, the length and the index each refused against what the
  driver posted; DRV-002's block half is `drivers::blk::request_range` - a count of zero or above
  the request limit and a range past the last sector are refused with `STATUS_INVALID`, never
  clamped; DRV-003 / WIRE-002 is `drivers::blk::write_source` over one `object_info` answer - type,
  rights and size are three separate refusals before anything is mapped. Each has a host test named
  for it and each test was watched to fail under a mutation of its check. The DMA policy row is
  `dma = "trusted-untranslated"` in the manifest (P02M0172), and admission reads the generated
  registry. THE ORACLE: the isolation gate's traffic phase now attaches `virtio-blk-pci` with
  `iommu_platform=on` carrying the paired bootable system volume, identifies the provider by the
  address in its own online line, requires the kernel's attach line for that address and
  StorageService's line that the partition table and superblocks were read over that provider's
  channel before the mount succeeded.

    REQUIRES   P02M0098 + P02M0161 + P02M0162 (claim, handshake, rollback); P02M0153's DMA/IOVA
                 contract AND P02M0172's declared DMA policy - BOTH, because virtio-blk masters the
                 bus and the matrix's DMA row names the pair (corrected 2026-09-01: this list carried
                 only P02M0172, so the one item declared ready to start contradicted the row it
                 claims to inherit. P02M0153 owns `DmaAddress`, the IOVA lifecycle and the three
                 admission outcomes; P02M0172 owns the mode this driver's entry declares. A driver
                 that has the second without the first has a policy and no contract to apply it to);
                 `rt::object_info`, which already reports a memory object's type, rights and size;
                 and the host-test seam, which is this subsection's first item and gates every host
                 test below
    OWNS       the three-part transferred-object contract in `virtio_blk::serve_write`; the count
                 and LBA-range validation on both block request paths; and CENTRAL VIRTQUEUE
                 VALIDATION in the shared virtio path, which no family owns and which this item owns
                 because it is the first Phase-2 item in that code with a gate attached
    GATE       `check.sh --gate qemu-virtio-iommu-x86_64` for the observable effect, plus the named
                 host tests below - each debt ID has its own case and each is watched to fail

  CLASSIFICATION: `driver` (maintenance of one that ships) AND `DMA-capable`, so BOTH prerequisite
  rows apply - the every-driver row and the DMA row - along with the three measured P02M0162 tick
  budgets. Naming only the first is what let the `REQUIRES` block above drop P02M0153: a
  classification that omits a row omits its prerequisites, and this per-item block is the executable
  list an implementer works from.
  ITS ACCEPTANCE CONTRACT INCLUDES THE DMA HALF: every buffer this driver hands the device is a live
  IOVA mapping in its own binding's domain, and the teardown returns them - which is P02M0153's
  contract, tested here rather than assumed from the enforcing gate's presence.

  WHAT IT OWNS, FILE BY FILE:
  - `src/user/drivers/core/src/virtio_blk.rs` - `serve_write`'s source-object contract and the count
    and LBA validation on read and write;
