  arithmetic; and a sense KEY acted on without the additional code that qualifies it, where NOT READY
  covers both a unit spinning up and one with no medium at all.

  THE DRIVER BINDS, IDENTIFIES AND READS ITS MEDIUM'S SIZE. The boot carries
  `driver.virtio-scsi: online (00:0e.0, target 0, 8192 x 512 bytes)` - the four-megabyte medium the
  harness attaches, out of READ CAPACITY - with a bind window of 52 ticks. Landing it also classified
  the TRANSITIONAL virtio-scsi PCI id: QEMU presents `virtio-scsi-pci` as 0x1004 by default rather
  than the modern 0x1048, and the kernel's own test caught the scan meeting a virtio device it could
  not name.

  **AND THE WRITE WORKS NOW. WHAT IT WAS IS WORTH MORE THAN THE DRIVER.**
  The used-element check refused every write with "the device reported more written than the chain
  offered", while the same chain shape carried the data-IN command that reads the capacity. The
  driver's frame was measured and correct - `req 51 resp 108`, which is 19 + `cdb_size` and
  12 + `sense_size` exactly - so the frame was never the question.
  WHAT THE USED LENGTH MEANS IS PART OF THE DEVICE'S SPECIFICATION AND NOT OF THE RING'S. Most
  devices report what they WROTE, which is what this tree's automatic bound assumes and what
  `virtio-blk` and `virtio-snd` do. `virtio-scsi` reports the DATA AND THE RESPONSE, in both
  directions: a read of eight bytes reports 8 + 108 and a write of a sector reports 512 + 108. The
  automatic bound accepts the first and refuses the second, which is exactly the asymmetry that was
  observed - and it took comparing against `virtio_snd`, the one other driver here that sends a
  payload OUT, to see that the difference was not the chain.
  THE CHECK WAS NOT RELAXED. `Queue::submit_bounded` lets a caller state the bound its device's
  accounting makes correct, and `submit_checked` keeps the automatic one for everybody else; a device
  claiming more than the caller's bound is still refused. The comment on it says that a caller passing
  something larger than its chain has removed the check, which is why the one use of it names the
  accounting it describes.

  **WHERE IT STANDS: UP ON x86_64 WITH ITS ORACLE PASSING.**
  `driver.virtio-scsi: online (00:0e.0, target 0, 8192 x 512 bytes)` - the four-megabyte medium the
  harness attaches, out of READ CAPACITY - with a bind window of 52 ticks. 406 tests pass, and all
  thirty-three staged drivers and services are covered. The oracle writes a pattern to LBA 0x0102 -
  not a single-digit block, so a command whose address bytes went out in the wrong order names a
  DIFFERENT block rather than the same one - reads it back, compares, and requires a range past the
  end to be refused with the typed status rather than clamped or truncated into the ten-byte form.
  Landing it also classified the TRANSITIONAL virtio-scsi PCI id: QEMU presents `virtio-scsi-pci` as
  0x1004 rather than the modern 0x1048, and the kernel's own test caught the scan meeting a virtio
  device it could not name.

  **AND THE THREE THAT WERE OPEN ARE DONE (2026-09-18). WHAT IS LEFT IS THE TWO EMULATED TARGETS.**

  MORE THAN ONE TARGET AND LUN. `REPORT LUNS` is in the shared SCSI core with seven host tests and
  five mutations, because the parse is where a multi-unit driver is silently wrong: the list length
  counts BYTES and not units - read as a count, a target with four units reports four bytes, which
  does not hold one whole entry, so the driver serves NONE of them and nothing anywhere contradicts
  the number. The top two bits of an addressing field choose how the rest is read, so a flat unit
  0x0101 read as peripheral is unit 1 - which exists on most targets and answers, serving the wrong
  medium under the right name. And the length is the DEVICE'S CLAIM, not the answer's: a target says
  "ask again with more" by claiming a longer list, and a driver indexing by the claim reads past its
  own DMA page and publishes whatever the last command left there as a medium.

  ONE BLOCK PROVIDER PER UNIT, EACH UNDER ITS UNIT'S OWN NAME. A kind says what a provider IS and a
  name says WHICH one: two block providers whose every other catalogue field is identical would hand
  a consumer asking for `block` whichever came first, which for a disk is a consumer writing to the
  wrong one with nothing anywhere to notice. The guest reports
  `driver.virtio-scsi: online (00:0e.0, 2 units: t0l0 8192 x 512 bytes, t0l1 4096 x 512 bytes)`, and
  the oracle asks the SECOND unit for its capacity and requires its own medium's size.

  AND `most` IS NOT A NUMBER TO PICK. The registry entry declares two block providers with four
  consumers each; a driver serves at most eight provider connections, and the manifest check refuses
  more. Four units at the four consumers a disk needs is sixteen, and the first attempt at this
  learned it from a boot that never reached its shell: the manager REFUSES the offer past `most` and
  the refusal takes the driver down mid-bring-up. Each unit keeps the four consumers every other disk
  declares, because a medium is probed by each StorageService instance that has to classify it.

  THE EVENT QUEUE, WITH AN INTERRUPT BEHIND IT. A unit arriving or leaving is announced there and
  nowhere else, so DeviceManager grants this driver an MSI-X vector - without one the only way to
  notice is a thread polling for the guest's whole life over a disk that may never be plugged. The
  decode is in the core with three tests and two mutations: the MISSED flag is OR'd INTO the event
  word rather than being a value of it, so a driver comparing the whole word stops recognising events
  exactly when the device says it has already dropped some - which reads as a quiet driver on a busy
  bus. And one transport-reset number covers a unit arriving, leaving and resetting itself; acting on
  the number alone re-enumerates a unit that is gone.

  PASSTHROUGH IS KEPT FROM ORDINARY CLIENTS BY THERE BEING NO OPCODE FOR IT. The block contract
  carries read, write, flush and capacity; a request outside that set is ANSWERED rather than turned
  into a command descriptor block of the caller's choosing, and the oracle sends one and requires the
  refusal plus a still-working medium after it. A driver that forwarded an unknown opcode would let
  any consumer of a disk send FORMAT UNIT.

  TWO DEFECTS THE SLICE FOUND, BOTH OUTSIDE THE DRIVER. The enumeration had no shared deadline, so a
  device that completes nothing cost a full poll budget PER COMMAND - nine targets at sixteen ready
  retries is 144 of them, spent before the driver answers its bind, which reads as a driver that
  never started. It is bounded now, and a queue that completed nothing ends the walk rather than
  being asked again. And the harness attached `virtio-scsi-pci` without the virtio options every
  other virtio device here carries: without `iommu_platform=on` the device masters the bus with
  guest-physical addresses, which the profile's virtio-iommu reads as addresses to translate, so the
  device completed nothing at all. The suite profile runs without an IOMMU and never saw it.

  **AND MORE THAN ONE TARGET IS IN (2026-09-19), AND WHAT WAS IN THE WAY WAS NOT THIS DRIVER.**

  THE WALK ALWAYS ASKED EVERY TARGET. `find_units` has walked `0..=max_target` since it was written;
  what it lacked was somewhere to put what it found. `MAX_UNITS` was TWO, and two is one target's
  worth on the machine this tree runs - so the table was full before the walk reached target 1, and
  "takes the units behind the first target that answers" was a description of the BOUND rather than
  of the code.

  AND THE BOUND WAS THE SERVING SET. Four units at the four consumers a disk needs is sixteen
  connections against a `MAX_PROVIDER_CLIENTS` of eight, and the manifest check refuses that before a
  boot can find out. EIGHT WAS CHOSEN WHEN A DRIVER PUBLISHED ONE PROVIDER: four consumers for a disk
  with room for a second. A SCSI host controller publishes one per LOGICAL UNIT and units live behind
  several targets, so the number had acquired a consumer it was never sized for. It is SIXTEEN now,
  in both of the places it is written - the driver library's and the manifest tool's, which a check
  already holds in step - and what it costs is the set itself: four arrays of that length, thirteen
  bytes an entry, about a hundred bytes in each driver that serves consumers. The SHAPE is unchanged:
  the set is still fixed, and a `CONNECT` past it is still refused by closing the endpoint.

  THE FIXTURE IS WHAT MAKES THE WALK OBSERVABLE, and two units behind one target could not: a driver
  that asks every target and one that stops at the first both find everything there is. So the
  harness attaches a third medium behind TARGET 1, of a third size - one mebibyte against four and
  two - because the capacity is how the oracle tells which medium it was handed. The guest reports

      driver.virtio-scsi: online (00:0e.0, 3 units: t0l0 8192 x 512 bytes, t0l1 4096 x 512 bytes, t1l0 2048 x 512 bytes)

  and the oracle asks the third unit for its capacity AND writes a pattern to it and reads it back,
  because a capacity can be answered by a driver that addressed the wrong unit and got lucky while a
  block that comes back is the medium itself. The two patterns differ, or reading one target's block
  out of the other would pass. MUTATION: stopping the walk at target 0 fails it with two units
  against three.

  AND THAT REPORT LINE WAS BEING CUT, which is how the third unit was noticed at all. `print_line` -
  the shared helper EVERY driver's online report goes through - copied into a 96-byte buffer while
  drivers assemble their lines in buffers of 160 and 192, so the guest said `... t1l0` and stopped:
  an operator told about two and a half disks. The truncation is deliberate and stays, because a
  driver that panicked writing a report is a device that never came up; what changed is that the
  bound is now above every report this tree writes rather than below one of them.

  AND THE TWO EMULATED TARGETS ARE IN (2026-09-19), WHICH WAS THE LAST THING OPEN. The driver suites
  pass on aarch64 (100 tests, 1539 s) and riscv64 (102 tests, 2097 s) under `drivers,pci,slow`, and both report
  `driver.virtio-scsi: online` with more than one target behind it. NOTHING IN THIS ITEM IS OPEN NOW.
  What it does NOT contain is written above rather than dropped: command passthrough, hotplug beyond
  bring-up enumeration, the event queue beyond bounding it, and sixteen-byte commands - each with the
  reason it was excluded, recorded before any code was written.

## 2. Generic and universal controllers

Standardized silicon interfaces: one driver per specification, every vendor's part behind it. Ordered
by what a real machine cannot be used without - storage and console first, because a machine that
cannot read its disk or print a line cannot be debugged - with server and legacy compatibility paths
at the end.

- [x] **NVMe** generic PCIe storage controller: controller reset/enable, admin plus I/O
  queues, identify controller/namespaces, PRP transfers, flush, read/write, status and
  timeout recovery behind the existing block contract used by `virtio-blk` and USB
  storage. Bound queue depth and transfer size, validate every completion ID/phase,
  support multiple namespaces/controllers and cleanly fail unsupported metadata,
  protection, zoned or vendor commands rather than partially interpreting them.

  **ITEM PLAN (2026-09-16), written before any code because the rule below says so.**

  CLASSIFICATION: `driver`, and it carries ONE `kernel/bus infra` piece it does not merely consume.
  The binder section above says what that piece is in as many words: every remaining PCI function
  already gets a `DeviceEntry` under `TRANSPORT_PLAIN_PCI` carrying the class triple and the address,
  and those entries carry `bar_phys: 0, bar_len: 0, msix_cap: 0`, so a rule can MATCH an NVMe
  controller today and nothing can hand the bound process a register window. "That, and not the
  entry, is what the first plain-PCI item owns", and this is that item.

  WHAT IT REQUIRES THAT ALREADY EXISTS. P02M0098's claim, generation and revocation; P02M0161's
  start and report; P02M0162's transactional bind and rollback, now complete and no longer a wait;
  P02M0172's registry DMA policy, now complete, which this driver declares
  `trusted-untranslated` under (CORRECTED 2026-09-16: this plan said `iommu-required` "because it
  masters the bus", which was written before the manifest was read. `iommu-required` binds only
  where an enforcing controller translates the device's addresses and REFUSES on a `no-iommu`
  machine, so it would mean this controller never binds on the profiles that would use it. Eight
  entries here declare `trusted-untranslated`, including `virtio_blk`, which is the disk this system
  boots from; one declares `iommu-required`, deliberately, as the refusal demonstration. The cost is
  the one the threat model already records: with no controller in the path, a DMA-mastering driver is
  a TCB member for memory safety); P02M0153's DMA, IOVA, admission, completion and quarantine contract;
  P02M0163's discovery breadth, which is what puts the function in the table at all; P02M0164's
  catalogue, per-consumer `open`, connection accounting and withdrawal, which it publishes a `block`
  provider through; P02M0165's STOP/STOPPED and bounded drain; P02M0166's operator policy. The
  manifest already validates a `plain-pci` rule with a class triple, and `xhci` is the proof: its
  entry matches `pci-class = 0x0c, pci-subclass = 0x03, pci-interface = 0x30` and it is a plain-PCI
  driver that works. The block REQUEST contract is `drivers::blk` and it is host-tested: the count
  and range refusals, the three-part transferred-object contract, and the ten-byte address refusal.

  WHAT IT OWNS THAT DOES NOT YET EXIST, and both halves are real work:
    - THE PLAIN-PCI RESOURCE PROFILE. A matched plain-PCI endpoint gets its BAR 0 window and its
      MSI-X vector the way `resolve_xhci` gets them, without a second copy of that function per
      family: AHCI, SDHCI, HDA, EHCI, OHCI/UHCI and TPM are all waiting behind the same missing
      mechanism, so the shape it lands in decides whether six later items inherit it or rewrite it.
      IT DOES NOT RESOURCE EVERY FUNCTION ON THE BUS. "A row with no resource profile is the point,
      not a gap in one" is a deliberate decision recorded above, and resourcing every function
      wholesale would reverse it silently. A function is resourced when a rule claims it.
    - THE CANONICAL BLOCK WIRE. `OP_READ`, `OP_WRITE`, `OP_CAPACITY`, `OP_FLUSH` and the three status
      codes are written out by hand in THREE places today - `virtio_blk.rs`, `usb_storage.rs` and
      `services/storage/src/service.rs` - and this item would make a fourth. The rule in the
      `virtio-scsi` item is that the wire is owned by the first implemented slice that publishes a
      block provider; it has no owner because nobody extracted it, and a fourth copy is how it stays
      that way. The extraction moves all three existing copies in the same change, or it has produced
      a shared copy plus three private ones, which is worse than what is there now.

  WHAT IT EXCLUDES, so a later reader does not read the exclusion as an oversight: namespace
  management (create, delete, attach), formatting, sanitize and firmware download; zoned namespaces;
  end-to-end protection information and any metadata-carrying LBA format; SR-IOV, multipath, ANA and
  reservations; controller memory buffer and host memory buffer; power-state transitions beyond the
  reset the bring-up performs; vendor-specific command sets. Each is REFUSED with a typed status
  rather than partially interpreted, which is what the item text above asks for. Interrupt-driven
  completion is also excluded from the first slice: bring-up and I/O are polled, matching the
  virtio-blk and xHCI drivers this tree already runs, and MSI-X is resolved and handed over so the
  later change is a driver change rather than a kernel one.

  TARGET DEVICE MODEL: QEMU `-device nvme`, which is in the tree's own QEMU already and needs a drive
  behind it. Two controllers and two namespaces are both expressible in that model, which is what
  "test at least two instances where the standard permits them" needs.

  OBSERVABLE-EFFECT ORACLE, stated to this tree's component-oracle convention, which rejects "the
  driver reached Online": a volume carried by the NVMe controller is READ THROUGH StorageService and
  its bytes compared against what was written into the disk image, and the same volume answers a
  capacity query with the image's real size. The negative controls are the ones the block contract
  already types: a request past the last sector and a write whose transferred handle is not a
  readable memory object of sufficient size are REFUSED with `STATUS_INVALID` and the controller is
  never asked. A second controller proves the binding is per function rather than per family.

  MEASURABLE LIMITS, each a number the driver enforces rather than a hope: queue depth, one admin
  and one I/O queue pair; entries per queue bounded and below the controller's reported `CAP.MQES`;
  one PRP list page, which bounds a single transfer and is the number `MDTS` is checked against; a
  bounded completion wait with a typed timeout rather than a spin; every completion validated on its
  phase bit AND its command id before its result is believed, with an unexpected id treated as a
  device fault rather than indexed with.

  DEFINITION OF DONE FOR ITS CLASS. Host tests over the pure decisions with deterministic malformed
  mutations before any MMIO: completion validation, the PRP split, the identify parse and the refusal
  set. A fake-controller seam covering completion ordering, timeout, reset and resource cleanup. The
  QEMU model proving the governed process, the DeviceManager binding and the destination-service
  contract. The bind window measured and recorded on each target it claims acceptance on, which
  P02M0162 leaves to its consumers. Registered in the verification model in the same change, and the
  QEMU device wired into the harness in the same change.

  WHAT THIS PLAN DOES NOT CLAIM: acceptance on aarch64 and riscv64 is a separate measurement on each,
  and x86_64 landing first does not carry them.

  **WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-16): the two things it OWNS are landed and tested; the controller
  itself is not written.** Stated this way round because the owned halves are the ones that would
  otherwise be rediscovered by whoever writes the next plain-PCI driver.

  - THE PLAIN-PCI RESOURCE PROFILE IS IN. `resolve_xhci` was one function per family, with a shim in
    each of the three architectures and a loop of its own in `device.rs` - six edits to add a second
    family, which is why there was only ever one. It is now `RESOURCED`, a table of class triples,
    resolved by one `resolve_endpoint` through one `scan_resourced`, and the resolved row carries
    WHICH family it is rather than having it implied by the name of its type. AHCI, SDHCI, HDA,
    EHCI, OHCI/UHCI and TPM each become one row in that table plus their driver. The deliberate line
    is kept and written into the code: it resources the families the table names, NOT every function
    on the bus, so a function this kernel resolves nothing for still gets an identity row with no
    resources. A missing BAR 0 is a refusal rather than a zero, so no row claims a profile and hands
    out a window of nothing.
    AND THE CLASS TRIPLES HAVE ONE DECLARATION. The NVMe triple was three bare literals inside the
    IOMMU bypass transition, and the resolver table was about to spell it a second time; both read
    `abi::PCI_CLASS_MASS_STORAGE` and its two companions now. `abi::DEVICE_TYPE_NVME` is 0x102,
    because 0x101 was already the unresolved-function row.
  - THE CANONICAL BLOCK WIRE IS IN, with all three existing copies moved onto it in the same change
    rather than a fourth added beside them: `driver_protocol::block`, fourteen host tests over the
    literal bytes. It found a live divergence on the way, which is the argument for extracting
    before the fourth copy rather than after: the two servers answer a REFUSED request differently,
    `virtio-blk` with `STATUS_INVALID` and the USB path with `STATUS_ERR`, so on that server the
    caller cannot tell a request it got wrong from a device that failed one it got right. Recorded
    and deliberately not repaired in that change, because a reply code on a path the change does not
    test is not a free edit. It belongs to the xHCI maintenance item's own gate.
    It also turned one client's private rule into the protocol's: StorageService accepted a
    twelve-byte capacity reply so a server predating the per-request bound still reports a size, and
    that was `len >= 12` written inside the service. `CAPACITY_SIZE_LEN` and `decode_capacity_bytes`
    name it and two tests hold it.
  - THE DECISION CORE IS IN AND HOST-TESTED, which is what the Definition of Done asks for before
    any MMIO. `drivers::nvme` is the register layouts, the completion rules, the PRP arithmetic and
    the namespace refusals as pure functions, with eighteen tests. Each watches one way an NVMe
    driver is wrong rather than asserting that a round trip agrees with itself: a queue one entry
    too long because `CAP.MQES` is zero-based; a doorbell rung at an assumed stride of eight, which
    works on every controller this tree has met and rings the wrong queue on anything else; a
    completion believed because its status CODE was zero while its status TYPE was not; a transfer
    one page short because `len / page` is wrong for any buffer not page aligned; and a controller
    that transfers nothing forever because `MDTS = 0` means "no limit" and was read as a limit of
    zero - which is QEMU's own model.
  - **THE CONTROLLER IS UP ON x86_64 AND SERVES THE BLOCK WIRE (2026-09-16).** `driver.nvme` resets
    and enables the controller, builds an admin pair and one I/O pair, identifies the controller and
    namespace 1, publishes a `block` provider and stops cleanly. QEMU's `-device nvme` is attached to
    the x86_64 profile behind a blank sixteen-megabyte per-run medium, and the guest log carries
    `driver.nvme: online (00:09.0, 32768 x 512 bytes)` - which is that medium, read back through
    IDENTIFY rather than assumed. The whole suite is 400 passed with the controller on the bus, so
    adding a PCI function behind the media disks renumbered nothing the boot chain depends on.
    It declares `trusted-untranslated`, matches on the class triple rather than a vendor id, and
    offers one `block` provider with four consumers.
  - WHAT THE BRING-UP COST, because it is the part worth carrying forward. The first run failed and
    the restart succeeded, and DeviceManager reported "the driver says the device is not responding" -
    its category, not a diagnosis, and the driver had answered `None` from six different places that
    all arrive as that one sentence. So the first repair was to the driver's READABILITY: `bring_up`
    now returns a typed reason and prints it, and every command names its opcode and carries the
    controller's own status type and code. The next run said "the I/O queue pair was not created",
    then "an admin command did not complete", succeeding on the third attempt, which is a different
    problem from the one the manager's wording suggested.
    A HYPOTHESIS WAS CHECKED AND WAS WRONG, and it is written down because it was nearly written down
    as the cause: the IOMMU bypass transition walks every PCI function, finds the NVMe class triple
    and clears `CC.EN`, which would disable a controller underneath its driver. The logs carry no
    bypass line at all on this profile, so that is not what happened.
    WHAT WAS GENUINELY WRONG, INDEPENDENT OF THE FLAKE: nothing ordered the sixty-four bytes of a
    submission entry against the doorbell write that announces it. `write_volatile` stops the COMPILER
    reordering them and says nothing about the machine; x86 store ordering usually hides that, which
    is exactly why it was worth fixing, because this tree also builds for aarch64 and riscv64 where a
    controller reading a half-written entry is not a hypothetical. The completion side had the mirror
    of it. Three consecutive runs since carry zero restarts.
  - **THE INTERMITTENT BRING-UP IS FIXED, AND WHAT IT WAS IS WORTH THE THREE ROUNDS IT TOOK.** The
    driver was failing at whichever command happened to be in flight - IDENTIFY, CREATE I/O
    COMPLETION QUEUE, the stop-time FLUSH - and DeviceManager's restart hid it. Three of ten runs
    carried a failure.
    IT WAS FOUND BY MEASUREMENT AND NOT BY READING THE CODE, in three rounds, each one teaching the
    driver to say something it could not say before. First a typed reason per give-up point, because
    `None` from six places arrived at the manager as one sentence. Then the opcode and the
    controller's own status, which turned "not responding" into "a completion for a command that is
    not outstanding". Then the id it saw and the raw dwords, which is the round that settled it:

        driver.nvme: sqe slot 0 dw0 00010006 entries 8
        driver.nvme: command 01 got id 0 waiting for 5, sq 0 head 0, dw3 00010000

    The submission entry was read back out of queue memory before the doorbell and carried the id
    that was sent, so the write landed where the controller reads. The completion did not: `sq 0
    head 0` says the controller had consumed NOTHING from that queue while five commands were
    outstanding, so it was not answering anything. Every field of the entry was zero except the
    phase bit.
    A SIXTEEN-BYTE WRITE FROM A DEVICE IS NOT ATOMIC TO ITS READER. The phase bit became visible
    before the id, the status and the queue head did, and a driver that reads the whole entry and
    then decides believes a completion that has not finished arriving. A barrier BEFORE the read
    orders nothing about a write still in flight while it runs.
    THE FIX IS TWO RULES. The phase is read on its own, and only once it says the entry is this
    pass's is a barrier taken and the entry read again. And command id ZERO IS NEVER ISSUED - a rule
    the driver keeps rather than a fact that happens to hold - so an entry still reading as id zero
    is by construction nobody's, which makes it a completion that has not arrived rather than one
    belonging to somebody else. Those want opposite responses: wait, or give up.
    EIGHT CONSECUTIVE RUNS CARRY NO RESTART, no unexpected completion, no timeout and no failed
    flush, against three failures in the ten runs before it.
    TWO EARLIER CANDIDATES ARE KEPT IN THE RECORD because each disproved something. Draining an
    unexpected completion and stepping over it was REVERTED: it made the symptom worse, which
    disproved "a stale entry is in the way of the right one". Zeroing each queue's doorbells after
    creation was KEPT - a queue that has just been created is empty, so the two stores cannot be
    wrong - but it is not what fixed this: six runs with it carried three failures.
  - **AND THE ORACLE PASSES, WHICH IS WHAT THE COMPONENT-ORACLE CONVENTION ASKS FOR** rather than
    "the driver reached Online", a claim this tree rejects on principle. The harness drives the
    driver exactly as DeviceManager does - one `BIND` stating its own resource count and one DEVICE
    resource, because this driver polls and never asks for an interrupt - takes the `block` provider
    it publishes, and then moves bytes: a capacity query answered from IDENTIFY NAMESPACE rather than
    from anything the test told it, a pattern written to LBA 7 and read back and COMPARED, and a
    range past the last block REFUSED with the typed status rather than clamped, granting no buffer.
    The pattern is neither zeroes nor a constant on purpose: the medium starts blank, so a read that
    returned untouched medium would match a zero buffer and one that returned a constant fill would
    match whatever offset it actually read from. LBA 7 rather than 0 for the same reason.
    IT FAILED BEFORE THE TORN-READ FIX AND PASSES AFTER IT, which is the strongest evidence that fix
    is what it claims to be: the harness's own instance was hitting the same half-arrived completion,
    and the boot it produced brought up five of twenty-four services. 402 tests pass with it in, and
    `component-oracles` reports all twenty-nine staged drivers and services covered - the exception
    line this driver carried while the oracle was out has been removed rather than left standing.
  - **TWO CONTROLLERS, WHICH IS A GATE REQUIREMENT AND NOT A FLOURISH.** This file asks for at least
    two instances wherever the standard permits them, and NVMe permits them. The harness attaches a
    second function behind an eight-megabyte medium, and the boot carries both:

        driver.nvme: online (00:09.0, 32768 x 512 bytes)
        driver.nvme: online (00:0a.0, 16384 x 512 bytes)

    EACH READ ITS OWN SIZE OUT OF ITS OWN CONTROLLER, which is the observation one instance cannot
    make: with a single controller, "the rule matched the class triple" and "the rule matched THIS
    function" are the same sentence, and a driver that bound per family rather than per function
    would look identical. Two different sizes from two functions is the difference. The device-table
    test asserts BOTH are resourced rather than stopping at the first one the scan met, which is the
    half of the plain-PCI resource profile a single row could not prove either.
  - **AND THE SAME DEFECT WAS FOUND IN A DRIVER THAT ALREADY SHIPS**, which is the part of this item
    worth carrying past it. Three programs in this tree consume a ring a device writes into and
    validate each entry by one field: `virtio.rs` by the used index, `xhci.rs` by the TRB cycle bit,
    and now `nvme_driver.rs` by the completion phase bit.
    `virtio.rs` HAS ALWAYS HAD THE RIGHT SHAPE - it reads `used.idx`, checks it advanced, FENCES, and
    only then reads the element. `xhci.rs` did not: `take_event` read the cycle bit, checked it, and
    read `param` and `status` with nothing in between. The cycle bit says the TRB is this pass's; it
    does not say the rest of it has arrived. Acted on torn, that is somebody else's slot id, somebody
    else's completion code, or a port number that is not the port that changed.
    NOTHING CLAIMS TO HAVE SEEN IT FAIL ON xHCI, and the fix is one fence, made because the shape is
    wrong rather than because it was caught. It was found by writing a third consumer of the same
    kind of ring and having that one fail loudly enough to be traced, which is an argument for
    writing the next driver rather than against it.
  - AND BOTH OF THOSE ARE IN (2026-09-19), WHICH CLOSES THIS ITEM. The driver suites pass on
    aarch64 (100 tests, 1539 s) and riscv64 (102 tests, 2097 s) under `drivers,pci,slow`, each bringing up two NVMe controllers. The bind
    window IS measured - 63 and 65 ticks for the two of them, in the table in this file's head, which
    reports every driver from one boot because that is how DeviceManager prints it. And
    `driver-protocol-note` is GREEN: the three volumes were rebuilt at the end of the job as that
    note said they would be, and the gate now reads thirteen notes in each volume and 42 driver
    artifacts across three architectures, all declaring protocol version 2.
- [x] **AHCI/SATA** standard HBA: enumerate implemented ports, identify SATA devices,
  issue bounded DMA read/write/flush commands, handle NCQ only after the single-command
  path, detect hotplug and recover a failed port without resetting unrelated ports.
  Serve the existing block contract and reject unsupported ATAPI/port-multiplier paths
  explicitly. Keep controller quirks and non-AHCI vendor modes in Phase 5.

  **ITEM PLAN (2026-09-16), written before any code because the rule below says so.**

  CLASSIFICATION: `driver`, and it is THE SECOND CONSUMER of the plain-PCI resource profile the NVMe
  item owns rather than an owner of anything new. That is the point of taking it next: a
  generalisation with one consumer is a rename, and the way to find out whether `RESOURCED` is a
  table or a disguised special case is to add the second row.

  WHAT IT REQUIRES THAT ALREADY EXISTS. All of NVMe's prerequisites, discharged the same way: the
  claim, start and report, the transactional bind, the registry DMA policy, the DMA and IOVA
  contract, discovery breadth, the catalogue and per-consumer `open`, STOP/STOPPED, operator policy.
  It consumes `driver_protocol::block` rather than defining a wire, and `drivers::blk` for the
  request refusals - the count, the range, the transferred object's type, rights and size. It
  consumes the plain-PCI resource profile rather than extending it, with ONE exception below.

  WHAT IT OWNS THAT DOES NOT YET EXIST:
    - THE BAR INDEX IN THE RESOURCE PROFILE, and this is the exception that proves the table was the
      right shape. An xHCI controller and an NVMe controller both put their whole register file in
      BAR 0, so "resolve BAR 0" was indistinguishable from "resolve the register file". AHCI does
      not: its ABAR is BAR 5. So the row gains a field rather than the resolver gaining a branch, and
      SDHCI, HDA and the OHCI/UHCI pair - each of which names its own BAR - inherit that field rather
      than discovering the same thing again.
    - THE AHCI COMMAND LAYER: the command list, the received-FIS area, one command table per slot,
      and the ATA commands the block contract needs. None of it is shared with NVMe, which has its
      own command set, and none of it is SCSI, which is why this item creates no shared contract.

  WHAT IT EXCLUDES: NCQ until the single-command path is proved, which the item text already
  requires; ATAPI and port multipliers, refused explicitly rather than half-driven; staggered
  spin-up; port hotplug beyond detecting it, because this tree has no arrival or removal event for a
  PCI function and a port is smaller than a function; vendor modes and controller quirks, which the
  item text puts in Phase 5. Interrupt-driven completion is excluded from the first slice for the
  same reason as NVMe: bring-up and I/O poll, matching every other driver here.

  TARGET DEVICE MODEL: QEMU `-device ahci` with a drive behind it, on the x86_64 profile, attached
  after the media disks for the reason the NVMe helper already records.

  OBSERVABLE-EFFECT ORACLE, to the same convention and for the same reason: a pattern written to a
  sector and READ BACK through the published `block` provider, a capacity answered from IDENTIFY
  DEVICE rather than from anything the test said, and a range past the last sector REFUSED with the
  typed status rather than clamped. "The driver reached Online" is not accepted here.

  MEASURABLE LIMITS: one command slot in use at a time on the single-command path; the PRDT bounded
  to one entry per contiguous span with a stated maximum byte count; a bounded wait on `PxCI` with a
  typed timeout rather than a spin; every port's `PxSSTS` checked for a device before its slot is
  built, so an empty port is skipped rather than waited on.

  DEFINITION OF DONE FOR ITS CLASS: host tests over the pure decisions with malformed inputs before
  any MMIO - the port enumeration, the FIS layout, the PRDT split and the refusal set; the QEMU model
  proving the governed process, the DeviceManager binding and the block contract; the bind window
  measured and recorded per target; registered in the verification model in the same change.

  WHAT THIS PLAN DOES NOT CLAIM: acceptance on aarch64 and riscv64 is a separate measurement on each.

  **WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-16): the driver is up on x86_64 and its oracle passes.**
  `driver.ahci: online (00:0b.0, 16384 x 512 bytes)` - the eight-megabyte medium the harness attaches,
  read out of IDENTIFY DEVICE's 48-bit sector count rather than assumed. 403 tests pass with it in,
  and `component-oracles` reports all thirty staged drivers and services covered.
  THE ORACLE IS THE SAME SHAPE AS NVMe's, over a different command set: a capacity answered from the
  disk, a pattern written to LBA 11 and read back and COMPARED, and a range past the last sector
  refused with the typed status rather than clamped, granting no buffer.

  **THE TABLE WAS THE RIGHT SHAPE, AND ONE FIELD PROVED IT.** AHCI's register file is in BAR 5, not
  BAR 0, so `RESOURCED` gained a BAR index and `resolve_endpoint` gained nothing. A second family
  therefore cost one row plus one field rather than a resolver, three architecture shims and a loop,
  which is what it cost before this generalisation existed. SDHCI, HDA and the OHCI/UHCI pair each
  name their own BAR and inherit that field rather than finding it again.

  **AND THE MACHINE ANSWERED A QUESTION NOBODY ASKED IT.** The q35 chipset carries its own SATA
  controller at 00:1f.2 with a CD-ROM on port 2, so every boot binds this driver TWICE without the
  harness arranging it. The second one exercises the refusal path for free:

      driver.ahci: port 2 carries an ATAPI device, which this driver does not serve
      driver.ahci: bring-up gave up at 00:1f.2 - no implemented port has a SATA disk on an active link

  THAT FOUND A REAL DEFECT IN HOW THE FAILURE WAS REPORTED. It was answered as retryable, so
  DeviceManager restarted a driver that had already given its final answer and the boot log carried
  the same refusal twice. "Whether a second attempt could differ" is a different question from "what
  went wrong", and it is the one the manager acts on: a controller whose only device is ATAPI is
  `UnsupportedDevice` - it read the device and will not drive it, and nothing changes that - while a
  resource shortage, a port that did not settle and a command that did not come back are states a
  second attempt can find differently. The node is now failed once, cleanly, and its resources are
  released.

  **AND THAT WAS TRUE OF THE CODE AND FALSE OF THE RUNNING SYSTEM UNTIL 2026-09-18.** The driver's
  code was right and the boot still carried the refusal twice, because the manager never READ it:
  DeviceManager routed every event to an outstanding teardown while one existed, and a teardown's
  notes consume exactly two events - `Exited` and `ClaimSettled` - and discard the rest. A driver's
  terminal answer went on the floor. Worse, `drain_channel` judged the bind deadline BEFORE reading
  the channel, so on the pass where the deadline fell the timeout entered the queue AHEAD of an
  answer already sitting there unread. The incident printed the contradiction and nobody read it:
  `last opcode 5 4 tick(s) ago` - a terminal frame, four ticks before the timeout that said nothing
  had been said. Both are fixed where they are: a teardown takes its own two confirmations and
  nothing else, and a deadline is judged after the frames are read, because a deadline is about
  silence and there is no silence when the answer has arrived. The boot now reads
  `ahci reported a permanent failure - the driver read the device and will not drive it` followed by
  `ahci did not bind (driver-reported-failure)`, once, with no restart - and two bind windows come
  back to the boot.

  **AND NCQ IS IN (2026-09-19), WITH THE CONCURRENCY THAT IS THE ONLY REASON TO HAVE IT.**

  NCQ ALONE WOULD HAVE BEEN CEREMONY. A queued command that is the only command in flight costs the
  same as an unqueued one; this file refuses a mechanism nothing executes, so the tags had to have
  somewhere to come from. THE BLOCK CONTRACT IS ONE REQUEST AND ONE REPLY PER MESSAGE, so a single
  consumer never has two outstanding - a disk's concurrency can only come from its FOUR CONSUMERS
  asking together, which is what the registry has always declared and what nothing used.

  SO THE DRIVER COLLECTS A BATCH. It blocks for the first request as before, then asks every other
  consumer whether one is already waiting - `poll_ready` is a wait against the current instant, so a
  consumer with nothing costs one syscall - puts each on its own tag, and answers each as ITS command
  finishes rather than when the batch does. A controller without `CAP.SNCQ` collects nothing, because
  its second command would wait for the first anyway and collecting it early only delays the answer.

  FOUR TAGS, BECAUSE FOUR IS WHAT A DISK HAS CONSUMERS. A fifth is a tag no caller exists for. Each
  tag has its OWN command table and its OWN data span: two commands in flight into one buffer is two
  transfers into the same memory, and the second to finish wins.

  THREE THINGS THAT ARE DIFFERENT ABOUT A QUEUED COMMAND, each held by a fixture in
  `drivers::ahci`'s own tests because each is a way a first NCQ driver is wrong:
  - THE SECTOR COUNT MOVES INTO THE FEATURES FIELD AND THE TAG TAKES ITS PLACE. `READ DMA EXT` puts
    the block count in the sector-count field; `READ FPDMA QUEUED` puts it in features and
    features_exp, and the sector-count field carries the TAG SHIFTED LEFT BY THREE. A driver filling
    these the ordinary way asks for tag 0 of a zero-length transfer, which on most controllers means
    the maximum - 65,536 sectors of somebody else's disk into a buffer sized for eight.
  - A QUEUED COMMAND IS OUTSTANDING IN `PxSACT`, NOT IN `PxCI`. The controller clears `PxCI` when it
    has SENT the command, which for a queued one happens at once and says nothing about the data, so
    a driver watching it reports every queued read complete the moment it was issued and hands back
    a buffer the disk has not written. `PxSACT` is what the device clears, through a Set Device Bits
    FIS, when the transfer is done.
  - A FAILURE STOPS THE WHOLE QUEUE. `TFD.ERR` halts the port and abandons every other outstanding
    tag, so the error is read BEFORE the active bit and every tag in the batch is answered as failed
    - which is the truth - and the port is stopped, cleared and started again, or every later batch
    times out against a controller that is no longer accepting.

  THE ORACLE PUTS A READ FROM EACH OF TWO CONSUMERS ON THE WIRE BEFORE EITHER REPLY IS TAKEN, over
  two sectors written with patterns that differ, and requires each consumer to get ITS OWN. That is
  what catches the defect a tagged driver introduces: two commands sharing a data span, or two
  answers going to the wrong consumers. The guest reports
  `driver.ahci: online (00:0b.0, 16384 x 512 bytes, ncq 4 tags)` - QEMU's controller advertises
  `CAP.SNCQ`, so the existing write-then-read oracle already runs over `READ/WRITE FPDMA QUEUED`,
  which is what says the field mapping is right on a real controller and not only against fixtures.

  TWO MUTATIONS, AND THEIR SIGNATURES DIFFER. Placing every request on tag zero fails with
  `both consumers should be answered: Empty` - one consumer's entry was overwritten by the other's.
  Reading a queued completion out of `PxCI` HANGS the suite rather than failing an assertion, which
  is worth knowing before somebody calls it flaky: the driver and the controller stop agreeing about
  what is outstanding, and neither the batch nor the port recovers.

  AND PORT HOTPLUG WAS TRIED AND IS NOT AVAILABLE TO TRY (2026-09-19). This item's plan excludes it
  and gives a reason - this tree has no arrival or removal event for a PCI function and a PORT IS
  SMALLER THAN A FUNCTION - and that reason is now measured rather than assumed: the emulator cannot
  produce the event either. `device_del` on the SATA disk answers
  `Bus 'sata.0' does not support hotplugging`, on the live instance, with the disk plainly there in
  `info qtree`. A driver taught to notice a port's presence changing would be a mechanism nothing in
  this harness can execute and no test can drive, which is what this file refuses beside NCM and
  CDC-ACM. The device carries an `id` now so a later attempt starts one step further on.

  WHAT IT DOES DO IS RECOVER A FAILED PORT, which is the other half of the item's own sentence and
  came with the queue: a command that fails stops the port, and the driver stops it properly, clears
  its errors and starts it again - and touches no other port, which is the sentence's own condition.

  AND THAT IS IN (2026-09-19), WHICH CLOSES THIS ITEM. The driver suites pass on
  aarch64 (100 tests, 1539 s) and riscv64 (102 tests, 2097 s) under `drivers,pci,slow`, each reporting `driver.ahci: online (..., ncq 4
  tags)`. The bind window IS measured - 68 ticks, in the table in this file's head. What this driver
  does NOT do is above and stays there: port hotplug beyond detecting it, which the paragraph before
  this one shows the harness cannot present, and the ATAPI and port-multiplier paths it refuses by
  name.
- [ ] **UEFI GOP / simple-framebuffer handoff**: formalize the already implemented boot
  framebuffer discovery as one architecture-neutral immutable descriptor and a generic
  early-display provider with checked geometry/pixel masks/cache policy. Define the
  ownership handoff to DisplayService/real GPU drivers, retain console output when no
  GPU binds and test x86_64/aarch64/riscv64 UEFI plus direct-boot simple-framebuffer
  paths. This is consolidation of existing paths, not a second framebuffer stack.

  ITS WRITTEN PLAN (2026-09-15), which the rule above requires before any code. Nothing here is
  started. What it is for is that the next implementer does not rediscover the four paths that
  already exist, and that "consolidation" names a specific set of changes rather than a mood.

    REQUIRES   `bootproto::BootInfo` and the loader's `gop.rs`; the kernel's early console and
                 `framebuffer_geometry`; `SYS_FRAMEBUFFER_MAP` and the `DisplayController`
                 privilege; DisplayService's scanout adoption and the provider catalogue it already
                 finds a GPU through; `pix::Target::packed`, which is where a mask becomes pixels
    OWNS       ONE descriptor for a boot surface, its cache policy as a stated field, and the
                 early display as a PROVIDER rather than as a syscall - and the retirement of
                 whichever of the two descriptors does not survive
    GATE       a boot on each of the three targets, UEFI and direct, asserting that the first
                 frame DisplayService presents reaches the same pixels the kernel's own console
                 wrote - which is the one claim a handoff has to make and the one nothing checks
                 today. The existing `qemu-arch-profiles` gate already boots the six combinations;
                 this adds the assertion to them rather than a seventh boot.
    SIZE       THREE PARTS, AND THEY ARE NOT THE SAME SIZE. Two are a day between them and are done;
                 the third is two new kernel-level mechanisms before a line of it can be written, and
                 the plan below says which. An item whose parts look alike in a list and differ by an
                 order of magnitude in the work is the reason this line is here.

  CLASSIFICATION: `driver` and NOT `DMA-capable` - a boot surface is firmware-mapped memory and no
  device masters anything through it - so the DMA prerequisite row does not apply and the
  every-driver row does.

  THE FOUR PATHS THAT ALREADY EXIST, measured in this tree rather than assumed:
  1. x86_64 and riscv64 UEFI: the loader's `gop.rs` locates a GOP, DERIVES the element size from the
     channel masks rather than assuming thirty-two bits, and refuses a mask that is split, that
     overlaps another channel, or whose reserved channel does either. That checking is DONE and is
     not what this item has left.
  2. aarch64 and riscv64 direct boot (`-kernel`, no loader): the kernel programs QEMU's `ramfb` over
     fw-cfg itself and brings its console up on it. XRGB8888, hard-coded, because ramfb has one
     format.
  3. The kernel's early console draws the boot log into whichever of those it got.
  4. DisplayService takes the surface through `SYS_FRAMEBUFFER_MAP`, gated by a `DisplayController`
     privilege and claimed once - `try_claim` is what stops two privileged callers both being handed
     the display - and the kernel console stops drawing at that moment.

  THE THREE THINGS THAT ARE ACTUALLY LEFT, each with the defect it fixes:
  1. THERE ARE TWO DESCRIPTORS AND THEY DISAGREE ABOUT UNITS. `bootproto::Framebuffer` carries
     `addr` and `bpp` in BITS; `abi::Framebuffer` carries `bytes_per_pixel` and no address. The
     bridge is `fb.bpp / 8`, written twice in `kernel/main.rs` - once for the early console and once
     for what userspace is handed - and neither division is checked.
     IT IS A HARDENING AND NOT A LIVE DEFECT, and saying which matters. Every producer today supplies
     a multiple of eight: the UEFI path derives the element size from the masks and ROUNDS UP to whole
     bytes, and the two ramfb paths hard-code thirty-two. So nothing truncates. What is wrong is
     where the check is not: `BootInfo` is a wire between two SEPARATELY BUILT artifacts, the kernel
     is the reader, and the reader divides a number it did not produce without asking whether the
     division is exact. A descriptor in one unit removes the question; until then the division
     refuses rather than truncating, which for a boot surface means serial-only - the same
     degradation as a machine with no framebuffer at all.
     AND THE MASK CHECKING IS DONE AND IS NOT THIS ITEM'S. The loader already refuses a split mask,
     an overlapping one, and a reserved channel that is either; whoever takes this item should not
     redo it.
  2. THE EARLY DISPLAY IS A SYSCALL AND EVERY OTHER DEVICE IS A PROVIDER. DisplayService has two
     ways to get a scanout - a `DisplayController` privilege and a syscall for the boot surface, a
     catalogue subscription for a GPU - and only the second can be replaced when its publisher goes
     away. Publishing the boot surface as an ordinary `ProviderKind::Display` would make the handoff
     a withdrawal and an adoption, which is the service's own path for "the GPU driver rebound".
     AND IT IS NOT A REFACTOR OF WHAT EXISTS, which this plan said it was and which was wrong
     (corrected 2026-09-15, after looking). It needs TWO mechanisms this tree does not have, both
     checked rather than assumed:
     - SOMETHING TO HAND OVER. `SYS_FRAMEBUFFER_MAP` maps into the CALLER's address space and answers
       with a virtual address; `MemoryObject::create_in` and `DmaBuffer::create_in` both ALLOCATE
       fresh physical pages. Nothing in this kernel wraps an EXISTING physical range in a
       transferable object, and `MemoryObject`'s `Drop` retires its frames to the allocator - which
       for a firmware aperture would hand MMIO out as ordinary RAM. That object is a capability
       decision: a handle that maps arbitrary physical memory is what a capability system must not
       hand out, so it would be minted by the kernel for one range it already owns and nothing else.
     - SOMETHING TO PUBLISH IT. Every publication in the catalogue is keyed to a `BindingId` -
       `publish_all(binding, entry, offers)` - which is a device CLAIM: a bus, a device, a function
       and a generation. A boot framebuffer is not a device and has no claim, so there is no path by
       which a program with no device publishes a provider at all. Either that path exists, or the
       boot surface becomes a synthetic device node; both are new mechanisms.
     Whoever takes this owns both first. The syscall would then be what the PUBLISHER uses rather
     than what the consumer uses, which is the shape - but the shape is the end of the work and not
     the start of it.
  3. THE CACHE POLICY IS NOT STATED ANYWHERE AND IS THEREFORE NOT CHECKABLE. `sys_framebuffer_map`
     maps `PRESENT | WRITABLE | NO_EXECUTE` with no memory type, which is write-back on x86_64 and
     Normal write-back on aarch64 - correct for every machine this system runs on, because in QEMU
     the surface is ordinary RAM, and WRONG for a discrete card's linear aperture, which wants
     write-combining. The descriptor carries the policy the surface was mapped with; the machines in
     the harness assert `write-back`; and the day a target needs write-combining, the field is where
     that is said rather than a third mapping path.

  ITS MEASURABLE LIMITS: a maximum extent and pitch refused rather than clamped (the same ceiling
  the image model already states), a pitch at least the minimum row for the element size, a surface
  whose length the address range actually holds, and an element size that divides into bytes. Every
  one of them is a firmware-supplied number, which is the reason they are limits at all.

  EXCLUDED, so the item cannot grow: no mode SETTING - a boot surface is the mode firmware left, and
  changing it is a real driver's business; no second renderer, no multiple outputs, no EDID (that is
  its own item), and no change to what the kernel's early console draws or how.

  WHAT HAS LANDED AGAINST THAT PLAN (2026-09-15): thing 1, whole.
  - THE WIRE STATES ONE UNIT AND IT IS BYTES. `bootproto::Framebuffer::bpp` is `bytes_per_pixel`, so
    the loader's descriptor and the `abi::Framebuffer` the kernel hands userspace now use the same
    word for the same number. The bits-to-bytes conversion happens ONCE, in the loader, where the
    masks it is derived from are read; the kernel copies rather than divides. The two ramfb paths
    state 4 where they stated 32.
  - AND THE KERNEL VALIDATES WHAT IT READS, which is the half that was missing entirely. `describable`
    asks what a geometry has to satisfy for a row to mean anything: a non-zero extent, an element size
    a pixel can be, and A PITCH THAT HOLDS A WHOLE ROW OF IT. That last one was never checked at all -
    the pitch was multiplied into a mapping and taken as given - and a pitch one byte short of a row
    is not a smaller picture, it is every row after the first read at the wrong offset. Refused rather
    than repaired, which for a boot surface means serial-only: the same answer a machine with no video
    mode gets.
  - THE FIXTURE IS ABOUT THE GEOMETRY AND NOT THE ARITHMETIC: a pitch one byte short, a zero element,
    an element past any format this system has, an empty extent - and the live machine's own surface,
    so a change that made this refuse a real boot surface fails there rather than on a blank screen.
  - AND THE `host-tests` GATE CAUGHT WHAT THE BUILD DID NOT. Adding `set-scale` to `display-admin`
    left two test doubles in `display-proto` without it; nothing but that gate compiles them, so a
    full build and a full guest suite both passed over it.
  - AND THE CACHE POLICY IS A STATED FIELD NOW, which is thing 3. `abi::Framebuffer` carries
    `memory_type`, the kernel sets it to `FRAMEBUFFER_WRITE_BACK` where it describes the surface -
    which is what `PRESENT | WRITABLE | NO_EXECUTE` with no memory-type bits IS on x86_64 and under
    this kernel's aarch64 MAIR - and the fixture asserts it. DisplayService states the same for a
    DEVICE scanout, which is a DMA buffer in ordinary RAM. IT IS NOT A FIELD NOBODY READS: this
    service COMPOSITES into the scanout, which reads it back, and that is the one access pattern a
    write-combining aperture makes orders of magnitude slower than it looks - a consumer that cannot
    ask has to assume the worse case or be wrong. The three values are named so the day a target
    needs write-combining, the answer is a value here rather than a second mapping path nobody can
    see from userspace.

  THING 2 IS NOT DONE AND IT HAS A PREREQUISITE NOBODY OWNS, which is what looking at it found.
  Publishing the boot surface as an ordinary `ProviderKind::Display` needs a publisher that can HAND
  DisplayService the surface, and there is nothing to hand: `SYS_FRAMEBUFFER_MAP` maps into the
  CALLER's address space and answers with a virtual address, and both kernel objects that could carry
  memory across a channel - `MemoryObject::create_in` and `DmaBuffer::create_in` - ALLOCATE fresh
  physical pages. Nothing in this kernel wraps an EXISTING physical range in a transferable object.
  THAT IS A CAPABILITY DECISION AND NOT AN OVERSIGHT: a handle that maps arbitrary physical memory is
  exactly what a capability system must not hand out casually, so the object would have to be minted
  by the kernel for one range it already owns and nothing else. Whoever takes thing 2 owns that
  object first; it is not a refactor of what exists.
  NOT YET VERIFIED ON THE TWO EMULATED PORTS: the ramfb descriptors those two boot paths build are a
  one-field rename with a literal value, and nothing but an aarch64 or riscv64 boot exercises them.
  They are read and they are arithmetic - thirty-two bits is four bytes - but they are unrun, and the
  final tri-architecture pass is what confirms them.
- [ ] **16550 UART** (BLOCKED on the authority and handoff mechanisms named above, or scoped to
  firmware-described MMIO and blocked on the identity prerequisite): retain the minimal existing
  kernel early-console path, then hand a
  discovered standard UART's MMIO/PIO and IRQ capabilities to a restartable userspace
  serial driver after DeviceManager is online. Support FIFO, RX/TX interrupt flow and
  bounded buffering behind the canonical serial contract; isolate baud/clock/platform
  description from the common 16550 register engine and preserve panic output fallback.
  AND THE SERIAL CONTRACT HAS BEEN DEFINED (2026-09-15), by the first of its three claimants to be
  implemented: virtio-serial multiport, which was in GROUP 1 and therefore ahead of both this item
  and USB CDC-ACM. THE BYTE STREAM IS THE BASE - `liber:device@1`'s `console-stream`, published as
  `ConsoleBytes` and served through `drivers::serial_port`, with no baud, parity, stop bits, DTR, RTS
  or break anywhere in it - and the LINE CODING AND CONTROL LINES ARE A SERIAL LAYER on top: a
  separate interface with its own provider kind, which a device that has those controls serves IN
  ADDITION. The reasoning is written out in full under that item; what this item inherits is that it
  does not get to decide it again. So a 16550 publishes `ConsoleBytes` over the shared port module
  like any other byte source, and the serial layer is owned by the first of 16550, PL011 and CDC-ACM
  that actually needs line coding - which is a smaller thing than a contract, and is not this item's
  precondition.
- [ ] **ARM PL011 UART**: BLOCKED: the firmware-node device identity is an unowned architectural
  prerequisite (see the identity section above) - this driver binds through an ACPI/FDT node rather
  than a PCI function, and that identity has no numbered owner. Then: perform the same early-console
  -> userspace handoff for the existing PL011 path, sharing the serial contract, buffering, lifecycle
  and tests with 16550 while retaining its distinct register/interrupt implementation. Bind through
  ACPI/FDT compatible identity and supplied clock/IRQ data, not a QEMU address constant.
- [ ] **SDHCI** standard SD/eMMC host controller: command/data inhibit handling,
  capabilities, voltage/clock negotiation, card identification, block addressing,
  PIO first and bounded ADMA only after the simple path is proven; expose removable
  media through the existing block contract. Keep the SD protocol core independent of
  PCI/ACPI/FDT attachment so board-specific clock, reset, regulator and pinmux glue can
  remain outside the universal driver. Cover insertion/removal and read-only cards.

  **ITEM PLAN (2026-09-16), written before any code because the rule below says so.**

  CLASSIFICATION: `driver`, and the THIRD consumer of the plain-PCI resource profile. It owns no
  shared contract: NVMe brought the profile, AHCI brought the BAR index it needed, and this one
  should cost a row in `RESOURCED` and nothing else. If it does not, the table is still wrong and
  this item is where that is found out.

  WHAT IT REQUIRES THAT ALREADY EXISTS: the same prerequisite set as the two storage drivers before
  it, discharged the same way, plus `driver_protocol::block` as the wire and `drivers::blk` for the
  request refusals. The bind window is measured for every driver by DeviceManager now, so this item
  inherits that rather than arranging it.

  WHAT IT OWNS THAT DOES NOT YET EXIST: THE SD PROTOCOL CORE, and the item text says where it must
  live - independent of how the controller is attached, so that board-specific clock, reset,
  regulator and pinmux glue can sit outside it. That is a `drivers::sdhci` of pure decisions with a
  host test each: the present-state inhibits, the command and response shapes, the OCR voltage
  window, the CSD capacity parse in BOTH its versions, and which addressing a card uses.
  THE CSD IS WHERE THIS DRIVER IS MOST LIKELY TO BE WRONG and it is worth naming now: version 1 and
  version 2 encode capacity differently, a version 1 card's size is a product of three fields and a
  version 2 card's is one field plus a constant, and a driver that reads the wrong one reports a
  32 GB card as 2 GB or a 2 GB card as something absurd. Both are parsed and both are tested.
  AND WHICH ADDRESSING A CARD USES IS THE OTHER: standard-capacity cards are addressed in BYTES and
  high-capacity cards in BLOCKS, decided by one bit of the OCR. A driver that sends a block number
  to a byte-addressed card reads the first 512 bytes of the medium for every request.

  WHAT IT EXCLUDES: ADMA, which the item text puts after the simple path is proven, so the first
  slice is PIO; UHS speed modes and 1.8 V signalling; eMMC-specific commands, boot partitions and
  RPMB; SPI mode; and the board glue the item deliberately keeps outside this driver.

  TARGET DEVICE MODEL: QEMU `-device sdhci-pci` with an `sd-card` behind it, which this machine's
  QEMU offers.

  OBSERVABLE-EFFECT ORACLE, to the same convention as the two before it: a pattern written to a block
  and read back and compared through the published `block` provider, a capacity answered from the
  card's CSD, and a range past the last block refused with the typed status rather than clamped.

  MEASURABLE LIMITS: one command outstanding, with `PRESENT_STATE`'s command and data inhibits
  checked before each rather than a delay; a bounded wait on the interrupt-status bits with a typed
  timeout; the PIO path bounded to one block per transfer, which is what makes the simple path simple.

  DEFINITION OF DONE FOR ITS CLASS: host tests over the decisions with malformed inputs before any
  MMIO; the QEMU model proving the governed process, the DeviceManager binding and the block
  contract; the bind window recorded per target; registered in the verification model in the same
  change. Acceptance on aarch64 and riscv64 is a separate measurement on each, and this plan does not
  claim it.

  **WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-16): up on x86_64 on the first attempt, with its oracle passing.**
  `driver.sdhci: online (00:0c.0, 131072 x 512 bytes)` - the sixty-four megabyte card the harness
  attaches, read out of the CSD rather than assumed, through the identification sequence that ends in
  it: GO_IDLE, SEND_IF_COND, ACMD41 until the card powers up, ALL_SEND_CID, SEND_RELATIVE_ADDR,
  SEND_CSD, SELECT_CARD, SET_BLOCKLEN. Bind window 19 ticks. 404 tests pass.

  **THE TABLE COST ONE ROW AND NOTHING ELSE**, which is what the AHCI item said this one would find
  out. AHCI needed a BAR index; SDHCI needed neither that nor anything new - `device.rs` and all
  three architecture shims are untouched by it. A third family for one line is the generalisation
  answering for itself.

  **IT IS THE FIRST DRIVER IN THIS TREE TO DECLARE `dma = "none"`**, and that is worth more than the
  card reader. Its first slice is PIO, so every block moves through the controller's data port under
  CPU reads and writes and the controller is never handed a physical address; `none` is exactly the
  policy P02M0172 built for that case - bus mastering stays off and the binding cannot mint a DMA
  buffer at all. Until now every staged driver was either the network one or a trusted exception, and
  the policy's most restrictive value had no consumer.
  THAT FALSIFIED A KERNEL TEST, CORRECTLY. `kernel.dma_policy.the_kernel_registry_is_the_manifest_migration_table`
  asserted that the network driver requires translation and EVERY OTHER ROW is the trusted exception.
  That was a true description of the table on the day it was written and had become a frozen list
  rather than a rule, so the first driver to use the policy the mechanism exists for failed it. The
  test now holds the rule - each entry carries the policy it DECLARES - with both named exceptions
  spelled out.

  **AND ADMA2 IS IN (2026-09-19), WHICH IS WHAT LIFTS THE ONE-BLOCK BOUND.** A request moves up to
  128 kibibytes - 256 blocks - through a descriptor table instead of 512 bytes through the data
  port, and the bound is PUBLISHED rather than assumed: the capacity reply carries it, and a request
  past it is refused rather than shortened, which is the failure a clamp would hide.

  THE DECLARATION WAS THE FIRST THING IN THE WAY, AND IT WAS RIGHT TO BE. This entry declared
  `dma = "none"` because the PIO path never masters the bus, and that is ENFORCEMENT rather than
  documentation: the first ADMA build's descriptor table was refused by the kernel with the driver
  reporting `no memory for the descriptor table`, because a claim under a `none` policy mints no DMA
  buffer at all. The declaration is `trusted-untranslated` now, at the cost the threat model already
  records for every other storage driver here - with no translating controller in the path, a
  DMA-mastering driver is a TCB member for memory safety.

  THREE DECISIONS IN THE CORE WITH FIXTURES, because each is a way this is written wrong:
  - THE DESCRIPTOR'S LENGTH FIELD IS SIXTEEN BITS AND ZERO MEANS 65536. So the per-descriptor bound
    is 65024 - the largest whole number of blocks below the field's range - and every length is a
    number the field holds literally. A driver writing 65536 writes zero, and a driver writing zero
    for an EMPTY span asks for the maximum.
  - AUTO CMD12 IS PART OF A MULTI-BLOCK TRANSFER AND MUST NOT BE PART OF A SINGLE ONE. Without it a
    multi-block read streams until the card is told to stop, so the next command meets a card that
    is still sending; with it after ONE block it is a stop for a transmission that already ended,
    and the card reports an illegal command.
  - THE LAST DESCRIPTOR IS MARKED. A table whose end is not marked is a controller reading whatever
    follows it as another descriptor - the memory after this driver's table, read as addresses to
    transfer into.

  **AND AN INTERMITTENT FAILURE FOUND A REAL DEFECT, WHICH IS THE PART WORTH CARRYING PAST THIS
  ITEM.** The suite passed on one tag set and failed on another, at the SINGLE-block write - a
  transfer the descriptor arithmetic cannot reach. It was found by making the driver say things it
  could not say before, in the same three rounds the NVMe bring-up needed: first the ADMA fault, then
  the command's own status, then the registers behind it, which settled it -
  `command 24 reported an error - int 02008001, adma-err 01`. Bit 25 of the interrupt status is an
  ADMA error and `01` is the FETCH-DESCRIPTOR state: the controller could not read the table.
  AN ADMA2 DESCRIPTOR CARRIES A 32-BIT ADDRESS AND THIS CONTROLLER'S `CAP` CLAIMS NO 64-BIT BUS, and
  the allocator had put the table above four gibibytes - so the low half reached the controller and
  named whatever lives there. WHICH SPAN LANDED WHERE DEPENDED ON WHAT ELSE HAD ALLOCATED FIRST,
  which is why it looked like flakiness. Both spans are checked now and a driver that cannot address
  its own descriptors keeps the PIO path and SAYS so, which is the same refusal the AHCI driver makes
  before it binds.

  **AND IT TOOK THE `none` DMA POLICY'S ONLY EXAMPLE WITH IT, WHICH IS WORTH SAYING OUT LOUD.**
  `sdhci` was the first and only entry in the registry to declare `dma = "none"`; after ADMA2 there
  is no `none` row at all. The policy is not untested for that - what it MEANS is held by the
  kernel's policy matrix, which covers all three against every DMA mode and needs no driver to
  declare one - and its ENFORCEMENT was demonstrated by the very change that removed the row: the
  first ADMA build was refused its descriptor table, because a claim under `none` mints no DMA
  buffer at all. A policy proven by a refusal it caused is better evidence than a declaration
  nobody exercises. Two tests that had the old table written into them as a list were rewritten as
  a RULE rather than renumbered.

  AND THAT LEAVES A GAP THAT IS NOT THIS ITEM'S TO CLOSE: **nothing in this system can ask for
  memory a 32-bit device can address.** `SYS_DMA_BUFFER_CREATE` takes a size and a device and the
  frame allocator takes a page count; neither carries an address limit, so a driver whose device
  addresses 32 bits can only check what it was given and give up. It is not rare hardware - it is
  this controller, and the next one - and the answer is an allocator that can be asked, which is a
  change to the memory ABI rather than to a driver. Recorded here so an owner can decide; until then
  ADMA2 is taken when the allocator happens to oblige and the PIO path serves when it does not,
  which is honest and is said in the log either way.

  THE ORACLE MOVES A SPAN THAT NEEDS MORE THAN ONE DESCRIPTOR - 128 blocks against the 65024-byte
  per-descriptor bound - and it writes a SECOND span in between, which is not belt and braces: the
  driver moves bytes through a bounce span it owns, and that span still holds the write's contents
  afterwards, so a read whose descriptors do not actually fetch would find the right answer already
  sitting there. A FIRST VERSION OF THIS TEST DID EXACTLY THAT and passed a driver whose descriptors
  all named the same address. With the second span in, that mutation fails on the array.

  WHAT IS STILL OPEN: insertion and removal at runtime, which needs an arrival event this tree does
  not have for a PCI function let alone a card slot.

  AND THE OTHER TWO ARE WALLS WITH NUMBERS UNDER THEM NOW (2026-09-19), which is what they were
  missing: each was listed as work and neither had been checked against the machine.

  UHS MODES ARE NOT OFFERED, AND THE DRIVER SAYS SO. `CAPABILITIES_1` bits 0, 1 and 2 are SDR50,
  SDR104 and DDR50, and this controller advertises none of them - `no uhs offered` in the online
  line. The reason that line exists rather than a sentence here: a controller with no faster mode and
  a driver that never asks for one look IDENTICAL from outside, and the log could not tell them
  apart. Now it can, on every machine this ever runs on, including ones that do offer it.

  eMMC HAS NO DEVICE TO ADDRESS. `-device help` on the pinned QEMU lists `sd-card` and `sd-card-spi`
  and no eMMC part at all, and `sd-card` takes two options - a drive and a spec version. eMMC
  commands are a different command set for a device class this harness cannot present.

  AND THE TWO EMULATED TARGETS ARE IN (2026-09-19). The driver suites pass on
  aarch64 (100 tests, 1539 s) and riscv64 (102 tests, 2097 s) under `drivers,pci,slow`, and on both of them this driver reports
  `adma2 256 blocks` rather than the `pio one block` fallback an x86_64 boot sometimes takes - the
  descriptor table lands below 4 GiB there, which is the same code taking the other branch.
- [x] **EDID parsing and the monitor vocabulary** (SHARED LIBRARY - closes on host tests, binds
  nothing): add one bounded EDID parser and typed monitor mode/physical-size/capability vocabulary
  shared by simple framebuffer, virtio-gpu and later real display drivers. Verify header/checksums,
  cap extension blocks and reject malformed timings before arithmetic. EDID is a helper/provider
  contract, not an independent process poking arbitrary buses.
  SPLIT FROM ITS TRANSPORT 2026-09-13, BY THIS FILE'S OWN RULE rather than by preference: "the same
  split applies to every other candidate whose only deliverable today is a parser: classify it as a
  shared library and close it as one, rather than holding it against a bind gate it cannot reach."
  DDC is I2C on the display connector, there is no I2C controller in this tree and no display driver
  that can reach one, so the fetch has no event that closes it and the parse does. The transport half
  is the item below.
  IMPLEMENTED 2026-09-13 as the `edid` crate.

    REQUIRES   nothing that does not exist: 128 bytes and the blocks after them
    OWNS       `src/user/libs/display/edid` - `Edid`, `Timing`, `Mode`, `Descriptor`, `PhysicalSize`,
                 `VideoInput` and `Manufactured`
    GATE       `host.edid`, 13 tests over a fixture assembled field by field, registered in the
                 verification model and in the frozen release-required set in the same change

  FOUR REFUSALS BEFORE A FIELD IS READ - the length, the header, the checksum and the version - and
  they are four because they answer different questions: the first two say these bytes are an EDID,
  the third says they arrived intact, and the fourth says this reader understands them. A parser that
  checked only the header reads a corrupted block as a monitor with strange opinions.
  AND THE TIMINGS ARE REFUSED BEFORE THEY ARE ARITHMETIC, which is what "reject malformed timings
  before arithmetic" means concretely: a mode with no active pixels, a mode with no BLANKING (the
  refresh rate divides by active plus blanking, so zero blanking is a division by zero in every
  consumer), and a sync pulse that runs past the blanking interval it lives in, which describes a
  signal no controller can generate. `refresh_millihertz` is checked arithmetic on top of that,
  because a caller can build a `Timing` this parser never produced.
  THE VOCABULARY IS THE HALF THAT STOPS THREE DRIVERS INVENTING THREE DESCRIPTIONS. `PhysicalSize` has
  THREE answers - centimetres, a bare aspect ratio, or nothing - because a compositor that computed a
  DPI from the third divides by zero; `VideoInput` says `None` for a bit depth on EDID 1.3, where the
  byte carried none, rather than reading the analogue flags as a colour depth; `Manufactured`
  distinguishes a week from a bare year from a MODEL year, because week 255 is not a week.
  AND AN EXTENSION IS CARRIED, COUNTED AND CHECKSUMMED HERE AND PARSED ELSEWHERE. The count is capped,
  the blocks must actually be there (a monitor that declares two and hands over one is a read that
  stopped early, and the base block's own tail is not an extension), and a block that does not sum is
  not handed on - it would be read for capabilities the panel does not have. What is INSIDE a CEA-861
  block belongs to the item that needs an audio or HDMI capability.

- [ ] **DDC/AUX transport and monitor discovery** (BLOCKED on a display controller that can reach a
  bus): fetch the EDID above over DDC/I2C or AUX, supplied by the BOUND display controller, and
  publish the monitor's modes and physical size to DisplayService. Split from the parser above on
  2026-09-13: there is no I2C controller item, no AUX path, and virtio-gpu's own EDID capability is
  the first fixture this could use - so the fetch is gated on a controller item and on the typed
  display-device interface `P02M0103a-wsi` owns, and neither is this item's to build.
- [ ] **PCIe hotplug, AER and PME** shared bus infrastructure: process standardized slot
  notifications, enumerate/remove functions dynamically, surface corrected/uncorrected
  Advanced Error Reporting records and power-management events, and coordinate safe
  driver stop before resource removal. Bound capability walks and event storms; a fatal
  error quarantines the affected function/subtree rather than resetting unrelated PCIe
  devices. Native platform slot controllers remain firmware glue behind this contract.
  THIS ITEM OWNS THE LIFECYCLE IT NEEDS, because nothing else does: `BindingState` has no
  `Removed` and cannot, since the inventory is boot-only. So this item adds the removal
  event, the transition through `Stopping`, and a terminal `Removed`. TERMINAL AND
  REPLUGGABLE ARE NOT A CONTRADICTION, AND THE MODEL HAS TO SAY WHY: the slot outlives the
  device as a tombstone, `Removed` is terminal FOR THAT BINDING, and only a NEW ARRIVAL on
  that slot opens a new one - whose claim mints the next generation, so a message stamped
  with the old one is refused by arithmetic rather than by anyone remembering. A removal
  whose teardown was NOT confirmed ends in `Quarantined`, not `Removed`: `Removed` says the
  device is gone and nothing is owed, which is the one thing an unconfirmed teardown cannot
  say. It also reaches into two neighbours and says so rather than assuming: P02M0165 gains
  a device-removed stop intent, and P02M0166 gains a rendering for `Removed`. A USB or SD
  child disappearing is NOT any of this: it withdraws a provider and its parent controller
  stays `Online`.

  **ITEM PLAN (2026-09-18), written before any code because the rule below says so.**

  CLASSIFICATION: NOT A DRIVER. It publishes no provider and claims no device: it is bus
  infrastructure plus a lifecycle, so it closes on the lifecycle's host tests and on a guest run that
  plugs and unplugs a device while the system is up.

  WHAT IT REQUIRES THAT ALREADY EXISTS: the kernel's PCI enumeration and its capability walk;
  DeviceManager's node table, its teardown and its claim generations; `driver_binding`'s state
  machine and stop intents.

  WHAT IT OWNS, IN THREE PARTS THAT ARE NOT THE SAME SIZE:
    1. THE LIFECYCLE, which is the part nothing else could do and the part the item text is mostly
       about: the removal event, the transition through `Stopping`, and a terminal `Removed`.
    2. THE SLOT, which is a PCIe capability on a root port: its status says what is in it and its
       control says what to be told about. Bounded capability walks and a bound on event storms,
       because both are numbers the HARDWARE chooses.
    3. THE COORDINATION, which is the item's own sentence - "coordinate safe driver stop before
       resource removal" - and is why `Removed` is reachable only from `Stopping`.

  WHAT IT EXCLUDES: AER beyond SURFACING a record - a fatal error quarantines the affected function
  or subtree and does not reset unrelated devices, which is a refusal rather than a recovery
  mechanism; and PME beyond noticing it, because what a wake means belongs to a power policy that
  does not exist.

  OBSERVABLE-EFFECT ORACLE: a device added to a live machine binds and serves, and one removed from
  it leaves its binding `Removed` with its resources released - driven from the monitor while the
  guest runs, which is what `pcie-root-port` and `device_add` exist for.

  **WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-18): PART 1 IS DONE. PARTS 2 AND 3 ARE NOT.**

  `BindingState::Removed` and `StopIntent::DeviceRemoved` are in `driver-binding` with four host
  tests, and the two neighbours the item said it reaches into have their halves: `liber:device@1`
  carries `removed` in `binding-state`, and SystemGraphService renders it as STOPPED rather than
  FAILED - nothing went wrong, the machine changed shape, and a red row for a disk somebody unplugged
  on purpose would also be indistinguishable from `quarantined`, which is the one that IS a problem.

  THE TWO RULES THE TESTS PIN, because both are places this could have been written wrong:
