  `Removed` is reachable ONLY from `Stopping` - a binding whose driver was never told to stop has
  resources nobody has confirmed are free, which is `Quarantined`'s question rather than this one's -
  and NOTHING LEAVES `Removed`. The slot outlives the device as a tombstone, and a device plugged in
  afterwards opens a new binding whose claim mints the next generation, so a message stamped with the
  old one is refused by arithmetic rather than by anyone remembering. An unconfirmed removal still
  lands at `Quarantined`, whatever the intent was.

  **AND PART 2 IS IN (2026-09-18): THE SLOT IS FOUND, ARMED AND SAID.**

  `resolve_slot`, `slot_event`, `slot_acknowledge` and `slot_arm` are in the shared PCI module with
  three in-guest tests over a synthetic config space. The boot now reads
  `pci: 00:0f.0 carries hot-plug slot 1 - empty`, which is the only way an empty slot is visible at
  all: an operator who plugs a disk into one needs to know the system was ever going to notice.

  THREE THINGS THE TESTS PIN, and each is a way this is written wrong:
  - THE CAPABILITY IS ON EVERY PCIe FUNCTION AND THE SLOT IS NOT. An endpoint carries the same
    capability at the same offsets, so a reader that goes straight to Slot Status reads an endpoint's
    reserved bytes and calls them a presence bit - a device that is always there, in a slot that does
    not exist. The port type and `Slot Implemented` are what make the rest mean anything.
  - PRESENCE AND THE CHANGE ARE TWO QUESTIONS. The state says what is in the slot NOW; the change bit
    is STICKY. A reader watching only the state never learns a device was swapped between two looks -
    the state is the same before and after - and one acting on the change bit alone knows something
    happened and not which of the two it was.
  - THE CHANGE BIT IS CLEARED BY WRITING A ONE. A reader that masks it out and writes the result back
    leaves it exactly as it was, and every later look reports the same change for ever: one plug read
    as thousands of arrivals, which looks like a device that keeps re-appearing rather than like a
    bug in the acknowledgement.

  **AND THE FIXTURE FOUND A DEFECT IN THE FIRST VERSION OF THIS, WHICH IS WHY IT MODELS RW1C.** Slot
  Control and Slot Status share ONE DWORD, and config space is written a dword at a time - so arming
  the slot with a read-modify-write carried the status bits back as ones and acknowledged every event
  the slot had recorded. The comment above the code said that must not happen and the code did it.
  Both writes are now deliberate dwords: arming writes ZERO into the status half, which is how
  "change nothing" is spelled in an RW1C register, and acknowledging writes exactly the one bit it
  means. The fake config space models the rule, because a fixture that stores what it is given makes
  the correct acknowledgement look like SETTING the bit and the incorrect one look like clearing it -
  the defect exactly upside down.

  AND LANDING IT FOUND SOMETHING ABOUT THE TREE: the kernel crate had NO `#[test]` anywhere, because
  its tests are `tagged_test!` and run in a booted guest. Writing the first one derived a `host.kernel`
  suite the verification model had no entry for and that `cargo test` cannot build - two gates red
  from one attribute. The tests are in the kernel's own form.

  **AND WHAT PART 3 ACTUALLY NEEDS WAS ESTABLISHED RATHER THAN GUESSED, which is the useful half of
  not having written it yet.** It needs AN INTERRUPT, not a poll, and the reason is this tree's own
  rule rather than a preference:

  DeviceManager's loop is a `wait_any` over channels WITH NO DEADLINE when it is idle. A slot epoch
  it checked "on every wake it already takes" would be checked only when something else woke it -
  which for a machine sitting at a prompt is never, so a disk plugged in at that moment is noticed
  when the next unrelated message arrives and not before. Giving the loop a periodic wake instead is
  the thing this tree warns about in as many words beside two other drivers: a spinning loop starves
  the cooperative scheduler for the guest's whole life, and here it would do so to watch a slot that
  is empty on almost every machine.

  SO THE PATH IS: the kernel binds the root port's hot-plug interrupt, and on a presence change it
  updates the inventory and signals an Event that DeviceManager holds - `SYS_EVENT_CREATE` and its
  wait are already the mechanism for exactly this, so no new syscall is needed for the notification
  itself. What IS new is the inventory becoming writable at runtime: the device table is built once
  at boot, and an arrival has nowhere to be recorded.

  AND AN EPOCH WAS WRITTEN AND TAKEN BACK OUT, deliberately. A counter saying how many times the
  inventory changed shape is needed by every design for this - but with no consumer it is a mechanism
  nothing executes, which is the same thing this file refuses beside NCM and CDC-ACM. It goes in with
  the reader that uses it.

  **AND PART 3 IS IN (2026-09-18): A DEVICE PLUGGED INTO A LIVE MACHINE BINDS, AND ONE ASKED FOR IS
  GIVEN BACK IN ORDER.**

  THE ROOT PORT'S INTERRUPT is bound at boot: `arm_hot_plug_interrupts` reads each port's interrupt
  line and pin, clears the interrupt-disable bit this kernel sets on every other function, routes the
  line and registers a handler. INTx AND NOT MSI, deliberately - every other interrupt here is
  per-device MSI-X because a driver owns the device, and a root port has no driver and no table of
  its own to program; its event rate is bounded by how fast a person can plug things in. A port with
  no pin, or a line firmware left at `0xff`, is SAID and polled instead: a handler on a vector
  nothing raises is a slot that works and a device that never arrives. The idle pass polls every slot
  as well, because a shared legacy line can be missed and a device nobody noticed is the one failure
  this path exists to prevent.

  THE INVENTORY IS WRITABLE AND DOES NOT RENUMBER. An index is what a claim, a binding and every
  message in flight are addressed by, so a departure clears `on_bus` and KEEPS the row; an arrival
  refills the row that address had, or appends. A refilled row gets a claim slot whose GENERATION HAS
  MOVED, which is what makes a replug a new binding: a message stamped with the departed device's
  generation is refused by arithmetic rather than by anyone remembering. `device-entry` carries
  `present` now and `lsdev` prints it - a listing that showed a disk somebody pulled out as though it
  were still there is the one thing an operator asking what is in this machine must not be told.

  THE NOTIFICATION REACHES DeviceManager over a channel the kernel holds, registered with
  `SYS_DEVICE_EVENTS` under the DeviceManager privilege - the same shape as `SYS_CONSOLE_ATTACH`, and
  for the same reason: the manager is asleep in its one wait, and a poll fast enough to feel
  immediate is a poll that runs forever for the one moment a year it has something to report. The
  channel joins that one wait rather than being watched beside it.

  THE COORDINATION IS THE PART THE ITEM'S OWN SENTENCE IS ABOUT, and the shape it took is the one the
  hardware already has. `device_del` does not remove anything: it presses the ATTENTION BUTTON, which
  is a request. The kernel takes the row off the bus and tells the manager; the manager stops the
  driver with `StopIntent::DeviceRemoved`; the teardown is confirmed and the binding lands at
  `Removed`; the kernel sees that nothing holds the claim any more and only THEN powers the slot
  down - which is what lets the port take the device out. The slot is powered back up afterwards,
  because a slot left down after a removal is a slot that works exactly once.

  THREE THINGS THAT WERE WRONG AND LOOKED RIGHT, each found by running it:
  - **An armed slot is not a powered slot.** A slot with a power controller comes out of reset with
    the power OFF, and a port holding an unpowered slot presents nothing behind it: the presence bit
    stays clear, the change bit never sets, and the interrupt just armed never fires. Everything
    looks correct and nothing happens.
  - **The power indicator's OFF is three and not zero.** The two bits encode on, blink and off as
    01, 10 and 11, and ZERO IS RESERVED. A port reads the indicator as part of deciding that the
    guest has finished with the slot, so writing the reserved value is a power-down request the port
    does not recognise: the slot goes dark and the device stays in it.
  - **q35's default hot-plug is not the port's.** QEMU's q35 signals `device_add` through an ACPI
    GPE even for a PCIe root port, so a kernel reading the slot registers - which is what the PCI
    Express specification says a root port reports through - sees nothing at all. The interactive
    profile now says `acpi-pci-hotplug-with-bridge-support=off`, which makes the port behave the way
    its own capability says it does.

  SIX IN-GUEST TESTS AND A LIVE GATE. The tests drive the slot protocol over a fake config space -
  that arming powers the slot, that a slot without a power controller is not told to power up, that
  the button is a REQUEST with the device still in the slot, that powering down says off on the
  indicator, that a poll answers each change once with the state it left behind, and that a port with
  no pin is not bound to a vector - plus two over the inventory: that a departure keeps the row and
  an arrival refills it with a new generation, and that a slot is powered down only once nothing
  holds the device. `./check.sh --gate qemu-pcie-hotplug` is the oracle: it plugs a device into a
  running machine, watches it bind, asks for it back, and reads the lines IN ORDER - a log that only
  counted them would pass for a surprise removal too - then plugs another one in, because a slot that
  works once passes every other check in this list.

  AND THE COUNTS ARE COUNTS AND NOT MATCHES, which is a gate that would otherwise pass on yesterday's
  evidence: an instance that is already up has every previous cycle in its log, so "does a line like
  this exist" is answered before anything is plugged in. What a step means is that a line ARRIVED.

  **AND AER AND PME ARE IN (2026-09-18): AN ERROR IS SURFACED, AND A FATAL ONE QUARANTINES.**

  THE FIXTURE EXISTS AFTER ALL, and finding it is what unblocked this. The note above said the
  machine reports no error to surface, which is true of a machine nobody makes misbehave - QEMU's
  monitor has `pcie_aer_inject_error`, which sets a function's error status registers the way the
  hardware would. Every other gate here drives the system through an interface it offers; this is the
  one that makes the hardware wrong on purpose, because nothing this system can be ASKED to do
  produces an error record.

  AN ERROR RECORD LIVES WHERE x86_64 COULD NOT REACH, AND THAT WAS THE REAL WORK. Advanced Error
  Reporting is a PCIe EXTENDED capability: it begins at offset 0x100, and the legacy configuration
  mechanism this port uses carries EIGHT BITS of register number. A read at 0x104 is a read at 0x04 -
  the command and status registers - so a walk that started without asking would find a capability
  whose id is whatever those bytes happen to be, on every function of every machine. So the kernel now
  finds ACPI's MCFG table, maps the memory-mapped window it describes, and routes any offset past 0xFF
  through it; `ConfigAccess` gained `extended_reach`, which the two ECAM ports answer `true` without
  asking and this one answers from whether firmware published a window at all. A machine with no MCFG
  is SAID once at boot rather than guessed at, because a guessed base is a read of whatever the map
  holds there.

  THE FIRST VERSION OF THE WINDOW PANICKED ON ITS SECOND ACCESS, and the reason is worth keeping: it
  borrowed ONE page and remapped it per read, under the config lock. `map_page` REFUSES to replace a
  live mapping - deliberately, because a mapper that overwrote one would silently drop whatever frame
  was there - so "remap a scratch page" is not an operation this kernel has. The window is mapped
  ONCE at boot instead, for the first sixteen buses, which is the same rule `MAX_BUS` already states
  for the bus walk: the walk stops where the mechanism does. Mapping all 256 buses the MCFG describes
  would be 256 MB of address space and five hundred page tables for a register almost nothing reads.

  WHAT IS SURFACED IS BOTH HALVES AND NOT ONLY THE FATAL ONE. A corrected error is not a failure - the
  hardware fixed it - but a link correcting a rising number of them is a link about to stop working,
  and the record is the only warning anybody gets; a machine that reported only the fatal ones would
  report nothing until the disk went away. Each is named in words rather than as a bit number, and the
  LOWEST set bit is named rather than all of them, because one fault sets several and six names for
  one event reads as six faults.

  AND THE STATUS IS READ AND CLEARED IN ONE OPERATION, because the bits are sticky: a reader that only
  read would report the same error for ever, and a machine whose log says the same thing a thousand
  times says nothing. The SEVERITY register is not touched - it is the function's own policy about
  which errors are fatal, and acknowledging it would rewrite that, so the next real error would be
  reported under a severity nobody chose.

  A FATAL ERROR QUARANTINES THE FUNCTION AND RESETS NOTHING, which is the item's own scope. Recovery
  would mean a link retrain or a secondary-bus reset, and a reset reaches every function behind the
  port - so a disk that failed would take a working network card with it, and a kernel doing that on
  its own is worse than the fault. What the kernel does instead has teeth: it clears BUS MASTERING
  first, because a function that reported a malformed TLP may still be writing into memory somebody
  owns and goes on doing it until it is told not to, and only then reports it. The INVENTORY ROW STAYS
  `on_bus`, because the device is on the bus: what changed is that nothing may use it, and that is the
  binding's state to hold rather than the inventory's.

  SO THERE IS A THIRD DEVICE EVENT AND A THIRD STOP INTENT, and both had to exist. `DEVICE_FAULTED` is
  neither an arrival nor a departure - the address is still valid and nothing was unplugged - and
  `StopIntent::DeviceFaulted` is the ONE intent whose CONFIRMED teardown still lands at `Quarantined`.
  A confirmation says the DRIVER released its resources; it says nothing about the device, and the
  device is what stopped being described by anything. Landing it at `Failed` would let a retry bind the
  same broken function, and landing it at `Removed` would say it had gone somewhere.

  AND PME IS NOTICED AND NOT ACTED ON, which is also the item's own scope: what a wake MEANS belongs to
  a power policy that does not exist. What a root port can say is that a function behind it asked to be
  woken and WHICH one, and the acknowledgement writes the status bit ALONE - the requester id shares
  the register and is read-only, so writing back the value that was read would be a write of reserved
  bits.

  SIX MORE IN-GUEST TESTS AND A LIVE GATE. The tests drive a fake config space that models extended
  space as its own half - because the two are reached by different MECHANISMS, and a fixture that made
  them one array would model a machine nobody has - and they pin: that an unreachable read answers the
  register 0x100 bytes BELOW rather than nothing, which is why the reach question exists at all; that a
  malformed capability list stops on a header of ones, on a pointer that goes backwards, and on one
  that points at itself; that a record is read and cleared in one pass and the severity register
  survives it; that severity is the FUNCTION'S and the same bit is fatal on one and not on another;
  that a function answering all ones has left the bus rather than reported every error there is; and
  that a PME names its requester without the acknowledgement writing it back. Two more cover the
  sweeps: only the functions that said something are filled, and the list is idempotent because a
  hot-plug slot's address is reused.

  `./check.sh --gate qemu-pcie-aer` IS THE ORACLE, and it makes three claims in order: a corrected
  error is surfaced; a fatal one on the ROOT PORT is said and NOT acted on, because a bridge has no
  binding and the kernel says so rather than inventing one; and a fatal one on a BOUND function - a
  virtio-serial device hot-plugged into the live slot with error reporting on - stops it mastering the
  bus, stops its driver, and lands the binding at `Quarantined`. The last step then checks that no
  removal was recorded, because the distinction between the two states is the whole reason both exist:
  the device is still in the slot.

  **AND THE THREE-ARCHITECTURE RUN HAPPENED (2026-09-18), AND IT FOUND SOMETHING.** Building this for
  aarch64 produced FIFTY-FOUR dead-code errors, and they were not noise: `settle_hot_plug` and
  `settle_pci_faults` were behind `#[cfg(target_arch = "x86_64")]`, so neither the slot protocol nor
  the error poll existed on the emulated ports AT ALL. Reading a slot or an error record is reading
  config space, which every backend here does; what is genuinely this architecture's is routing a
  legacy interrupt line through an I/O APIC. Both settle passes now run on every port and only
  `arm_hot_plug_interrupts` is gated - which is the same fallback an x86_64 port whose firmware left
  a slot with no interrupt pin already takes.

  WHAT THE TWO EMULATED RUNS SHOW, STATED EXACTLY. The kernel suites pass on both - 89 tests each -
  and the aarch64 boot says `pci: no function on this machine reports errors` WITHOUT the "extended
  config space is unreachable here" clause, which is the precise evidence that the port reached
  extended config space (through its device-tree ECAM window; MCFG is x86_64's problem alone) and
  found no function carrying an AER capability. There is no `carries hot-plug slot` line either.

  AND HALF OF THAT WAS A STALE ASSUMPTION, CHECKED AND SPENT (2026-09-19). The reason recorded for
  keeping the root-port fixture off the emulated test profiles was that "adding a function would
  renumber the bus addresses several oracles print". THAT IS TRUE OF A FUNCTION ADDED AMONG THEM AND
  FALSE OF ONE ADDED AFTER THEM: QEMU assigns an address to each device without one in the order the
  arguments appear. The port is appended last on the profile the emulated targets use, and the
  driver oracles print the addresses they printed before it existed - `ahci 00:0b.0`, `sdhci 00:0c.0`,
  `hda 00:0d.0`, `xhci 00:05.0`, unchanged. The aarch64 boot now says
  `pci: 00:12.0 carries hot-plug slot 1 - empty`, which it had never said.

  AND THE OTHER HALF OF THE REASON WAS CIRCULAR: "a fixture no test asserts on yet". There is an
  oracle now - `the_machine_reports_a_hot_plug_slot_the_scan_found` - and it asserts the MACHINE'S
  SHAPE rather than a device: that the scan reaches a port at all, that the port carrying the
  harness's platform slot number is among those found, and that it is empty. A scan that stopped
  short, a capability walk that missed the slot, or a fixture quietly dropped from the harness each
  leave the hot-plug path built, armed and pointed at nothing - which is indistinguishable from
  working until somebody plugs a disk in.

  **AND ASKING THE QUESTION FOUND A DEFECT NOBODY COULD HAVE SEEN WITHOUT IT: FOUR IN-GUEST TESTS
  WERE OVERWRITING THE RUNNING KERNEL'S PORT TABLE.** `arm_hot_plug_slots` and `poll_slots` work
  through one static, and the tests that drive the slot machinery over a synthetic bus called them
  directly - so after they ran, the kernel held THEIR fake: a port at `00:00.0` with slot number
  zero that no machine has. The idle pass polls that table, and anything asking what hot-plug ports
  this machine has was answered with the fixture.

  IT WAS INVISIBLE BECAUSE NOTHING ASKED. The tests assert on their own return values and pass
  either way; the kernel polls a port that is not there and finds nothing, which is what it finds
  when a real port is empty. The first thing to ask the table was this oracle, and it failed on
  aarch64 and passed on x86_64 - THE SAME TESTS IN A DIFFERENT ORDER, which is the signature of
  shared state rather than of a machine difference.

  THE FIX IS A GUARD AND NOT A RULE, because a restore somebody has to remember is the same defect
  again: `HeldPorts` snapshots the table and the "slots reported" flag and puts both back when it
  drops, on every path out of a test including a panic. With it the oracle passes on aarch64 where
  it failed - the same test, the same order, the guard the only difference.

  ON ALL THREE PORTS, WITH EVERYTHING ELSE OF THE DAY IN THEM: `drivers,pci,slow,usb` passes with
  x86_64 104 tests, aarch64 101 in 1835 s and riscv64 103 in 1913 s, and all three print the same
  five lines - `carries hot-plug slot 1 - empty` where the emulated ports had never printed it at
  all, `2 UAS commands outstanding at once, each on its own tag`, `task management answered`,
  `codec 0 of 2` and `no uhs offered`.

  WHAT IS STILL OPEN, AND IT IS THE SMALLER HALF: a slot CHANGING STATE, and an error record being
  read back, on the emulated ports. Those need `device_add` against a live machine and a function
  with error reporting on, and the tooling for both - `lab.sh` and the two gates - is x86_64. What
  the two ports now show is that the scan reaches the port, arms it and reports it honestly.
- [ ] **TPM 2.0 TIS/CRB**: discover either standardized transport, serialize bounded TPM
  commands, enforce locality/timeout/cancel, validate response headers and expose only
  typed operations needed for random data, PCR measurement/quote and sealed keys.
  Applications never receive raw MMIO or unrestricted command passthrough; integrate
  with future measured/verified boot and identity policy without making TPM presence a
  boot requirement. Vendor firmware and proprietary security processors remain Phase 5.

  NOT STARTED, AND THE FIXTURE WAS CHECKED BEFORE ANY PLAN WAS WRITTEN (2026-09-19). QEMU offers two
  TPM backends and this machine can supply NEITHER: `emulator` needs `swtpm`, which is not installed,
  and `passthrough` needs a real `/dev/tpm*`, which does not exist here. So a TPM driver written
  today would bind nothing on every machine in this harness - the same wall CDC-ACM records, and the
  same reason it is written down rather than discovered by an implementer. What would close it is
  `swtpm` in the environment, which is a harness prerequisite and not a driver.
- [x] **FADT and GAS parsing** (SHARED LIBRARY - closes on host tests, binds nothing). Split out on
  2026-08-30, because the item below claimed the kernel "already reads" the FADT and it does not:
  the kernel has a GENERIC x86 ACPI table lookup and parses `APIC`, `SRAT` and `SLIT`; the only FADT
  consumer in the tree is the UEFI loader's ARM PSCI-field decoder. Absence of AML does not supply an
  FADT parser. This item owns bounded FADT field extraction and Generic Address Structure decoding
  with hostile-input fixtures over truncated, mis-versioned and out-of-range tables, and it publishes
  no device and claims nothing.
  IMPLEMENTED 2026-09-13 as the `acpi` crate, and closed on its own host suite - which is what a
  shared-library item closes on, by the standard stated in the completion section.

    REQUIRES   nothing that does not exist. It reads bytes: no binder, no claim, no device, no QEMU
                 model, and no allocation
    OWNS       `src/acpi` - `Table` (a checked system-description-table header), `Gas`,
                 `AddressSpace`, `AccessSize`, and `Fadt`'s field extraction
    GATE       `host.acpi`, 22 tests, registered in the verification model and in the frozen
                 release-required set in the same change

  THREE RULES, BECAUSE THEY ARE THE THREE WAYS A FIRMWARE TABLE LIES.
  THE DECLARED LENGTH IS CHECKED AGAINST THE BYTES AND NOTHING IS READ PAST IT. A header claiming 276
  bytes inside 200 is refused whole rather than read for the fields that happen to fit, and a table
  that declares 116 inside a page reads as 116 - the fixture for that one pads the buffer with a
  recognisable byte and requires every later field to be absent rather than that byte.
  A FIELD THE REVISION OR THE LENGTH DOES NOT COVER IS `None` AND NEVER ZERO. Zero is a legal value of
  almost every field here: a zero `SMI_CMD` means "no SMI command port" and a zero century index means
  "no century byte", so a reader that answered zero for absent would have destroyed the difference
  between a firmware that said nothing and one that said none. The ARM boot flags make the same point
  from the other side - `None` from a revision-4 table is "this table cannot say", which is not the
  same answer as "this machine has no PSCI".
  ARITHMETIC IS CHECKED BEFORE THE FIELD IS BELIEVED. A Generic Address Structure whose address plus
  its own span leaves the address space is refused once, here, rather than in each consumer that would
  have computed an end address from two firmware-chosen bytes; an access size that is not one of the
  five the standard defines is refused rather than read as "byte"; and an address with no width, or a
  width with no address, is refused as half a register rather than being resolved differently by two
  consumers reading two different fields.
  AND THREE ANSWERS ARE DECISIONS RATHER THAN FIELDS. A hardware-reduced machine has no PM1, PM2, PM
  timer or GPE blocks, so those accessors answer `None` there rather than handing back fields the
  standard says to ignore. The reset register needs its flag, its length AND a register that describes
  something, because a firmware that fills in a field it does not support is common. The century byte
  is refused on a machine whose own boot flags say it has no CMOS clock to index.
  THE TWO FORMS OF EVERY BLOCK ARE RESOLVED BY THE STANDARD'S RULE - the 64-bit `X_` structure wins
  when it is present - and a table whose two forms DISAGREE is reported through `Block::agrees` rather
  than resolved silently, because which of them to believe is the consumer's decision and it can only
  make it if it is told.
  WHAT IT DELIBERATELY DOES NOT DO: it does not touch a register, enable ACPI mode, arm a
  general-purpose event or reset a machine. Those are the item below and the two after it, and this
  one exists so that each of them reads the table through one parser rather than three.
  THE LOADER'S ONE-FIELD READER STAYS WHERE IT IS. `src/boot/uefi/src/acpi.rs` decodes the ARM boot
  flags itself because it does that before it has a memory map or a slice, and switching it is an
  aarch64 boot path that cannot be exercised while the slow architectures are held for the end of the
  job. The parser carries the same rule under test; the consolidation belongs to whichever item next
  touches that path.

- [ ] **ACPI fixed-hardware event delivery** (ARCHITECTURAL PREREQUISITE - a decision plus a
  mechanism). A PM1a status/enable pair reached through a GAS is either an SCI the KERNEL routes and
  hands on, or a claimable firmware-described node with a port-I/O or MMIO resource. That choice is
  not a detail of the power button, and until it is made no fixed-hardware ACPI event has a delivery
  path. It owns: the decision, written down; ACPI SCI routing - a shared, level-triggered legacy
  interrupt, which the MSI-X path does not provide; the handoff to whatever consumes the event; and,
  if the answer is a claimable driver, a RANGE-SCOPED REVOCABLE PORT-I/O RESOURCE KIND, since the
  public driver resource vocabulary is device MMIO, one IRQ, keys, system power and console and has
  no port-I/O object at all. Its fixture is named here too, because the item cannot be gated without
  one: a QEMU machine whose PM1a event can be raised on demand.

- [ ] **ACPI fixed-hardware power button** (driver, or in-kernel consumer if the item above decides
  so): publish a typed power-button provider with a capability-gated SHUTDOWN request, over the
  parser and the delivery path above. Validate event storms; keep board EC methods in Phase 5.
  SUSPEND IS NOT IN THIS ITEM (2026-08-30). It was listed beside shutdown, and the `system-power`
  interface has exactly two operations - `reboot` and `power-off` - so a suspend request had nothing
  to call. Suspend is a platform-lifecycle feature with its own device-quiesce, memory and wake
  contract; it is a separately approved item and is named as a prerequisite by anything that wants
  it, not smuggled in as a second verb on a button driver.
  This item is NOT closable today, and the previous version said it was on the strength of the FADT
  claim above.

- [ ] **ACPI battery, AC and thermal classes** (BLOCKED THREE TIMES: on the FIRMWARE-NODE IDENTITY
  prerequisite - `PNP0C0A`, `ACPI0003` and `THERMALZONE` are ACPI NAMESPACE devices, and the identity
  section above says in as many words that a namespace device is not expressible by the PCI-shaped
  binding identity this system has; that block was missing from this bullet and the rule says every
  consumer carries it in its own (added 2026-09-02) - on the AML prerequisite below, AND on
  the unowned PLATFORM POWER-STATE service - `system-power` is the narrow reboot/power-off interface
  and there is no typed battery, AC or thermal destination to publish to, so AML alone would leave
  this item with values it can read and nowhere to put them): publish typed battery/AC and
  thermal-zone state behind that service. `_BST`, `_BIF` and `_TMP` are
  CONTROL METHODS, and this kernel has no AML interpreter and no ACPI namespace at all: it reads
  `APIC`, `SRAT` and `SLIT` and nothing else. Validate package/object depth and event storms; keep
  fan curves and vendor power policy in Phase 5.
  SPLIT HERE RATHER THAN LEFT TO THE IMPLEMENTER (2026-08-30). This was one checkbox whose text told
  whoever picked it up to split it first - which is an instruction to do planning inside
  implementation, and it left a closable item welded to a blocked one.
  Validate package/object depth and event storms; keep board EC methods, fan curves and
  vendor power policy in Phase 5. FDT platforms use equivalent typed providers rather
  than pretending ACPI is universal across firmware ecosystems.
- [x] **High Definition Audio (HDA)** standard PCI controller: reset and enumerate
  codecs/function groups/widgets, build a minimal deterministic pin -> converter route,
  configure stream descriptor/BDL/rate/format and expose playback/capture through the
  same PCM contract as `virtio-snd` and USB Audio. Bound CORB/RIRB and unsolicited
  responses, isolate codec/controller failure, and leave vendor DSP firmware, jack
  policy and machine-specific routing quirks to Phase 5.

  **ITEM PLAN (2026-09-16), written before any code because the rule below says so.**

  CLASSIFICATION: `driver`, the fourth consumer of the plain-PCI resource profile and the first of
  them that is not storage. That matters for what it tests about the profile: the three before it all
  published `block`, so "a resourced plain-PCI function can serve a provider" had only ever been
  shown for one kind of provider.

  WHAT IT REQUIRES THAT ALREADY EXISTS: the same prerequisite set as the storage drivers, and the
  PCM contract `virtio-snd` already serves, published under `provider::AUDIO`. This item consumes
  that contract and defines none: the roadmap says in as many words that playback and capture go
  through the SAME contract as `virtio-snd` and USB Audio, so a second audio wire would be the block
  wire's mistake repeated in a different family.

  WHAT IT OWNS THAT DOES NOT YET EXIST: THE HDA COMMAND AND CODEC LAYER, and it is almost entirely
  decisions rather than register access, which is why most of it belongs in a host-tested module:
    - The CORB and RIRB rings: a command is a 32-bit verb and a response is a 64-bit pair, both in
      rings whose read and write pointers the controller and the driver each own half of. The
      arithmetic is the same shape as every other ring in this tree and is where this driver would be
      wrong in the same way the NVMe one was.
    - The VERB ENCODING: a codec address, a node id, a command and a payload packed into one word,
      with two payload widths depending on which command it is. A verb built with the wrong width
      addresses a different node with a different command and is answered, not refused.
    - THE WIDGET GRAPH WALK, which is the work the group-2 table warns about when it calls this item
      large. A codec reports function groups, each reports widgets, each widget reports a type and a
      connection list, and a route from a pin to a converter is a path through that. It is bounded
      here: the FIRST pin complex that reports an output-capable connection to an audio-output
      converter, with the walk bounded by the node counts the codec itself reports.
    - The stream format word and the buffer descriptor list.

  WHAT IT EXCLUDES: capture in the first slice, because a route to a converter is the same problem
  twice and playback is the one with an oracle; unsolicited responses beyond bounding them;
  multiple codecs beyond taking the first that answers; vendor DSP firmware, jack policy and
  machine-specific routing, which the item text puts in Phase 5; and MSI, because this driver polls
  like every other one here.

  TARGET DEVICE MODEL: QEMU `-device intel-hda` with an `hda-output` codec behind it.

  OBSERVABLE-EFFECT ORACLE: the convention rejects "the driver reached Online", and for audio it
  would be especially empty. The effect is that the CONTROLLER CONSUMED THE BUFFER: a stream is
  started over a known buffer descriptor list and the controller's own link position counter is read
  advancing past the bytes it was given, which is the hardware saying it played them. A codec that
  answered its identity verb with a real vendor id is the second half, because a route built on a
  codec that answered nothing would still start a stream that plays silence.

  MEASURABLE LIMITS: a bounded CORB/RIRB depth with the response wait typed rather than spun; the
  widget walk bounded by the codec's reported node counts and refused if they exceed what one page of
  descriptors can hold; one stream, one buffer descriptor list, one fragment.

  DEFINITION OF DONE FOR ITS CLASS: host tests over the verb encoding, the ring arithmetic, the
  format word and the graph walk with malformed codec answers; the QEMU model proving the governed
  process, the DeviceManager binding and the destination contract; the bind window recorded per
  target; registered in the verification model in the same change. Acceptance on aarch64 and riscv64
  is a separate measurement on each and this plan does not claim it.

  **WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-16): UP ON x86_64 WITH ITS ORACLE PASSING.**
  `driver.hda: online (00:0d.0, codec 0, converter 2)` - the controller out of reset, the command and
  response rings running, the codec found by its identity verb, its widget graph walked, an output
  converter and a pin complex that can drive one selected, and the PCM service published under the
  SAME provider kind `driver.virtio-snd` uses. 405 tests pass.

  THE ORACLE IS THE HARDWARE SAYING IT TOOK THE BYTES, which for audio is the only thing that means
  anything: a driver that brings a controller up, routes through a codec that answered nothing and
  starts a stream over an empty buffer reports exactly what a working one reports, and plays silence.
  So the harness hands it one period over the PCM contract and then reads the controller's OWN LINK
  POSITION COUNTER, requiring it to have advanced. Reaching READY is the other half and is a claim in
  itself: this driver refuses to report in without a codec that answered with a real vendor and a pin
  that reaches a converter.

  **WHAT IT TOOK WAS FOUR ROUNDS OF MEASUREMENT AND NO REASONING THAT SURVIVED.** Every wrong guess
  is listed because each one was plausible and each one cost a build:
    - The command ring's pointer reset. The specification requires the bit written as one, READ BACK
      AS ONE - that read is the confirmation - then written as zero and read as zero. Writing one and
      waiting for it to clear waits for something the controller never does: the register sat at
      0x8000 for ever. REAL, and fixed.
    - The ring size. Sixteen entries is OPTIONAL; only 256 is required of every controller, and one
      that declines sixteen leaves the driver wrapping at sixteen while it wraps at 256. Wrong, fixed,
      and NOT the cause.
    - A diagnostic that borrowed another failure's sentence and printed the wrong register beside it,
      which sent the reader to GCTL for a problem in CORBRP.
  WHAT IT ACTUALLY WAS, and only the pointers could say it: `CORBWP 2 CORBRP 1` - the controller had
  fetched exactly one command and stopped. `RINTCNT` is how many responses it writes before raising
  the response interrupt AND HALTING THE COMMAND RING until `RIRBSTS` is cleared. At one, which is
  what an interrupt-driven driver wants, a POLLING driver gets its first answer and then silence,
  with every other register looking correct. It is 255 now and the status is acknowledged after every
  response.

  **AND ONE DEFECT IT FOUND WAS NOT ABOUT AUDIO AT ALL**, which is the part worth carrying to every
  driver here. The waits were bounded in SPIN COUNTS, and a spin is not a time: one iteration is an
  MMIO read, tens of nanoseconds on silicon and a trap into the hypervisor under emulation, three
  orders of magnitude apart. Ten million of them outlasted the two-second bind window, so
  DeviceManager killed this driver for silence before it could say what it was waiting for - and
  outlasted the BOOT window too, bringing up nine of twenty-four services, which looked like adding
  one audio driver had broken the system. The machine already has the unit: 100 Hz everywhere, READY
  due 200 ticks after BIND, every driver here binding inside 44. Bring-up takes one deadline in ticks
  and every wait shares it, so a bring-up where everything times out still finishes in time to say so.

  **AND CAPTURE IS IN (2026-09-19) - AND GETTING THERE FOUND THAT THIS DRIVER WAS NOT SPEAKING THE
  CONTRACT AT ALL.**

  THE ITEM ASKS FOR PLAYBACK AND CAPTURE THROUGH THE SAME PCM CONTRACT AS `virtio-snd`, and that
  contract was three constants and a comment INSIDE `virtio_snd.rs`. This driver, the second server
  of the same `ProviderKind::Audio`, had never read it. Two things followed, and neither was visible
  from either driver alone:
  - A ONE-BYTE MESSAGE MEANT DIFFERENT THINGS TO THE TWO SERVERS. To `virtio-snd` it is a command -
    `CMD_CAPTURE` asks for a period - and to `hda` it was a period one byte long. A consumer asking
    this machine for a microphone got a click.
  - AND THIS DRIVER NEVER REPLIED TO A PERIOD AT ALL. AudioService marks the driver PENDING when it
    hands a period over and sends the next one only when the reply arrives, so on a machine whose
    audio provider is this one it played EXACTLY ONE PERIOD PER BOOT and then went quiet - with
    nothing anywhere reporting it, because a driver waiting for a message it will never be sent
    looks exactly like a driver with nothing to do.

  SO THE WIRE IS `driver_protocol::audio` NOW, beside the block and stream wires and for the reason
  that section already gives: a contract written inside its first driver is copied by its second.
  Except this one was not copied - it was ignored. The shapes are an ENUMERATION rather than a
  length comparison spelled out three times, `message()` names them, and both servers and the one
  consumer read it. THE LENGTH IS THE TAG and that is what makes the enumeration worth having: a
  period is exactly `PERIOD_BYTES`, an empty message ends playback, one byte is a command, and every
  other length is refused rather than played as a short period.

  THE CAPTURE ROUTE IS THE OUTPUT ROUTE WITH EVERY QUESTION REVERSED: an audio INPUT converter, a
  pin that can be driven INTO - pin capability bit FIVE, beside output's four - stream tag two so
  two converters do not share one descriptor, and pin control `0x20`, which is IN enable where
  `0x40` is OUT. A codec with no input is a machine with no microphone rather than a bring-up
  failure, and its capture commands are refused with the empty reply the wire defines.

  THE STREAM RUNS BETWEEN REQUESTS and periods are taken from alternate halves on the buffer's
  completion status - sticky, write-one-to-clear, so a reader that only read would hand back the
  same half for ever. Starting and stopping per period would drop whatever arrived while nobody was
  asking, which is a recording with holes wherever the consumer was busy.

  THE FIXTURE IS `hda-duplex` NOW, because `hda-output` has no input to route. The oracle asserts a
  period is ANSWERED for playback - the reply this driver never sent - and for capture that a whole
  period comes back rather than the wire's empty refusal, AND that the INPUT stream's link position
  advanced, which is the device saying it filled the buffer rather than the driver handing back its
  own zeros. Dropping the capture converter fails it with 0 against 2048.

  AND WHAT THE FIXTURE CANNOT SEE IS WRITTEN INTO THE TEST rather than left to be assumed: telling
  the input pin to DRIVE instead of to listen - the one bit that decides which way a jack faces -
  passes every assertion, because QEMU's codec model runs the stream either way. On real silicon
  that is a microphone that records nothing.

  **AND THE SECOND CODEC IS IN (2026-09-19), AND IT FOUND A DEFECT THAT ONE CODEC WAS HIDING.**

  THE HARNESS HAD NEVER PRESENTED TWO. With one codec on the link, "take the first that answers" and
  "walk the codecs until one has a route" are the same code, so this driver did the first and the
  open point could not be closed by reading it. `hda-micro` now sits beside `hda-duplex` on the same
  controller, and the online line says `codec 0 of 2`: which one is driven, and how many answered.
  Those are different facts and the log said neither.

  THE DRIVER TRIES EACH CODEC IN TURN. A codec that answers its identity and then has no audio
  function group, no output converter or no pin that can drive one is not a bring-up failure while
  another codec on the same link is fine - it is the first of several, and stopping there is how a
  machine with a modem codec at address zero ends up with no sound. The refusal reported when none
  of them works is the LAST one's, because returning the first would name a codec the driver went on
  to look past.

  AND THE DEFECT: THE RIRB READ POINTER WAS RESET WHEN SWITCHING CODECS. The response ring is ONE
  ring shared by every codec on the link, and that pointer is where the driver has read up to in it,
  compared against the controller's write pointer. Putting it back to zero makes the driver re-read
  entries it has already consumed and take an old answer for a new one. It was harmless for exactly
  as long as one codec was probed and the loop broke out of it - and the first two-codec boot failed
  with `no pin complex reaches an audio output converter`, which is what a widget walk reading stale
  answers looks like from outside. That run IS the mutation: the reset put back, the bring-up gone.

  AND THE LAST OPEN POINT IS A WALL WITH A CHECK UNDER IT (2026-09-19). Unsolicited responses are
  raised by a codec when something changes that nobody asked about - in practice a jack going in or
  out. `-device hda-duplex,help` on the pinned QEMU offers `audiodev`, `cad`, `debug`, `mixer` and
  `use-timer`: no jack presence, and no runtime control that changes one. The event cannot occur, so
  the path beyond bounding them cannot be exercised. The bounding itself is in and is what keeps a
  codec that sent one from desynchronising the response ring.

  THE BIND WINDOW IS MEASURED - 71 ticks, in the table in this file's head.

  AND THE PORTS WERE MEASURED AGAIN WITH THE CODEC WALK IN THEM (2026-09-19), because the earlier
  three-target run was against a driver that stopped at the first codec and reset the response ring's
  read pointer to get there. `drivers,pci,slow,usb` passes on aarch64 (100 tests, 1513 s) and riscv64
  (102, 1822 s), and both report `codec 0 of 2` - two codecs answering on a link whose controller and
  PCI enumeration have nothing in common with x86_64's. NOTHING IN THIS ITEM IS OPEN NOW.

  AND THE TWO EMULATED TARGETS ARE IN (2026-09-19). The driver suites pass on
  aarch64 (100 tests, 1539 s) and riscv64 (102 tests, 2097 s) under `drivers,pci,slow`, each reporting `driver.hda: online (..., codec 0,
  converter 2)` with the capture path built in the same boot.

## 3. USB device classes and the remainder

Most of these sit on the xHCI stack that already exists, so each is a class driver rather than new
controller machinery, which is why the group follows the controllers. HID over I2C is the exception
and is here for its class rather than its transport: it reuses the HID parser and reaches its device
over a firmware-described I2C controller, so it is also the first item in this group that needs the
firmware identity named above. Ordered by what a person sitting at the machine notices, with the
mutating and niche transports last.

- [x] **THE USB EXECUTION MODEL IS DECIDED ONCE, BEFORE THE FIRST USB ITEM IN THIS GROUP** - and
  "USB" is the word that matters, because HID over I2C is in this group and does not wait on it. It
  attaches to no xHCI controller, holds no USB claim, and neither of the two answers below is about
  anything it does; gating it on this decision would hold a non-USB driver behind a USB one for no
  reason but its position in a list. Today HID and mass storage are MODULES INSIDE the xHCI
  process, and the controller holds a single
  `Option<(UsbDevice, Storage)>` - one storage device, in one Domain, with one claim. Every bullet
  below asks for a registry entry, a claim, a transactional bind and DeviceManager binding per
  driver, and neither shape gives it that. Two answers, and this item is choosing between them:
  class drivers stay inside the xHCI Domain, in which case the per-driver registry, claim and bind
  requirements do not apply to them and this document says so; or one USB-interface binding unit
  exists, with endpoint capabilities, its own generation and composite devices, in which case it is
  a SUB-FUNCTION IDENTITY, which is the same cross-cutting case as the firmware node and is
  therefore NOT owned by a class driver either (corrected 2026-08-30: this said the first implemented
  USB-class item owns it, while the identity section above expressly removes sub-function identity
  from driver ownership - the fixed-name rule applied to the one class of contract this file says
  must not have it). If the second answer is chosen, the USB-interface binding unit is an UNOWNED
  BLOCKED PREREQUISITE on the same terms as the firmware node, and every USB class item is blocked
  until it is numbered. That cost is exactly why the first answer exists.
  P02M0163 refused the second as "splitting a driver into several and giving each its own authority"
  and said the milestone that needs it builds it. This is that milestone, and the choice is its first
  item rather than a thing each class driver rediscovers.

  **AND IF THE FIRST ANSWER IS CHOSEN, THE CLASS MODULE NEEDS ITS OWN CLASSIFICATION AND ITS OWN
  DEFINITION OF DONE** (added 2026-08-30). The head classifies an item as a `driver` when it
  independently claims and binds a device, and a `shared library` when it has no device and no
  binding. An in-controller class module is NEITHER: it touches a real device and it does not claim
  one, so under the current classification it either inherits P02M0098/P02M0161/P02M0162 gates it
  cannot satisfy or falls through to a parser's gates that prove nothing about a device. Both
  readings were available, which is how a USB item could have closed against whichever it preferred.

  So the head's classification gains a fifth kind, `in-controller class module`, and it closes on a
  contract of its own:

    authority     the module holds NO claim and NO Domain of its own; it operates strictly through
                    the controller's claim and the controller's resources, and the item states which
                    controller operations it may invoke and which it may not
    isolation     a bound per-class resource budget inside the controller's Domain - endpoints,
                    buffers, in-flight transfers - so one class module cannot starve another or the
                    controller, with a typed refusal at the bound
    interface     ONE named owner for the controller-to-class-module interface, which is the
                    controller item, not each class module; a class module that needs a new
                    controller operation extends that interface rather than reaching around it
    lifecycle     attach, detach and per-class teardown driven BY THE CONTROLLER, replacing the
                    independent bind/rollback a driver would have. Detach releases the class
                    module's resources and leaves the controller serving; controller teardown tears
                    down every module with it

  **DECIDED 2026-09-13: THE FIRST ANSWER. A USB class driver in this system is a MODULE INSIDE THE
  CONTROLLER'S PROCESS AND DOMAIN.** It holds no claim, binds no device and has no Domain of its own,
  and it reaches the bus only through the controller that claimed it. The per-driver registry entry,
  claim and transactional bind that a DRIVER owes therefore do not apply to a class module, and this
  document says so here rather than leaving each USB item to decide.
  WHY THIS ANSWER AND NOT THE OTHER. The second makes a USB interface a binding unit with its own
  endpoint capabilities, its own generation and composite devices - which is a SUB-FUNCTION IDENTITY,
  the same cross-cutting case as the firmware node, owned by no class driver. P02M0163 refused it once
  already as "splitting a driver into several and giving each its own authority", and choosing it here
  would put every USB class item behind an unowned prerequisite. The cost of the second answer is
  exactly why the first exists, and nothing in the class items needs what the second buys.

  AND THE MECHANISM EXISTS, which is the other half of what an architectural prerequisite closes on.

    REQUIRES   the xHCI controller and its two class modules, which is what there is to isolate
    OWNS       `drivers::usb_class` - the execution model written down, the per-class budget and its
                 typed refusal - and its wiring in `xhci.rs` and `usb_storage.rs`
    GATE       `host.drivers`, 8 new tests at each ceiling and one past it, plus the guest `usb` and
                 `storage` tags (59 tests) over the real controller

  WHAT A MODULE MAY INVOKE IS NOW A LIST AND NOT AN UNDERSTANDING: address and configure a device,
  bring an endpoint up and take it down, queue a transfer on a ring it was given, wait for a
  completion, read a descriptor. It may NOT touch the controller's registers, allocate from the Domain
  directly, enable or disable a slot, or reach any device other than the one it was attached to. A
  module that needs an operation the list does not have extends the interface in the CONTROLLER, which
  is one place and one owner.
  THE ISOLATION IS A NUMBER PER CLASS, CHARGED ON ATTACH AND GIVEN BACK ON DETACH. It was not
  hypothetical: the HID module's device list was an unbounded `Vec` that a tier of hubs full of
  keyboards fills, each one taking an endpoint and a DMA page of the controller's, and the
  mass-storage module's data buffer grew to the largest request anybody ever made and never shrank -
  inside the controller's Domain, for the life of the process. Eight HID devices with eight endpoints,
  eight rings and eight reports in flight; one mass-storage device with two endpoints, two rings and a
  data buffer bounded at one megabyte, which is the number `fit_data` now refuses past.
  THE REFUSAL IS TYPED AND SAYS WHICH CEILING IT REACHED, because "the keyboard budget is full" and
  "this controller serves one disk" are different things to tell somebody with a cable in their hand.
  A refused device is left ADDRESSED and in the inventory rather than released, so a later detach still
  gives its pages back - and a refused admission charges nothing, which the tests check by refusing
  sixteen times and requiring the usage to be where it was.
  THE LIFECYCLE IS THE CONTROLLER'S. It admits before a module takes a device and releases when the
  port goes away, in the same two places that already allocate and free the pages - so the budget
  cannot drift from the memory, and a hundred plug/unplug cycles end where they started.
    gate          the observable effect through the destination service, plus a detach-under-load
                    case and a per-class budget-exhaustion refusal - the two things an independent
                    bind would otherwise have proved

  The prerequisite matrix reads that classification: an in-controller class module is NOT on the
  every-driver row, and it does NOT separately need P02M0172 - the CONTROLLER's declared DMA policy
  covers every transfer it performs, which is precisely what makes the module not a driver. If the
  second answer is chosen instead, class drivers ARE drivers and every row applies to them
  unchanged. Each USB item below states which model it was written against, and the models are not
  mixed within one item.

- [ ] **Generic USB HID expansion** beyond the implemented keyboard and pointer paths:
  extend the shared report-descriptor parser and InputService vocabulary for gamepads,
  joysticks, consumer controls, tablets and multi-touch collections. Preserve usage
  page/id, logical ranges, units, contact identity and simultaneous controls instead of
  flattening every device into keys or a mouse. Bound report sizes, collection depth,
  contact count and malformed descriptors; existing keyboard/pointer behavior must not
  regress.

  **ITEM PLAN (2026-09-18), written before any code because the rule above says so.**

  CLASSIFICATION: `in-controller class module` for the transport half, which already exists and is
  bound; what this item adds is in `drivers::hid`, a SHARED LIBRARY that closes on host tests and
  binds nothing, plus the InputService vocabulary it feeds.

  WHAT IT REQUIRES THAT ALREADY EXISTS: `drivers::hid`'s report-descriptor parser, which decodes
  segments with their page, usages, logical bounds and report ids; the xHCI HID module that posts
  reports and diffs them; InputService's key stream and pointer ring.

  **WHAT IS ACTUALLY WRONG TODAY, AND IT IS ONE LINE.** The parser keeps a segment only when its
  usage page is one of FOUR - generic desktop, keyboard, button, consumer - and drops every other
  one before it is ever decoded. A digitizer reports on the DIGITIZER page, a gamepad's hat and axes
  on the game and simulation pages, and every one of those fields is discarded at parse time. That is
  what "flattening every device into keys or a mouse" IS in this tree: not a lossy mapping downstream,
  but a filter upstream that never let the fields exist.

  AND THE BOUNDS THE ITEM ASKS FOR ARE NOT THERE EITHER. Collection depth is not tracked at all -
  `Collection` and `End Collection` are main items the parser passes over - so a descriptor that
  opens collections and never closes them is not refused by anything; and `Push` grows a `Vec` with
  no ceiling, which is the same unbounded growth from the same input. Both are DEVICE-SUPPLIED
  numbers, which is what makes them limits rather than style.

  WHAT IT OWNS:
    - THE PAGES A SEGMENT MAY BE ON, as a stated set rather than a filter nobody can see from
      outside. Digitizer for tablets and touch, game and simulation for gamepads and joysticks.
    - COLLECTION DEPTH, tracked and BOUNDED, with the depth on each segment - because contact
      identity in a multi-touch report is expressed by COLLECTION: each finger is its own collection
      containing a contact identifier, a tip switch and a pair of axes, and a parser that flattens
      them cannot tell the second finger's X from the first's.
    - THE UNIT AND ITS EXPONENT, which the item names and which nothing reads today. A tablet
      reports absolute position in a unit; a driver that ignores it publishes counts as if they were
      pixels.
    - CONTACT IDENTITY AND COUNT as things a consumer can ASK for, so a report with three fingers is
      three contacts rather than one pointer that jumps.

  WHAT IT EXCLUDES, so the item cannot grow: no force feedback, no output reports, no per-device
  quirk table, and no change to what the keyboard and pointer paths already produce - which is the
  item's own regression bar and is asserted rather than assumed.

  OBSERVABLE-EFFECT ORACLE: the existing keyboard and pointer oracles must pass unchanged, and a
  digitizer descriptor must decode into contacts with their identifiers - measured on the parser,
  where every input is bytes a device chose.

  WHAT THIS PLAN DOES NOT CLAIM: acceptance on aarch64 and riscv64 is a separate measurement on
  each.

  **WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-18): THE PARSER HALF IS DONE AND GATED. THE VOCABULARY HALF IS NOT.**

  THE PAGES ARE STATED AND THE DIGITIZER IS AMONG THEM, with simulation and game beside it for
  joysticks and gamepads. Six host tests and six mutations.

  CONTACTS ARE DECODED AS CONTACTS: `Layout::contacts` walks the report IN BIT ORDER - what groups a
  finger's fields is where they sit, not the order a descriptor declared them in - and begins a new
  one at each CONTACT IDENTIFIER, which is how a multi-touch report says where one finger ends and
  the next begins. `CONTACT COUNT` bounds what is reported, because a digitizer declares slots for
  every finger it can ever carry and leaves the unused ones holding whatever was there before: a
  reader that takes every declared slot reports phantom fingers at stale positions that nothing is
  touching. And a count larger than the report carries does not invent one.

  COLLECTION DEPTH IS COUNTED AND BOUNDED, which it was not: `Collection` and `End Collection` were
  main items the parser passed over, so a descriptor that opens them and never closes them nested
  without limit and nothing refused it. The `Push` stack was an unbounded `Vec` fed from the same
  input and is bounded now too. Both numbers come from the device, which is what makes them limits.

  THE UNIT AND ITS EXPONENT ARE READ AND READABLE. The exponent is a FOUR-BIT SIGNED NIBBLE - 0x0F is
  minus one, not fifteen - so a driver reading it as a byte scales a tablet's position by ten to the
  fifteenth instead of dividing it by ten, and the number is small either way.

  **AND IT FOUND A LIVE DEFECT IN THE POINTER PATH THAT HAD NOTHING TO DO WITH DIGITIZERS.** An
  absolute axis was read SIGN-EXTENDED whatever its descriptor declared. An eight-bit axis over
  `0..255` reports 0xF0 for a touch near the right-hand edge; sign-extended that is MINUS SIXTEEN,
  and `scale` clamps it to zero - so the right-hand half of such a surface reads as the left edge,
  with nothing refusing anything anywhere. It survived because the only absolute pointer this harness
  attaches declares sixteen-bit axes over `0..0x7fff`, whose high bit is never set; the first
  eight-bit digitizer would have found it on hardware, as a tablet whose right half does not work.
  The parser already made this distinction for the logical MAXIMUM - `logical_max_raw` exists because
  a maximum that parses negative under a non-negative minimum was meant unsigned - and the same rule
  now decides how the field itself is read. A RELATIVE axis stays signed whatever its minimum says,
  because what it carries is a delta and a delta with no sign is a mouse that only moves right.

  THE REGRESSION BAR IS ASSERTED RATHER THAN ASSUMED: the boot keyboard's own descriptor parses to a
  keyboard and not a pointer or a digitizer, and its modifier bitmap and key array still diff into
  the same usages. 55 tests pass over `input,mouse,drivers,slow`.

  **AND THE VOCABULARY HALF IS IN TOO (2026-09-18).**

  `liber:input@1` GAINED A `contact-event` AND A `subscribe-contacts` OP, focus-gated on the same
  one-shot proof `subscribe-keys` takes and for the same reason: a touch is input, and input belongs
  to whoever owns the display.

  NORMALISED AXES AND NOT CELLS, which is the one place this package's events differ in shape - and
  the decision is written where the record is. A console cursor IS a cell, which is why a pointer's
  canonical form is the grid; a finger is not. A cell is sixteen pixels wide, a touch moves within
  one, and an interface that can only say which cell a finger is in cannot express a drag at all. A
  consumer that wants cells divides, which it can; a consumer handed cells cannot multiply back.

  AND A TOUCH SURFACE IS ITS OWN PROVIDER KIND. `ProviderKind::Touch` sits beside `Input` and
  `Pointer` rather than reusing either, for the reason `local-stream` sits beside `net`: a consumer
  of `pointer` is handed ONE cursor, and a surface published as one would be flattened into whichever
  contact was decoded last. InputService discovers it through the catalogue like the other two.

  FOCUS LOSS RELEASES A FINGER THE WAY IT RELEASES A HELD KEY. The service tracks which contacts are
  down and lifts them before the stream closes, so a new foreground application cannot inherit a
  touch it never saw begin - the contact half of what `close_key_stream` already did for keys.

  `kernel.services.a_touch_surface_reports_contacts_and_not_a_cursor` stands in for the driver, as
  every other input test here does: two fingers down at once keep their own identifiers and their own
  positions, one lifts and the other does not, a forged proof opens nothing, and focus loss releases
  the one still down and closes the stream. Gates `declared-interfaces`, `provider-routing`,
  `no-fixed-provider-slots` and `source-hygiene` pass with it in.

  WHAT IS STILL OPEN: the DRIVER SIDE of the touch provider - the xHCI HID module publishes no
  `Touch` yet, because the parser half landed first and there is no multi-touch device model in this
  harness to feed it - and the gamepad and joystick AXIS MAPPING, which the vocabulary now makes
  expressible. Both are waiting on a device to bind against, not on an implementer.

  THE PORTS ARE IN (2026-09-19): the driver suites pass on
  aarch64 (100 tests, 1539 s) and riscv64 (102 tests, 2097 s) under `drivers,pci,slow`, with the keyboard and pointer paths this item
  extends reported on both.

  AND THE AXIS MAPPING HAS THE SAME WALL AS THE TOUCH PROVIDER, WHICH THIS NOTE DID NOT SAY
  (measured 2026-09-19). The USB HID devices the pinned QEMU registers are `usb-kbd`, `usb-mouse`,
  `usb-tablet` and `usb-wacom-tablet` - a keyboard, two pointers, and a "PenPartner Tablet" whose
  HID mode presents a MOUSE report descriptor. There is no gamepad, no joystick and no multi-touch
  collection to bind, so a driver-side mapping for them would bind nothing on every machine in this
  harness - which is what the note beside NCM already calls a claim rather than a driver. The
  PARSER half is where the work went and it is held by fixtures; what both of these need is a device
  model to write, not a driver.
- [ ] **USB CDC-ACM** serial class: bind communication/data interfaces and alternate
  settings, parse functional descriptors, serve bounded RX/TX with line-coding and
  control-line operations, propagate disconnects, stalls and short packets, and define
  one typed serial/byte-stream contract reusable by console tools, MCU links and modems.
  Exercise composite devices and several simultaneous adapters; do not expose a global
  ambient `/dev/tty*` namespace.

  **ITEM PLAN (2026-09-18), written before any code because the rule above says so.**

  CLASSIFICATION: `in-controller class module`, on the model the USB section states - a module inside
  the controller's process, holding no claim and reaching the bus only through it, under
  `drivers::usb_class`'s per-class budget. It creates NO shared contract: the byte stream it
  publishes is `ConsoleBytes` over `drivers::serial_port`, which virtio-serial multiport defined and
  which this item's own text is told not to decide again.

  WHAT IT REQUIRES THAT ALREADY EXISTS: the xHCI controller's transfer rings, endpoint configuration
  and class binding; `drivers::descriptor` for the walk; `drivers::cdc` for the communications-class
  plumbing - the union descriptor, the data interface and the alternate setting its endpoints live in
  - which the ECM and NCM models already share; `drivers::serial_port` for the byte stream and its
  bounded buffering.

  WHAT IT OWNS: the ACM half of `drivers::cdc`, and it is four decisions rather than a parser.
    - WHICH DATA INTERFACE, which is the union descriptor's answer and not "the next one". On a
      composite device - a modem that is also a storage device, an MCU that is also a HID - the
      interface after the communications one belongs to something else, and binding it takes a bulk
      pair that another function is using. The ECM path already answers this; ACM asks the same
      question of a different subclass.
    - WHAT THE DEVICE ACTUALLY SUPPORTS, from the ACM functional descriptor's capability bitmap.
      `SET_LINE_CODING` to a device that does not implement it STALLS, and a driver that treats that
      stall as fatal throws away a byte stream that works - which is most MCU links, where the line
      coding is meaningless because there is no UART behind the USB.
    - THE LINE-CODING STRUCTURE, whose seven bytes use TWO DIFFERENT CONVENTIONS side by side. The
      rate is a little-endian u32; the data bits are a COUNT; and the stop bits are an ENUMERATION
      where 0 is one stop bit, 1 is one and a half and 2 is two. A driver that writes the number it
      means into the stop-bits byte asks for one and a half stop bits when it means one - which some
      devices refuse and others accept, quietly framing every byte differently.
    - AND THE CONTROL LINES ARE A BITMAP IN wValue, not a payload: DTR is bit 0 and RTS is bit 1, and
      the request carries no data stage at all. A driver that sends them as bytes sends a request
      with a length the device did not expect.

  WHAT IT EXCLUDES, so the item cannot grow: the typed SERIAL layer - line coding and control lines
  as an INTERFACE other programs call - which the 16550 item says is owned by the first of the three
  claimants that actually needs it. This item implements the requests against the device and
  publishes a byte stream; it does not define a second provider kind. No modem commands, no
  `/dev/tty*` namespace, and no flow control beyond what the byte stream's own bounded buffering
  already does.

  OBSERVABLE-EFFECT ORACLE: bytes written into the published `console-bytes` provider come back
  through it, off a host process on the other end of the adapter - the same shape as the vsock
  oracle, and for the same reason: a device whose peer is the HOST proves nothing by being brought
  up. "The module reached its class binding" is not accepted.

  MEASURABLE LIMITS: the per-class budget `drivers::usb_class` already bounds; a bounded
  notification buffer; short packets and a zero-length packet treated as the end of a transfer
  rather than as an error; a stalled control request recovered from rather than fatal.

  WHAT THIS PLAN DOES NOT CLAIM: acceptance on aarch64 and riscv64 is a separate measurement on
  each.

  **WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-18): THE DECISIONS ARE DONE AND GATED; THE BINDING HAS NOTHING TO BIND.**

  IN `drivers::cdc`, HOST-TESTED AND MUTATED: `bind_acm` over the ACM subclass, the line-coding
  structure both ways, the capability bitmap and the control-line word. Seven tests and FOUR
  mutations, each named for the mistake it plants - the union's data interface against "the next
  one", one stop bit encoded as one instead of zero, a stop-bits value outside the enumeration
  rounded rather than refused, and the line-coding capability read as "any bit set". `ClassKind` and
  the per-class budget carry a `Serial` entry, sized like the network adapter's with one more
  endpoint and no frame buffer, and bounded at TWO devices because the item asks for several
  simultaneous adapters and two is what makes "several" a case the budget has a refusal for.

  AND LANDING IT REMOVED A SECOND COPY OF A RULE. "The lowest alternate setting that carries a bulk
  pair wins" existed once for the network models; the ACM binding needed the same rule, and two
  copies of it is how the two come to disagree about a device that offers three settings. It is one
  function now, used by both, and the mutation gate points at that one.

  **THE BINDING PATH IS NOT WRITTEN, AND THAT IS THE RULE THIS TREE ALREADY STATES.** The note beside
  NCM in `usb_net.rs` says it in as many words: "a binding path nothing executes is a claim rather
  than a driver". There is no CDC-ACM device model in this harness to bind against. Checked rather
  than assumed, against the QEMU this tree pins: the USB devices it registers are `usb-net`,
  `usb-uas`, `usb-bot`, `usb-storage`, `usb-mtp`, `usb-ccid`, `usb-kbd`, `usb-mouse`, `usb-tablet`,
  `usb-wacom-tablet`, `usb-audio`, `usb-braille`, `usb-redir`, `u2f-passthru` and `usb-serial` - and
  `usb-serial` is an FTDI adapter, vendor class 0xFF with a vendor protocol, NOT a communications-class
  device. A CDC-ACM module written against it would bind nothing on every machine in this harness.

  WHAT WOULD CLOSE IT: a CDC-ACM gadget in the harness - which is a device model to write, not a
  driver - or real hardware. The decisions are in and gated either way, so what is left is the
  transport and its oracle rather than the parsing, which is the half that is silent when wrong.
- [x] **USB Attached SCSI (UAS)**: reuse the bounded SCSI command/sense layer from BOT
  while implementing UAS command/status/data pipes, stream IDs, tagged concurrency,
  task management, timeout and endpoint recovery. Expose the same block contract and
  retain BOT as a negotiated fallback for devices that offer both. Cap outstanding
  commands and reject duplicate/unknown tags or inconsistent residue deterministically.

  **ITEM PLAN (2026-09-17), written before any code because the rule below says so.**

  CLASSIFICATION: `in-controller class module`, which closes on the contract in the USB section -
  controller-driven lifecycle, a bounded per-class resource budget and an observable effect - and
  never on an independent bind. It creates NO shared contract: the SCSI core the item names as the
  thing to reuse was extracted by `virtio-scsi` and the USB BOT path already sits on it, so this is
  the third consumer of a contract that exists.

  WHAT IT REQUIRES THAT ALREADY EXISTS: `drivers::scsi` for the commands and the sense; the block
  wire; the xHCI controller's transfer rings, endpoint configuration and class binding, which the BOT
  path already drives; and `drivers::usb_class`'s budget, which is what bounds a class module inside
  a controller's Domain.

  WHAT IT OWNS: the UAS PIPE MODEL, which is the whole of the difference from BOT and is where this
  driver would be wrong. BOT is one command, one data stage, one status, strictly in order. UAS is
  FOUR pipes - command, status, data-in, data-out - with a tag joining them, and the device may answer
  them in any order. That turns three questions into decisions worth testing on the host:
    - THE INFORMATION UNIT HEADER, whose first byte is the kind and whose tag is BIG-ENDIAN in bytes
      two and three. A tag byte-swapped is a tag that matches nothing, and the answer is then a status
      for a command nobody is waiting for - which is indistinguishable from a device fault unless the
      tag is decoded correctly.
    - WHICH INFORMATION UNIT A RESPONSE IS, because a sense IU and a response IU arrive on the same
      pipe and mean different things: one carries a status and sense for a command, the other the
      answer to a task-management request.
    - TAG ZERO IS RESERVED AND MUST NEVER BE ISSUED, which is the same rule the NVMe driver keeps for
      its command ids and for the same reason: it makes an unmatched answer distinguishable from a
      half-arrived one.

  WHAT IT EXCLUDES: streams, which are the xHCI mechanism UAS uses for concurrency and which this
  tree's xHCI driver does not implement - so the first slice is ONE COMMAND OUTSTANDING on a
  stream-less device, which is what `usb-uas` offers when streams are not negotiated; task management
  beyond refusing what it cannot do; and BOT fallback selection, because the two are separate
  interfaces on the device and the item asks for BOT to be RETAINED, which it is - it is still the
  path `usb_storage.rs` drives.
  THE FIRST TWO EXCLUSIONS ARE SPENT (2026-09-19): the controller grew bulk streams when this driver
  was written, and the per-stream rings and task management that the first slice left out are in. BOT
  retention is unchanged and is still what satisfies that clause.

  TARGET DEVICE MODEL: QEMU `-device usb-uas` with a `scsi-hd` behind it, on the xHCI the harness
  already attaches.

  OBSERVABLE-EFFECT ORACLE: the same one every storage driver here answers to - a capacity from the
  target, a pattern written to a block and read back and COMPARED, and a range past the end refused
  with the typed status.

  MEASURABLE LIMITS: one command outstanding; tags bounded and never zero; an unknown or duplicate tag
  refused rather than matched to the nearest outstanding command; a residue inconsistent with what was
  asked for treated as a failure rather than as a short answer.
  SUPERSEDED IN ITS FIRST CLAUSE (2026-09-19): the cap is now the number of command tags rather than
  one, and the plan's exclusion of streams above went with it. The other three limits stand unchanged
  and are what the tag rules in `drivers::uas` enforce.

  DEFINITION OF DONE FOR ITS CLASS: host tests over the information-unit encoding and decoding with
  malformed units, and a mutation for each; the QEMU model proving the controller's bind, the class
  module's own budget and the block contract; and acceptance on aarch64 and riscv64 as a separate
  measurement on each, which this plan does not claim.

  **WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-17): THE INFORMATION UNITS ARE DONE; THE TRANSPORT IS NOT STARTED.**
  `drivers::uas` is the command unit, the answer decode, the tag rules and the residue check, with
  nine host tests and two mutations. The SCSI half of this item needed no work at all, which is the
  return on the extraction `virtio-scsi` did: `drivers::scsi` is shared and this driver consumes it
  unchanged, so what is left here is only what UAS adds to BOT.
  WHAT THE TESTS HOLD is the difference between the two transports: BOT is one command, one data
  stage and one status strictly in order, and UAS is four pipes joined by a TAG that the device may
  answer in any order. The tag is BIG-ENDIAN in a transport that is little-endian everywhere else, so
  a swapped one matches no answer - and an answer matching nothing is indistinguishable from a device
  fault. A sense unit and a response unit arrive on the same pipe and mean different things. A READY
  unit is not a completion, and a driver treating one as a status reports a transfer finished before a
  byte has moved. Tag zero is reserved and never issued, for the same reason the NVMe driver reserves
  command id zero. And a residue larger than the request is refused rather than subtracted, because
  subtracting it computes a negative length as an enormous positive one.

  WHY THE TRANSPORT IS NOT STARTED, AND IT IS A RISK RATHER THAN AN OMISSION. UAS's concurrency is
  xHCI STREAMS, which this tree's xHCI driver does not implement - the plan above excludes them and
  says the first slice is one command outstanding on a device that has not negotiated them. Whether
  QEMU's `usb-uas` offers that shape depends on the speed it enumerates at, and finding out costs a
  controller-side implementation before the answer is known. It is written down here rather than
  discovered by whoever starts it: the cheap check is to attach the device and read the endpoints'
  pipe-usage and companion descriptors BEFORE writing the transfer path.

  **THAT CHECK IS DONE (measured 2026-09-17), AND THE ANSWER SENT THE WORK INTO THE CONTROLLER.** The
  device is attached and the controller reads it. What the probe reports is:

      driver.xhci: UAS interface in configuration 1, alternate 0
      driver.xhci:   pipe command  on endpoint 01, packet 1024   companion: no streams
      driver.xhci:   pipe status   on endpoint 82, packet 1024   companion: streams 2^4
      driver.xhci:   pipe data-in  on endpoint 83, packet 1024   companion: streams 2^4
      driver.xhci:   pipe data-out on endpoint 04, packet 1024   companion: streams 2^4
      driver.xhci:   this device enumerated at SuperSpeed and carries endpoint companions

  So the four pipes are there and named exactly as the information units expect - and the three that
  carry a transfer each advertise SIXTEEN STREAMS. The command pipe does not, which is the
  specification being consistent rather than a reprieve: commands are serialised by the tag and the
  answers are what streams multiplex.

  AND THE OBVIOUS WAY TO GET THE OTHER SHAPE DOES NOT EXIST HERE. A UAS device at HIGH speed has no
  endpoint companions and therefore no streams to demand, which is the one command at a time this
  controller could drive. The way to force high speed is to put the device behind a high-speed hub;
  QEMU's `usb-hub` is a FULL-speed hub and refuses a high-or-super-speed device outright - measured,
  as a startup error: `speed mismatch trying to attach usb device "USB Attached SCSI HBA"
  (high+super speed) to bus "usb.0", port "1.4" (full speed)`. There is no other high-speed path to a
  UAS device in this harness.

  SO THE REMAINING WORK WAS xHCI BULK STREAMS, AND IT IS DONE. The stream context array, `MaxPStreams`
  and `LSA` in the endpoint context, and the stream id in the doorbell - about eighty lines in the
  controller, written as a structure of their own rather than as a branch inside the existing bulk
  path, so nothing that four other things depend on changed shape.

  **WHERE IT STANDS (2026-09-17): THE TRANSPORT IS UP ON x86_64 AND ITS ORACLE MOVES BYTES.**
  `driver.xhci: UAS target ready after 1 attempt(s), 4194304 bytes over 16 stream(s)` and
  `driver.xhci: online (00:05.0, 6 device(s)) (keyboard) (pointer) (storage) (network) (uas)`. The
  oracle writes a position-dependent sector at LBA 0x0102 through the UAS pipes and reads it back
  byte for byte.

  ONE COMMAND AT A TIME, UNDER ONE TAG, ON ONE STREAM, which is the slice the plan names - and the
  three are the same fact rather than three decisions: UAS requires the stream id to BE the task tag,
  so one outstanding command is one stream. The array is full size because the hardware fixes its
  length from `MaxPStreams`; the entries nothing selects are never read.

  AND THE CONTROLLER NOW PUBLISHES TWO BLOCK PROVIDERS, which is the first time anything in this tree
  has published two of one kind from one binding. A Bulk-Only stick and a UAS disk are two devices
