  queue/transfer limits, throughput, latency, peak memory and recovery times.
  AND FOR A CONTROLLER THAT IS SUPPOSED TO HOST THE SYSTEM VOLUME, THAT GATE IS NOT ENOUGH
  (added 2026-08-30). Group 2 is justified as the hardware a real machine needs before anything can
  be installed on it, and this gate proves only a SECONDARY disk: format, mount, read/write, flush
  and reopen through StorageService all pass while the machine still boots from virtio-blk. The
  staging problem that hides is real - the sole bootstrap block driver is pinned and boot-critical,
  DeviceManager cannot load volume-stage drivers until the bootstrap driver has already mounted the
  volume, and the manifest refuses a boot-critical driver stored on the volume it must make
  accessible. So every controller eligible to host `vol://system` - NVMe and AHCI today -
  additionally states its pinned/boot-critical staging, its deterministic boot-volume selection and
  its fallback behaviour, and closes on an END-TO-END BOOT whose system volume is on THAT controller.
  The secondary-disk test stays as coverage; it is not the integration proof.
- **THIS MILESTONE HAS NO COMPLETION CONDITION, BY DECISION** (2026-08-30). The head already says
  it is a cross-phase roadmap and not one piece of work, and a "Done when: every item is done" line
  underneath contradicted that in the only place a reader looks for the answer - and it applied one
  driver-shaped gate to parsers, kernel infrastructure and architectural prerequisites alike. It is
  therefore a NON-COMPLETABLE INDEX: it is never ticked, it never appears in a phase's completion
  report, and what closes is an ITEM. What follows is the per-item standard, not a milestone gate.
- An ITEM is done when it has the bounded contract and implementation scope
  its bullet describes; each binds by public standards rather than product whitelists; each survives
  hostile descriptors, hostile completions and REMOVAL AS ITS GROUP CAN SEE IT without escaping its
  Domain - a driver exiting, a channel dropping, a child disappearing from a bus that has one, which
  is what exists today; DEVICE removal is the PCIe-hotplug item's to build and only items after it
  can be asked for it; each releases its
  resources ON A CONFIRMED TEARDOWN and leaves them CHARGED AND QUARANTINED where the teardown was
  not confirmed, with no provider of that binding usable either way; and each passes the available
  QEMU/fixture plus tri-architecture build gates.
  A group is finished when every item in it is, but an unfinished later group never holds back an
  earlier one that is - and no group being finished closes this file.
  AND THE STANDARD ABOVE IS THE DRIVER ONE. A SHARED LIBRARY item closes on host tests and hostile
  fixtures with no bind and no device; a KERNEL/BUS INFRASTRUCTURE item closes on the mechanism
  existing with its own negative tests; a DESTINATION SERVICE item closes on the contract being
  versioned and a second instance arriving through it; an ARCHITECTURAL PREREQUISITE closes on the
  decision being recorded and the mechanism existing. Holding any of those against a bind gate is
  what made four AML items and half of HID-over-I2C uncloseable.
  ITEMS WITH NO ATTAINABLE PATH ARE NOT IN THE SET. The four AML items and the binding half of
  HID-over-I2C are blocked on prerequisites that have no milestone; they are listed as candidates and
  are not counted anywhere. Device-specific hardware support remains explicitly in Phase 5, and
  development-only virtio-fs never becomes an ambient or production storage path.
- Concept: P02M0098 (the binder/lifecycle foundation every driver must use - "completed" stood here
  and is a verdict, which belongs in the matrix above and nowhere else), the
  Phase-2 virtio-plus-universal-driver policy in `docs/CONCEPT_CZ.md` and
  `docs/CONCEPT_EN.md`, the existing xHCI USB class stack, and the Phase-5
  device-/board-specific boundary.
## Fourth audit (2026-08-16): `AI/audit/drivers.md`, and `AI/audit/wire.md` WIRE-002

  **THE MUTATION HALF IS DELIVERED FOR THE FOUR FAMILIES WRITTEN SO FAR (2026-09-16).**
  `check.sh --gate driver-mutations` plants ten deliberate defects in the decision modules and
  requires the host test NAMED FOR EACH to fail: the queue length taken from a zero-based register,
  a completion believed on half its status, a transfer one page short because the buffer was not page
  aligned, a command-issue bit read as success, a port bitmap read as a count, a card sized by the
  wrong CSD version, a capacity bit read before the card says it is valid, a codec verb built with
  the wrong payload width, a format word written as the numbers it names, and the block wire's
  refusal collapsed into its device failure. Ten of ten are caught. It runs on a copy of the tree,
  takes seconds because the modules are host-testable, and is in the verification catalogue and the
  release-required list rather than being a script nobody selects.
  WHY THIS AND NOT MORE TESTS: every one of those ten defects is SILENT in production. None produces
  an error anywhere - a wrong capacity, a sector read from the wrong place, a stream configured at a
  rate nobody asked for. "The family has host tests" is a count; a watched-fail mutation is the only
  thing that says what they would notice.
  THE HARNESS WAS WRONG THREE TIMES BEFORE THE THING IT TESTS WAS, and each is fixed and worth the
  warning for whoever writes the next one. `cp -a` and `rsync -a` PRESERVE TIMESTAMPS, so restoring a
  mutated file also restored its mtime and cargo reused the object compiled from the MUTATED source:
  the copy was byte-identical to the tree and its tests failed anyway. Under `set -e` with `pipefail`,
  the deliberately-failing command inside a substitution killed the harness at the first successfully
  planted defect, before it could record that the defect was caught. And a test filter that matches
  nothing prints a passing result with no tests run, which is indistinguishable from a mutation that
  went uncaught unless the COUNT is checked as well as the verdict.
  **AND THE FAKE-CONTROLLER HALF IS IN FOR THE COMPLETION RING (2026-09-17).** The tests above ask
  about ONE entry; this asks about a RING - a model of the controller's half that fills slots in
  order, wraps, and flips the phase as it goes, with a reader walking behind it. The properties it
  covers cannot be seen one entry at a time: completion ORDERING over three passes of a four-entry
  ring with every slot reused; RESET, in the sense a wrap is one, where the slots all hold real
  completions from the previous round and only the phase distinguishes already-read from new, so a
  reader that lost track would replay the whole ring; TIMEOUT, as the fact that an untouched ring has
  no state in which it reports anything but empty, which is why a driver's own bound is the only
  thing that ends the wait; DISCONNECT, as an answer to a command the reader gave up on turning up in
  the slot the next one was waiting at, told apart from an entry still arriving because one says stop
  and the other says wait; and RESOURCE CLEANUP at this layer, which is the reader staying in step
  when a completion fails, because the entry is consumed either way.

  **THE RULE THAT ENDED THE WORST DEFECT OF THIS WORK IS NOW A TESTED DECISION RATHER THAN A LINE IN
  A DRIVER.** A completion whose phase bit arrived before the rest of its sixteen bytes is
  `Reaped::Arriving`, told apart from `Unexpected` because the two want opposite answers; the id the
  driver never issues is named `NEVER_ISSUED` and the driver's counter skips it by that name. It was
  written inside `nvme_driver.rs`, in the one place no host test could reach - which is exactly where
  it had already cost three rounds of measurement to find. It has four tests and a mutation now.

  **AND ALL FOUR FAMILIES HAVE IT NOW (2026-09-17).** Each model writes what its own controller
  writes and nothing else, so the assertions exercise the decision layer against something that
  behaves the way the hardware does:
    - THE HDA PAIR, which is where this driver actually failed and where the failure was invisible for
      four rounds. A command ring and a response ring are not one ring twice: the driver owns one
      write pointer and reads the controller's, and the controller owns the other. Each of those four
      numbers can be off by one alone, and each produces a different plausible stall. The model stops
      after `RINTCNT` responses until the status is acknowledged, so the defect is now a PROPERTY
      rather than an anecdote: with the bound at one and no acknowledgement, the command ring's write
      pointer moves and the controller's read pointer stands still, which is exactly what the machine
      reported. Both halves of the repair are covered, and both rings are walked twice round a
      sixteen-entry ring so an off-by-one in either direction shows up as a reader permanently one
      answer behind or ahead.
    - THE AHCI PORT, which is two registers that disagree ON PURPOSE: the slot clears on COMPLETION
      and the task file says what the device thought. A second slot outstanding, a latched error, and
      the error clearing when the next command is taken are all sequences rather than single reads.
    - THE SDHCI SEQUENCE, where a command is TWO events and not one: the command completes, the
      buffer becomes ready, and the transfer completes, and a driver waiting on the wrong one either
      reads a buffer the controller has not filled or reports success while the card is still
      writing. Including an error arriving TOGETHER with a completion, which some controllers do and
      which a driver testing for completion first calls a finished transfer.
  142 host tests over the four decision modules, eleven of eleven mutations caught.

  WHAT REMAINS OF THIS ITEM: the families not yet written.

Sixteen driver findings, ten of them High. The first is reported independently by two audits, which
is why it is first: `AI/audit/wire.md` reaches the same code from the `Buffer` contract's side.

THIS DEBT BELONGS TO THE FAMILIES IT TOUCHES, NOT TO THE MILESTONE AS A WHOLE. It is not in the
ordering above and it is not in the Definition of done, and that was right: making sixteen findings
about today's drivers a gate on forty new ones would block every item on defects in code most of them
never touch. Each finding is owned by the family whose driver it is in, and is closed when that
family's next item is - which for the ones below means block storage, USB, virtio sound, virtio GPU
and HID. AND THE FINDINGS ARE RE-VERIFIED BEFORE THEY ARE ACTED ON: they were written on
2026-08-16 and the tree has moved, as the first bullet's correction shows.

- [x] **A block write maps a transferred object and copies `count * 512` bytes out of it without
  CLOSED 2026-09-14 by `virtio-blk maintenance` and `xHCI maintenance`, both of which name this ID
  in their gate and whose evidence has run: `drivers::blk::write_source` and `usb_storage`'s
  equivalent check the transferred object's TYPE, RIGHTS and SIZE as three separate refusals before
  anything is mapped, each with a host test watched to fail under a mutation of its check.
  ever asking how big it is (DRV-003 / WIRE-002, both High).** VERIFIED IN BOTH DRIVERS.
  `virtio_blk::serve_write` and `usb_storage::serve_write` each take a caller-supplied `count`,
  compute `bytes = count as u64 * SECTOR as u64`, call `map_object(src_handle)`, and then
  `core::ptr::copy_nonoverlapping(src as *const u8, dst, bytes as usize)`. The bound that IS checked
  - `span.fit(bytes)` and `st.fit_data(bytes)` - bounds the DESTINATION DMA span, not the source
  object. A client that sends `count = 1000` with a 512-byte MemoryObject drives a 512 kB read out
  of a 512-byte mapping: either a fault that kills the isolated driver, or adjacent mapped memory
  written to the disk. `rt::object_info` already reports a memory object's byte size, so the check
  is available today. RE-VERIFIED 2026-08-28 AND NARROWED TWICE: the original finding said object
  type and rights were unchecked on the same path. `sys_memory_map` goes through
  `current_typed::<MemoryObject>(handle, ObjectType::MemoryObject, Rights::MAP)`, so the TYPE and the
  `MAP` right are checked - which is not the same as "rights are checked", and the distinction is the
  finding: `READ` is not asked for on an object the driver reads OUT of. What is missing is that and
  the SIZE, and the size is the whole of the copy defect. The 512 kB example is virtio-blk's exactly;
  USB storage clamps its count, so its over-read is smaller and still an over-read.
- [x] **Virtqueue size, used IDs, used lengths and used-index movement are not validated centrally
  (DRV-001, High).** A device - or anything that can act as one - controls those fields.
  CLOSED 2026-09-14 by `virtio-blk maintenance`, which is where this file assigned it: the shared
  virtio path carries `check_used_element`/`check_used_advance`, applied by `take_used` and `submit`
  for every driver, with the id, the length and the index each refused against what the driver posted.
- [x] **Block drivers silently clamp counts and never validate LBA ranges, and USB truncates the LBA
  to 32 bits (DRV-002, High).** A silent clamp turns a wrong request into a wrong write.
  CLOSED 2026-09-14: the block half by `virtio-blk maintenance` and the USB half by `xHCI
  maintenance`. A count of zero or above the request limit and a range past the last sector are
  refused with `STATUS_INVALID`, never clamped, and the USB LBA is no longer truncated to 32 bits.
- [x] **USB flush converts every repeated failure into success without reading sense data
  (DRV-005, High).** A durability barrier that reports success on failure is worse than none.
  CLOSED 2026-09-14 by `xHCI maintenance`: a device that fails a flush twice is reported as a failed
  flush, with the sense read, and never as success.
- [x] **USB Bulk-Only Transport ignores actual transfer lengths and CSW residue (DRV-006, High).**
  A short transfer is then read as a complete one.
  CLOSED 2026-09-14 by `xHCI maintenance`: a CSW whose residue exceeds the transfer, and one whose
  length disagrees with what was sent, are both refused rather than read as a short success.
- [x] **xHCI loses DMA handles and slot ownership across hotplug and partial failure, and its
  synchronous waits consume and discard port-status events (DRV-007, -008, High).**
  CLOSED 2026-09-14 by `xHCI maintenance`, both halves rather than the one the gate used to name: a
  connect and a disconnect in the same status window produce exactly one of each and a storm is
  bounded; a device removed mid-enumeration and an enumeration that fails part way each leave no DMA
  handle allocated and no slot owned, counted before and after.
- [x] **Virtio sound treats any interrupt as TX completion, ignores status, reuses stale PCM and
  always replies success (DRV-009, High).**
  CLOSED 2026-09-14 by `virtio-snd maintenance`: the wait is bounded and repeats, every status code
  that is not `S_OK` is a failure, the status region is cleared before each submission and a short
  completion is refused before the status is believed, and a refused period is an empty reply that
  AudioService reads.
- [x] **Virtio GPU trusts unbounded dimensions and uses unchecked framebuffer and rectangle
  arithmetic (DRV-010, High).**
  CLOSED 2026-09-12 by the display half of `virtio-gpu maintenance`, and recorded here rather than
  only there. The geometry the device reports is bounded by the image model's own maximum extent with
  the pitch and the backing size both checked; the rectangle union saturates, so a crafted pair
  becomes a rectangle the clip removes rather than one that is somewhere else; the clamp became a
  CLIP, because clamping an origin moves a rectangle that was entirely off the display to its edge
  and presents pixels nobody asked to present; and the transfer offset - the last arithmetic before
  the device is told where to read - is checked, with a rectangle whose offset does not fit refused
  rather than sent. The mutations were watched to fail: believing the reported extent fails the
  geometry test and wrapping the union fails the union test.
  WHAT REMAINS IS THE GATE AND NOT THE FINDING. `virtio-gpu maintenance` also asks for the display
  endpoint under translation on the profiles those gates name, and that needs a display phase added
  to them; that clause stays open in its own item, where the work is.
- [x] **USB configuration walkers read fixed fields outside descriptor records and accept short
  descriptor transfers (DRV-012, High).**
  CLOSED 2026-09-14 by `xHCI maintenance`: a descriptor whose record is shorter than the fixed
  fields it declares, and a transfer shorter than the descriptor it claims to carry, are both
  refused rather than read past.
- [x] **HID arithmetic can overflow before or during report decoding; a short report leaves the
  previous-report tail stale; left and right modifiers share one boolean and release each other
  (DRV-011, -013, -014).**
  CLOSED 2026-09-14. All three, each with a fixture that FAILS against the code as it was.
  THE PARSER MOVED INTO THE DRIVERS LIBRARY FIRST, because it was a module inside the xHCI binary
  where it could not be tested on the host at all - which is how all three of these survived. Nothing
  about a report descriptor is transport-specific: a USB HID device, an I2C one and a Bluetooth one
  all speak the same descriptors.
  DRV-011, TWICE. The segment-admission check was `*cursor + bits <= MAX_REPORT_BYTES * 8` against a
  cursor that SATURATES as a descriptor walks past the bound - so the plain addition overflowed on
  exactly the descriptor the check exists to refuse: a panic in a debug build, and in a release one a
  wrap to a small number that ADMITS the segment. A fixture builds that descriptor and the old code
  panics with "attempt to add with overflow" on it. And in the array decoder, `v - seg.logical_min`
  was an `i32` subtraction of two values the DEVICE wrote: a logical minimum of `i32::MIN` against a
  reading of zero overflows it. It is now `i64` and clamped.
  DRV-013. A report shorter than the last one overwrote only the bytes it carried, so the previous
  report's tail stayed in the state the next diff runs against - and a key whose usage lives in that
  tail is never released. It reads as a key that sticks down after a truncated report, which is what
  a device sends when it is unplugged mid-transfer. The state update is now `hid::remember`, one
  place with one rule and one test: copy, then ZERO the tail.
  DRV-014. `Mods` folded both Shift keys into one boolean, so releasing the right one cancelled a
  left one that was still held - the defect that drops the capital letter out of the middle of a word
  typed with both hands, and which nobody reproduces on purpose because it needs two keys in a
  particular order. Each side is now its own bit and the public boolean is derived from both, for
  Ctrl, Alt and Meta as well as Shift. `Mods::release_all` is there for the other half of the same
  problem: a device that goes away with a key down otherwise leaves it down for ever, and the next
  thing typed arrives shifted.
- [x] **Unexpected transferred handles are ignored and leaked across several service protocols
  (DRV-004).**
  CLOSED 2026-09-14 ON ITS STATED TRIGGER - the first item that changes a service protocol's handle
  handling - which was the display service's move to client-supplied images and attenuated replies.

  THE DEFECT IS NOT THE REFUSAL, IT IS WHAT HAPPENS TO THE HANDLE. A dispatch that decoded the bytes
  it understood and ignored the rest leaves a live capability in nobody's hands and nobody's list:
  not refused, not closed, and still charged to the sender's Domain for the life of the process. One
  per request, from any client, with no privilege at all.

  THE GENERATED DISPATCH CATCHES IT AT `Reader::finish`, which answers BOTH halves of "this message
  is over" - the bytes AND the handles - and which every generated op calls. What reaches a service
  is a request whose signature accounts for everything it carried; what is left in the caller's list
  is what the serve loop then closes.

  AND EVERY HAND-WRITTEN RECEIVE IN THE TREE WAS CHECKED rather than assumed: the fifteen sites that
  take a message with its capabilities each close what they did not adopt - the driver header reader
  on both malformed paths, the four stream readers in the tools, the console's event reader on both
  paths, and the serve loops in the display, input, audio, storage, network and device services.

  EVIDENCE: a host fixture in `display-proto` sends a well-formed `ack-configure` twice, once bare
  and once with a capability attached, and requires the first to reach the service and the second to
  be refused with the handle still in the caller's list; and the guest's hostile-input test sends the
  same request over a real channel and requires the service not to answer it AND the smuggled
  channel's peer to be closed - which is the half a host fixture cannot show.
- [ ] **Roughly 5,700 lines of unsafe device logic have no direct tests (DRV-016).** This is the one
  that makes the others expensive to fix safely, so it is worth doing first for whichever driver is
  touched first.

  AND THAT IS WHAT HAS BEEN HAPPENING, WITH A NUMBER (2026-09-19). The `drivers` crate carries 247
  host tests over its pure decisions today. Every driver touched in this milestone's recent work
  gained some, and each one is named for the mistake it plants rather than asserting that a round
  trip agrees with itself: AHCI's queued-command field mapping and its `PxSACT`-not-`PxCI`
  completion, SDHCI's ADMA descriptor bound and its Auto-CMD12 rule, UAS's two task-management tags
  and its two success codes, CDC's notification decode and its lying length field, and the whole of
  `drivers::uac`.

  WHAT THE NUMBER DOES NOT MEASURE, AND SAYING SO IS THE POINT. 430 `unsafe` sites remain in that
  crate and they are the MMIO and DMA paths: what a register write does is not a decision a fixture
  can hold, and the way those paths are exercised here is the in-guest oracle against a device model.
  The strategy this item asks for - extract the decision, hold it with a fixture, leave the transport
  to the oracle - is working, and what is left is the drivers nobody has touched yet.

  **AND "IT IS WORKING" WAS TURNED INTO A CHECKED NUMBER (2026-09-19), WHICH FOUND THE GATE RED.**
  Every decision module in the crate now has a fixture - the only file without one is
  `dev_channel.rs`, which is a transport program's entry point whose logic lives in the shared
  `serial_port` module that does. So the measurement to make is not how many tests exist but whether
  they KILL anything, and `check.sh --gate driver-mutations` is what answers that.

  IT WAS FAILING, AND FOR A REASON WORTH KEEPING: TWO ANCHORS HAD STOPPED BEING UNIQUE. A planted
  defect is located by a line of source, and this milestone's own recent work duplicated two of them.
  NCQ brought `queued_outcome`, whose error test is written exactly like the single-command path's,
  so `if tfd & TFD_ERR != 0 {` matched twice in `ahci.rs`. Task management brought a second
  information unit that writes its tag exactly like the command unit's, so the two-line tag anchor
  matched twice in `uas.rs`. THE GATE REFUSED RATHER THAN PLANTING THE DEFECT IN WHICHEVER IT FOUND
  FIRST, which is the right refusal and is the only reason the ambiguity was visible at all - and
  nobody saw it because the gate had not been run since that code landed.

  BOTH ANCHORS NOW CARRY THE LINE THAT DISTINGUISHES THEM, and the two new decisions got mutations of
  their own rather than riding on the old ones: a queued tag is outstanding while its `PxSACT` bit is
  SET - the opposite register and the opposite sense from `PxCI` - and a task-management header
  carries the REQUEST'S tag and not the doomed command's. Both are caught by the tests written for
  them, which is what makes those tests evidence rather than decoration.

  40 OF 40 PLANTED DEFECTS CAUGHT, each by the test named for it.
- [x] **Without an IOMMU a compromised driver or device can DMA outside its process-owned buffers
  (DRV-015, design).** Same boundary as KERN-CORE-020; state it as a threat-model limit if it is not
  being closed.
  CLOSED 2026-09-14 ON ITS OWN STATED TRIGGER: it closes by being written into the threat model, not
  by a driver, and `docs/THREAT_MODEL.md` now states it in three places rather than one.
  Section 2.2 says what an enforcing profile buys and what a machine without a controller does not:
  a DMA capability there hands the driver the buffer's RAW PHYSICAL ADDRESSES, nothing between the
  device and RAM checks what its descriptor rings contain, and a driver that programs its device to
  read or write outside its own buffer SUCCEEDS. It also says why the kernel's own mappings do not
  help - they bound where the DRIVER may reach and not where its DEVICE may reach - and it names the
  three things that narrow the window without closing it: bus mastering disabled until ownership is
  established, reclaim behind a quiescence claim, and quarantined frames after a fault.
  It says WHICH STATE A BOOT IS IN IS STATED RATHER THAN INFERRED: the DMA mode comes from one
  producer per entry path, admission compares the machine against the statement, and a degraded boot
  lists every affected device by name under `dma: DEGRADED ISOLATION` - so the limit is never silent.
  And section 5 carries it as a NON-GOAL in as many words: IOMMU isolation on every machine and every
  architecture is not claimed, the x86_64 `virtio-iommu` profile enforces it, a machine with no
  controller does not, and its DMA-capable drivers remain TCB members for memory safety. Native
  VT-d, AMD-Vi, SMMU and RISC-V IOMMU backends are named as unimplemented rather than assumed.

AND SIX OF THESE HAVE NO FAMILY ITEM TO RIDE ON, so each is given a closure trigger here rather than
a milestone of its own. DRV-009 (virtio sound) and DRV-010 (virtio GPU) were given one in an earlier
round that named items THIS ROADMAP DOES NOT HAVE: there is no `virtio-sound` checkbox and no
`virtio-gpu` checkbox, only references to those drivers from inside other bullets. They are defects
in drivers this system ALREADY SHIPS, and a roadmap of drivers to ADD has nowhere to put them. So
they close where those drivers are next changed, AND THE FIX IS PART OF THAT ITEM'S GATE RATHER THAN
ADJACENT TO IT - which is the difference between a trigger and a hope. Writing HDA does not oblige
anybody to fix `virtio_snd`'s interrupt handling, and writing an EDID parser does not oblige anybody
to bound `virtio_gpu`'s rectangle arithmetic.

**AND THEY NOW HAVE ITEMS OF THEIR OWN, WHICH IS BETTER THAN A TRIGGER** (2026-08-30). Section 0
above adds bounded maintenance items for the seven drivers that already ship, and THE MAPPING IS
COMPLETE RATHER THAN EXEMPLARY (corrected 2026-08-30 - a previous round named four IDs and left six
High findings with no actionable gate, so the next change to those families could close in direct
contradiction to the rule this section states):

    DRV-001 (virtqueue validation)          `virtio-blk maintenance`, plus the shared-validation
                                              owner rule below
    DRV-002 (silent count clamp, no LBA     `virtio-blk maintenance` AND `xHCI maintenance`. The
      range check; USB truncates LBA to       finding is TWO defects in one ID: both block drivers
      32 bits)                                clamp and skip the range check, and USB additionally
                                              truncates the LBA. Each item closes its own half
                                              (corrected 2026-08-31 - this row named only virtio-blk
                                              and would have left the USB half with no gate)
    DRV-003 / WIRE-002 (type/rights/size)   `virtio-blk maintenance` AND `xHCI maintenance`, both
                                              on the complete three-part contract
    DRV-005 (flush reports false success)   `xHCI maintenance` (corrected 2026-08-31 - this row said
                                              `virtio-blk maintenance` and the defect is in USB: the
                                              debt entry is "USB flush converts every repeated
                                              failure into success without reading sense data", and
                                              virtio-blk has no sense data to read. Assigning it to
                                              the block item would have left it open on the driver
                                              that has it)
    DRV-006 (BOT length and CSW residue)    `xHCI maintenance`
    DRV-007, DRV-008 (port-status events)   `xHCI maintenance`
    DRV-009 (virtio sound)                  `virtio-snd maintenance`
    DRV-010 (virtio GPU, display half)      `virtio-gpu maintenance`
    DRV-012 (descriptor transfers)          `xHCI maintenance`

Each of those items carries its IDs and their negative tests in its own gate -
named in the item, not only here. A previous round put these triggers in this section and in no
actionable bullet, which meant an item plan could follow its own bullet and close without the fix,
which is precisely the failure this section warns about. DRV-003/WIRE-002 is likewise in the
`virtio-blk` and `xHCI` maintenance gates. Where a LATER family item touches the same code - HDA or
USB Audio for the sound path, EDID/DDC for the display path - it carries the same ID as a regression
gate over ITS OWN driver, which is a different instance of the defect and not the same one twice.

AND DRV-001 NOW HAS ONE. Central virtqueue validation - size, used ids, used lengths, index movement
- lives in code every virtio driver shares, so no single driver family's item contains it. It was
assigned to "the FIRST VIRTIO ITEM in group 1 to be written, whichever that turns out to be", which
is a race rather than an owner. It is owned by **`virtio-blk maintenance`** in section 0: that item
is already in the shared virtqueue path with a gate attached, it is the first Phase-2 maintenance
item with a DMA policy to migrate, and it exists today rather than being whichever future item
arrives first. Its gate carries DRV-001's hostile-descriptor fixtures.

The four that are cross-cutting close on a stated event instead: DRV-004, unexpected
transferred handles leaked across service protocols, closes with the first item that changes a
service protocol's handle handling; DRV-015 is a THREAT-MODEL LIMIT and closes by being written into
the concept's threat model, not by a driver; DRV-016, the untested unsafe device logic, closes per
family as each family's item lands, which is what its own text already says - "worth doing first for
whichever driver is touched first" - and is recorded here so it is not read as one debt anybody could
pay in one go.

## Fifth audit (2026-08-28)

An external review of this milestone, checked against the tree before anything was changed. Seven of
its eight findings were real and are folded into the sections above rather than listed here; what is
worth keeping is which, and what was declined.

Accepted: this is a roadmap and said so nowhere; "not thirty-one items" had been forty-one for some
time; the binder reaches virtio and xHCI and nothing else, while groups 2 and 3 assume three things
P02M0163 refused by name; the USB group asks every class driver for a registry entry, a claim and a
bind while HID and storage are modules inside one process holding one `Option<(UsbDevice, Storage)>`;
PCIe hotplug needs a lifecycle that deliberately does not exist; three shared contracts have no
owner; the group order was written as a gate when it is a priority; and the Definition of done said
every driver releases all resources on crash, which contradicts the quarantine this tree built - an
unconfirmed teardown stays CHARGED, and saying otherwise would have made the safe behaviour a
milestone failure.

It also caught a stale status line outside this file: [P02M0162](P02M0162.md) said IN PROGRESS with
every item done and TODO.md ticked, and this milestone names it in its hard gate. Correcting the
status line alone was not enough and the sixth audit said so - see below.

Declined, and both for the same reason: splitting this file into per-item milestones, and going
through all forty-one bullets to give each a `requires`/`owns`/gate triple now. The rule is written
once in "Order, gates and completion" and an item states its three lines when somebody writes it -
filling in forty-one sets of them today would be inventing prerequisites for drivers nobody has
started, which is how a roadmap acquires answers before it has the questions.

One of the review's own claims was wrong and is corrected in place rather than repeated: object type
and rights are NOT unchecked on the mapping path - `sys_memory_map` refuses a handle of the wrong
type or without `MAP`. The size check and `READ` are what is missing, and the size is the whole of
the copy defect.

## Sixth audit (2026-08-28)

The same reviewer over the file the fifth audit produced. Every finding held; nothing was declined,
because none of them proposed adding a mechanism - each named a contradiction the fifth round's own
edits left behind or a claim this file makes that the tree does not support.

- **The hard gate was reported closed on a status line and left open underneath.** Turning
  P02M0162's status over did not touch the paragraph beneath it, which named three items as not
  started while all seven were ticked. That is corrected in that file, together with the thing this
  audit was right to hold against it: one value in it is still NAMED-OPEN - the three boot-window
  constants are round counts being read as ticks, and the milestone deliberately hands the
  measurement to whoever changes the contract. A milestone here that depends on the boot window
  inherits that, and the correction says so rather than letting "COMPLETE" imply otherwise.
- **The USB prerequisite contradicted the conformance gate two hundred lines later.** The
  prerequisite allows class drivers to stay inside the xHCI Domain; the gate demanded a governed
  process and a DeviceManager binding per family unconditionally. The gate is now conditional on
  which answer the prerequisite gives, which is what a decision item is for.
- **The gate also still contradicted the quarantine** that the Definition of done had just been
  corrected to state. What must not survive a termination is a usable authority or provider, not
  every charged resource.
- **Three ownerships were wrong.** P02M0161 owns start and report and says in its own goal that
  `STOP`/`STOPPED` is P02M0165's; the DMA/IOVA contract and the `iommu-required` policy MOVED from
  P02M0098 to P02M0153 and this file cited the old owner twice; and P02M0098 is not "the binder
  foundation" on its own - the gate is the three milestones together.
- **`Removed` was terminal and repluggable in the same sentence.** The slot outlives the device as a
  tombstone, `Removed` is terminal for that binding, a new arrival opens a new one, and an
  unconfirmed teardown ends in `Quarantined` instead - which is the state that says something is
  still owed.
- **A published provider cannot be withdrawn today.** `subscribe_open` exists in the generated
  protocol and DeviceManager never calls it, so xHCI publishes a block provider whether or not a
  stick is present and leaves it standing on detach. The first item that attaches a child at runtime
  owns that, named.
- **`virtio-scsi` said "reuse the common SCSI layer" for a layer this file admits does not exist.**
  It owns extracting one.
- **The count was 41 and there are 42 checkboxes,** because the fifth round added one that is not a
  driver. Named as what it is.
- **HID over I2C does not sit on the xHCI stack** its group's own introduction says everything sits
  on.
- **DRV-003's correction was itself imprecise.** "Type and rights are checked" is not what the code
  does: the type and `MAP` are checked, `READ` is not asked for, and that distinction is half the
  remaining finding. Four other findings had no family item to close them and now have a stated
  trigger each.

## Seventh audit (2026-08-28)

The reviewer over the file the sixth audit produced, together with P02M0167. Six findings, all held,
and one of them is a correction of a sentence the sixth round itself added.

- **"A published provider cannot be withdrawn" was wrong.** `Withdraw` is opcode 6 and DeviceManager
  handles it. Two narrower things are missing and they are the ones a driver actually hits: the
  catalogue's live stream carries additions and no withdrawal event, and no driver sends the message.
- **The head forbids a source `match` arm and resources are handed out by one.** A literal list of
  driver names decides which drivers get an MSI-X vector, so the rule this file states is broken by
  the mechanism every new driver must use. The first item needing a shape that list cannot express
  owns a declarative resource profile.
- **The closing boundary was never decided.** A roadmap of forty-one drivers across product phases,
  sitting as an open Phase-2 item, with no statement of which. It is a cross-phase umbrella; Phase 2's
  own definition of done does not name any of these drivers.
- **The hard gate is complete with two verifications outstanding.** P02M0161 says only x86_64 has
  booted the new handshake; P02M0162 hands on three unmeasured bind windows. Neither blocks writing a
  driver and both block accepting one on three targets - which is exactly what the integration gate
  asks for, so the first driver inherits them rather than discovering them.
- **The first checkbox is not work.** `virtio-iommu`'s backend and first slice are P02M0153's; what
  is left here is "the remaining drivers and architectures", which is not a list.
- **Every item was asked to survive removal, and removal arrives with an item most of them precede.**
  Qualified to the removal each group can see, with device removal left to the item that builds it.

## Eighth audit (2026-08-28)

Four findings, all about ownership, and the first is a correction to the seventh round's own fix.

- **The declarative resource profile's trigger was narrower than the rule it serves.** It was owned
  by "the first item needing a shape the name list cannot express", which leaves a driver with an
  ordinary MSI-X need to be handled by appending one more name - the exact edit the head forbids. The
  trigger is the first new driver that would otherwise require a change to the list.
- **PL011, not HID over I2C, is the first firmware-bound driver.** It binds through ACPI/FDT
  identity and it stands eighty lines earlier, so the firmware-node identity is its to own.
- **16550 sits behind a "canonical serial contract" that USB CDC-ACM defines**, a hundred and twenty
  lines later and in a later group. Whichever lands first owns it.
- **virtio-balloon and virtio-mem wait on ResourceManager contracts nobody owns**, which is a
  precondition with nothing at the end of it. The item owns them or it does not start.

## Ninth audit (2026-08-28)

Four findings; two of them correct rounds seven and eight of this same file, which is the argument
for a reviewer reading the fixes rather than the original.

- **Two closure triggers named checkboxes this roadmap does not have.** There is no `virtio-sound`
  item and no `virtio-gpu` item - only references to those drivers from inside other bullets - so
  DRV-009 and DRV-010 had no trigger after all. They are defects in drivers this system already
  ships, and a roadmap of drivers to ADD has nowhere to put them; they now close where those drivers
  are next touched.
- **The serial contract has three claimants, not two.** `virtio-serial multiport` is in group 1,
  ahead of both 16550 and CDC-ACM, and gives its ports "independent capability-scoped byte streams".
  Whoever gets there first also answers whether a virtio port, a UART and a USB adapter are one
  contract or a base with a serial layer on it.
  ANSWERED 2026-09-15 by multiport, which got there first: the byte stream is the base and the line
  coding is a layer above it. The full statement is under that item; the other two claimants use it.
- **`USB hotplug` was named as an owner and is not a checkbox.** The catalogue subscribe and the
  attach/detach messages belong to the first group-3 item written, since every one of them attaches a
  child at runtime.
- **HID over I2C waits on a bus nothing provides.** No I2C driver, no I2C provider kind. The item
  owns a bounded I2C contract and its fake, or names the controller item it waits for.

## Tenth audit (2026-08-28)

Two findings, and the first corrects a fix from the ninth round - the second wrong owner named for
the same unowned work.

- **"The first group-3 item written" cannot own USB hotplug**, for the reason this file states two
  hundred lines earlier: group 3 contains HID over I2C, which is not USB and attaches nothing to a
  USB bus. The owner is the first item that actually attaches a USB child at runtime.
- **A trigger that names a neighbouring item is not a fix.** Writing HDA does not oblige anybody to
  correct `virtio_snd`, and writing an EDID parser does not oblige anybody to bound `virtio_gpu`'s
  arithmetic - so DRV-009 and DRV-010 are named in those items' GATES rather than left beside them.
  And DRV-001 had no owner at all: central virtqueue validation lives in code every virtio driver
  shares, which is why no family's item contains it. It goes to the first virtio item written.

## Eleventh audit (2026-08-28)

One finding worth acting on, and it is the same shape as the one found in P02M0167 the same day: a
contract that named two answers and chose neither.

- **HID over I2C was left to choose between owning an I2C contract and waiting for a controller
  item.** Only one of those answers exists, because this roadmap has no I2C controller item to wait
  for. It owns the contract and its host fake; its bind half is hardware-deferred, since QEMU models
  no I2C HID device either.

The other two open points the review names are open on purpose and stay that way: the first driver
cannot be ACCEPTED on three architectures until P02M0161's handshake has booted on aarch64 and
riscv64 and P02M0162's bind windows have been measured, and group 3 cannot start until its execution
model is decided. Both are stated where somebody will meet them, and neither blocks writing code.

## Twelfth audit (2026-08-28)

Two findings, both of them this file contradicting itself rather than missing something.

- **`NVMe` and `AHCI` were listed as the cheapest true starts while the same file made them wait on a
  contract `virtio-scsi` owned.** Correcting it turned up the real error underneath: the SCSI core
  and the block wire were treated as one thing with one owner, and they are two with different
  consumers - NVMe has its own command set and AHCI speaks ATA, so neither needs SCSI and both need
  the block wire. The block wire goes to the first block driver written, the SCSI core to the first
  SCSI-speaking one, and the cheapest-starts list keeps only items that create no shared contract.
- **The group-3 execution-model prerequisite gated the whole group, including a non-USB item.** HID
  over I2C attaches to no xHCI controller and holds no USB claim, and neither answer the prerequisite
  chooses between is about anything it does.

## Thirteenth audit (2026-08-28)

Four findings, and all four are about the twelfth round's own fix. Two of them are the same mistake
made a second time in the act of correcting it the first time, which is worth stating plainly.

- **The block wire got a list of three owners where it needed a rule.** UAS publishes block, and so
  can SDHCI and NFIT. It is owned by the first implemented slice that publishes a block provider,
  named as a rule so the next block-shaped item is not left out of it too.
- **The extracted SCSI core has three consumers, not two.** It comes OUT of the USB BOT code, so BOT
  moves onto it in the same change - otherwise an extraction has produced a second copy and called it
  sharing.
- **"No shared contract to create on the way" was false for three of the four items it was written
  about.** `16550`, `PL011` and `virtio-serial` share the serial contract that this same file says has
  three claimants and no definition. `virtio-rng` is the only genuine one, and the list now says so
  and says what the real question is: not how big the driver is, but how many later items consume
  what writing it forces you to define.
- **Firmware-node identity was assigned to PL011 by name** while `16550` needs a claimable discovery
  path and is earlier in the list. It goes to the first firmware-bound driver written - and an x86
  legacy-port 16550 turns out to need no firmware node at all, which makes it startable before any of
  it exists.
